### ⭐ [Klein tunneling through an asymmetric barrier: Symmetric transmission and directional pair creation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22337v1)

**Highlighted author(s):** Denys I. Bondar  
**Authors:** Andre G. Campos, Denys I. Bondar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22337v1>

The two curves separate. (c) Population of the negative-energy states. In the first run the packet starts to the left of the barrier and moves toward +x: we then call the probability of finding the particle at x &lt; −12 the reflection probability and the probability of finding it at x &gt; 12 the tunneling probability (see Fig. 2). In the sec- ond run the packet starts to the right of the barrier and moves...</figcaption>
</figure>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> FIG. 3. Phase-space portrait of the two propagations, dis- played as the Wigner function W(x, p) in natural units. (a) Initial state, in the geometry in which it faces the smooth edge. (b) Final state of that run. (c) Final state of the run facing the sharp edge, plotted after the phase-space inversion W(x, p) →W(−x, −p), which maps the mirrored geometry onto the axes of panel (b) and allows the two final states to be compared directly. The dashed lines at x = ±12 delimit the interior region entering Fig. 2. The sharp edge generates a rich interference pattern, the phase-space signature of zit- terbewegung, concentrated underneath the barrier.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper proves that the transmission probability for Klein tunneling through an asymmetric barrier is symmetric. However, using time-dependent simulations, the authors show this asymmetry is not in the transmitted current but rather in the generation of negative-energy states within the barrier region. Therefore, directional control in this regime must be sought by analyzing pair production rather than just particle flux.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that asymptotic transport is robust while internal dynamics are sensitive provides a nuanced view of how symmetry breaking can be 'hidden' from simple current measurements, which is relevant for understanding non-equilibrium quantum transport in mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether Klein tunneling through a spatially asymmetric potential barrier maintains left-right symmetry for Dirac fermions, contrasting with expectations of asymmetry.

**Main result.** Transmission probability remains symmetric ($T_L = T_R$), but the asymmetry manifests in the interior dynamics as a directionally dependent population of negative-energy states (pair creation).

**Method.** The analysis combines time-independent scattering matrix arguments with time-dependent Wigner-function simulations in phase space.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the relativistic Dirac equation describing quantum particle scattering off a one-dimensional, spatially asymmetric potential barrier $M(x)$.

**Key observables.** Transmission probability ($T$), Reflection probability ($R$), and the population of under-barrier negative-energy states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to asymptotic leads carrying exactly one propagating channel per direction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stationary state analysis assumes current conservation, while the time-dependent part relies on Wigner function propagation in phase space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the tunneling probability, reflection probability, and negative energy population for both left and right incidence across an asymmetric barrier, comparing sharp vs. smooth edges.

**Paper structure.** The paper first uses time-independent scattering theory to prove $T_L = T_R$. It then employs time-dependent Wigner-function simulations to locate the source of asymmetry in the interior negative-energy population, concluding that pair production, not current, carries the directional information.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that the transmission probability for the Klein tunneling through a spatially asymmetric barrier is the same for left and right incidence whenever each asymptotic lead carries a single propagating channel per direction. Time-dependent Wigner-function simulations confirm this and locate the missing directionality in the barrier's interior, where a sharp edge generates several times more under-barrier negative-energy population than a smooth one. Directional control in the Klein regime therefore resides in pair production rather than in the transmitted current.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates how Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions induce a net magnetization in hematite ($\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$), leading to non-reciprocal optical conductivity. By calculating the anomalous Hall conductivity using magnon theory, the authors demonstrate that this material can be engineered for use in high-frequency non-reciprocal circulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects fundamental magnetism (DMI, canting) directly to macroscopic quantum phenomena like non-reciprocity and device physics (circulators), which is highly relevant for understanding spin-charge coupling in magnetic insulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-reciprocal optical conductivity in hematite ($\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$) within the GHz to THz frequency range.

**Main result.** The finite net magnetic moment, induced by Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions (DMI), leads to a non-zero anomalous Hall conductivity and allows for the design of non-reciprocal circulators.

**Method.** Linear response theory is used with Kubo's formula applied to single-magnon contributions derived from linear spin wave theory analysis of the magnetic Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is hematite ($\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$), modeled as a canted easy-plane antiferromagnet. The physics is governed by a spin Hamiltonian including exchange, DMI, and anisotropy terms.

**Key observables.** Non-reciprocal optical conductivity tensor components ($\sigma_{xy}$), anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma^{(as)}$), and circulator transmission amplitude ($|S_{13}|/|S_{31}|$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** DMI energy scale ($\sim 0.1 	ext{ meV}$); magnetic anisotropy terms ($d_2, d_6$); operating frequency range (GHz to THz).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the single-magnon approximation for conductivity and treats the system in the weak ferromagnetic phase above $T_M$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnon spectrum near $\Gamma$, the dependence of magnon gaps on anisotropy, and simulated non-reciprocal responses $|S_{13}|/|S_{31}|$ vs. frequency for a circulator.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the magnetic ground state via spin Hamiltonian analysis to calculating the optical conductivity using linear response theory, culminating in the application of these results to model a three-port non-reciprocal circulator.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the non-reciprocal properties of the iron oxide $α$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ (hematite) in the canted easy-plane antiferromagnetic phase, specifically in the GHz to THz frequency range. First, using the the microscopic spin Hamiltonian, we obtain the correct classical ground state where the canting is induced by the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions (DMI). The magnon spectrum is simulated using linear spin wave theory. We then compute the polarizability and the sub-gap optical conductivities using linear response. We find that the conductivity tensor contains frequency peaks at the zero momentum magnon gaps of order $0.1~$meV which can be tuned by the DMI and on-site anisotropic spin interactions. Furthermore, we show that the canting-induced net magnetic moment $\mathbf{m}$ represents a measure for the effective time-reversal-symmetry breaking and non-reciprocity of the system: a finite $\mathbf{m}$ results in a non-zero Hall conductivity. Finally, we discuss the prospective application of hematite in non-reciprocal circulator design, by computing the non-reciprocal circulator transmission amplitude using the conductivities as input.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive theoretical framework for analyzing how correlated coherent errors affect stabilizer codes during QEC. By developing the PROSE encoding strategy, the authors show that selecting the optimal codespace can actively suppress these structured errors. Crucially, they demonstrate that positive noise correlations, often viewed as detrimental, can actually be harnessed as a resource to improve error correction performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to treat structured, non-Markovian noise (coherent errors) analytically is highly valuable for designing robust quantum hardware protocols, especially in superconducting or trapped-ion platforms where correlated noise sources are common.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a general analytical framework to treat correlated coherent errors occurring during repeated Quantum Error Correction (QEC) cycles in stabilizer codes.

**Main result.** It introduces Protected Stabilizer Eigenspace (PROSE) encoding, which can exploit noise correlations to reduce logical infidelity below the level predicted by uncorrelated error models. Furthermore, PROSE combined with Pauli twirling matches or outperforms standard error suppression techniques.

**Method.** The authors derive the exact logical channel using a general cumulant-expansion framework, yielding a non-perturbative expression for the noise-averaged logical infidelity.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is quantum information encoded in stabilizer codes. The noise model specifically considers correlated coherent dephasing errors (Z-noise) acting on data qubits during each QEC cycle.

**Key observables.** Logical infidelity ($\epsilon(R)$), which quantifies the error rate after $R$ cycles, and the dependence of the induced channel on the chosen stabilizer eigenspace.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The noise correlation structure (positive correlations are shown to be beneficial), the number of QEC cycles ($R$), and the strength of coherent errors ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the syndrome measurement and recovery operations within a single QEC cycle are noiseless. The derivations rely on cumulant expansions, which provide analytical insight into the noise average.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum circuits for multiple QEC cycles with coherent Z errors; captions highlight how positive noise correlations can reduce logical infidelity below the uncorrelated baseline when using PROSE encoding.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first deriving the exact channel under correlated noise, then developing the general cumulant framework. It introduces PROSE encoding based on the dependence of the channel on the codespace choice. Finally, it compares PROSE+twirling against standard techniques like dynamical decoupling and Pauli twirling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherent errors in stabilizer codes are often correlated across qubits and QEC cycles. Having a general analytical treatment of such noise would thus be extremely valuable. We derive here the exact logical channel induced by repeated QEC cycles under correlated coherent $Z$ noise, and develop a broadly general cumulant-expansion framework that yields a tractable expression for the noise-averaged logical infidelity. Crucially, this expression is non-perturbative in the noise, and applies to arbitrary stabilizer codes and correlation structures. It reveals a feature with no analogue in standard stochastic Pauli error models: the induced channel depends on which stabilizer eigenspace is chosen as the codespace. Exploiting this, we introduce protected stabilizer eigenspace (PROSE) encoding, an error-suppression strategy that selects the optimal codespace. We show that this eigenspace can be efficiently identified in many relevant situations. Further, when combined with logical Pauli twirling, PROSE matches or outperforms standard error suppression techniques (dynamical decoupling, Pauli twirling of physical qubits). We also show that noise correlations, usually assumed to be harmful to QEC, can instead be a resource: with the right encoding, even positive correlations reduce the logical infidelity below the uncorrelated baseline. Our results offer a new, broadly applicable lens on correlated coherent noise in stabilizer codes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a powerful framework using ZX-calculus to design quantum many-body scars based on fractal geometries. By constructing these states and their associated Hamiltonians analytically, the authors demonstrate how to embed exact, atypical eigenstates into otherwise chaotic energy spectra. This provides a novel, diagrammatically verifiable route for Hamiltonian inverse design in condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges advanced diagrammatic quantum computation tools (ZX-calculus) directly into condensed matter theory by providing an explicit, constructive method for generating non-trivial, non-thermal eigenstates (scars) in complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a systematic framework using ZX-calculus for Hamiltonian inverse design, specifically constructing quantum many-body scars (QMBS) that are atypical eigenstates embedded within otherwise thermalizing systems.

**Main result.** The paper demonstrates that the ZX-calculus can serve as this inverse design tool, allowing the explicit construction and mathematical relationship between fractal scar states, their local annihilators, and the chaotic Hamiltonians embedding them.

**Method.** The authors utilize diagrammatic languages (ZX-calculus) to encode fractal many-body states derived from Sierpiński structures. They combine these with parent-Hamiltonian techniques and local ZX identities to construct exact scars and then deform the Hamiltonian to induce chaos while preserving the scar state.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum many-body systems whose states are represented by fractal patterns (Sierpiński triangle/carpet) encoded in ZX-diagrams. The dynamics are governed by Hamiltonians constructed via local terms derived from these diagrams, allowing for embedding into chaotic spectra.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy (showing area law or logarithmic scaling), local expectation values ($\langle X_i angle$), and spectral statistics (diagnosing transition between Poissonian/integrable and Wigner-Dyson/chaotic regimes).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fractal generation parameter ($g$) defining the level of self-similarity, and system size ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the ZX-calculus provides a sufficiently rigorous graphical language to certify both the state structure and the Hamiltonian constraints (annihilation conditions).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the methodology: selecting fractal ZX-diagrams as scars, using parent Hamiltonians/local identities for construction, and showing entanglement entropy scaling ($	ext{S}$ vs. bipartition ratio) for triangle (area law) and carpet (logarithmic scaling) families.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the use of ZX-calculus for state representation; then, it details the fractal construction of states with specific entanglement properties; subsequently, it applies parent-Hamiltonian methods to find annihilators; finally, it constructs a local deformation to embed these scars into chaotic Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Diagrammatic languages such as ZX-calculus provide compact and intuitive descriptions of quantum processes and have become established tools for circuit simplification, verification, and compilation. However, their potential as a framework for constructing many-body states and the Hamiltonians that host them remains largely unexplored. Here, we introduce families of fractal many-body states obtained from ZX-diagrams based on the Sierpiński triangle and Sierpiński carpet. By construction, the underlying graph connectivity imposes atypical subvolume-law minimum-cut upper bounds on the entanglement, while the actual states are parametrically less entangled still: the triangle family obeys an area law, whereas the carpet family displays approximately logarithmic scaling across the available system sizes. Additionally, their local observables retain fractal-like spatial structure, identifying these states as natural candidates for atypical eigenstates in otherwise thermalizing systems. For the triangle family, we combine parent-Hamiltonian methods, insights from ZX-calculus, and local ZX identities that certify exact annihilation of the target state, producing frustration-free Hamiltonians whose terms admit simple representations in the same diagrammatic language as the states themselves. We then construct a local deformation that produces chaotic level statistics while embedding the fractal ZX state in the bulk of the energy spectrum as an exact quantum many-body scar. Our results demonstrate, through this explicit construction, that ZX-calculus can serve as a framework for Hamiltonian inverse design, in which quantum many-body scars, their local annihilators, and the chaotic Hamiltonians embedding them can be constructed and related through a set of graphical identities.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the slow relaxation dynamics of magnetic monopoles in frustrated spin ice. It shows that single monopoles exhibit hydrodynamic memory characterized by a $t^{-3/2}$ decay tail. Crucially, interactions between these quasiparticles at finite density introduce an exponential cutoff to this long-time memory effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a concrete, physical realization—frustrated magnets—to model complex non-Markovian dynamics, which is highly relevant for understanding relaxation in open quantum systems or disordered materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates self-interacting stochastic processes, hydrodynamic memory effects, and non-Markovian quasiparticle transport in frustrated magnetic systems.

**Main result.** Single monopoles exhibit an algebraic long-time tail ($t^{-3/2}$) in the velocity autocorrelation function, while finite density introduces an exponential cutoff due to inter-monopole interactions.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating the Mean-Square Displacement (MSD) and the Velocity Autocorrelation Function (VACF), alongside analyzing the Power Spectral Density (PSD).

**Model / system.** The system is based on nearest-neighbor classical spin ice (nnSI) on a pyrochlore lattice, where excitations are deconfined magnetic monopoles. The dynamics are modeled as stochastic single-spin-flip processes.

**Key observables.** Mean-Square Displacement ($	ext{MSD}$), Velocity Autocorrelation Function ($	ext{VACF}$), and Power Spectral Density ($	ext{PSD}(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Monopole density ($ho$), temperature ($T$), and time scales ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often focuses on the low-temperature, dilute gas regime of monopoles; comparisons are made against idealized models like structural percolation clusters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between monopole dynamics (showing $t^{-3/2}$ scaling) and other walks (like SPC showing $t^{-5/2}$), and demonstrate the exponential decay of $	ext{VACF}$ at finite density.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model (spin ice monopoles), calculating single-particle dynamics ($	ext{MSD}/	ext{VACF}$ scaling), introducing the effect of particle interactions (exponential cutoff), and finally analyzing spectral properties ($	ext{PSD}$) across different dynamic regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a simple, clean but constrained magnet, we identify a self-interacting random walk with memory. For the motion of a single monopole -- a fractionalized quasiparticle in spin ice -- this produces a subtle and unusually slow relaxation toward diffusive motion. This is manifested as an algebraic long-time tail in the velocity autocorrelation function, decaying as $t^{-3/2}$. At finite monopole density, the interactions between the trails of different monopoles introduce an additional timescale, corresponding to the disruption of a monopole's memory by other monopoles, and leading to an exponential cutoff of the long-time tail. Our results identify clean frustrated magnets as microscopic platforms for studying self-interacting stochastic processes, hydrodynamic memory, and the emergence of non-Markovian quasiparticle transport from local Markovian dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates using a photonic-crystal Fano membrane within an optomechanical cavity to overcome limitations in linewidth control. By modeling both transparent and reflective membrane regimes, the authors show that the unique hybridization provided by the Fano resonance generates narrow optical modes. These engineered modes enable efficient ground-state cooling of the mechanical oscillator even when standard cavity parameters suggest otherwise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses advanced topics in cavity quantum electrodynamics (cavity QED) and open quantum systems, specifically focusing on engineered coupling mechanisms to enhance ground-state cooling capabilities beyond standard limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional optomechanical systems suffer from limited control over optical linewidth, which restricts their performance when attempting ground-state cooling in the unresolved-sideband regime.

**Main result.** The incorporation of a photonic-crystal Fano membrane enables spectral engineering, generating narrow optical normal modes that maintain efficient coupling to external drives and allow for ground-state cooling even when the bare cavity is in the unresolved sideband regime.

**Method.** The authors employ quantum Langevin equations combined with a transfer-matrix description of the optical scattering problem to analyze the system dynamics across different reflectivity regimes.

**Model / system.** The system is an optomechanical setup involving a Fabry-Pérot cavity containing a photonic-crystal Fano membrane. The model considers two limits: a transparent-membrane regime (single mode) and a reflective-membrane regime (two coupled subcavity modes).

**Key observables.** Final phonon occupation number ($ar{n}_{fin}$), effective sideband resolution ($\kappa^-$), and the required laser power for cooling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Membrane reflectivity, coupling strengths ($g_{d,0}, g_{\lambda,0}$), cavity loss rates ($\kappa$), and mechanical frequency ($\Omega_m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linearizing the dynamics in the weak-coupling regime and assumes that the Fano membrane provides a frequency-dependent optical response superior to conventional dielectric membranes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates schematics of the setup, detailing diagrams for both the transparent-membrane (TM) limit (single mode coupling) and the reflective-membrane (RM) limit ($\hat{a}_L$, $\hat{a}_R$ subcavities).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of conventional MIM systems. It then develops the theoretical framework by analyzing the Hamiltonian and dynamics for both TM and RM regimes using Langevin equations. Finally, it derives key results showing how Fano-induced hybridization enables superior cooling performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional membrane-in-the-middle (MIM) optomechanical systems offer limited control over the optical linewidth, which can limit their performance when operating in the unresolved-sideband regime. We investigate cavity optomechanics with a photonic-crystal Fano membrane placed at the center of a Fabry-Pérot (FP) cavity. In contrast to a conventional dielectric membrane, the photonic-crystal membrane supports a localized optical resonance, which hybridizes with the cavity field and enables spectral engineering of the relevant optical modes. Besides the usual dispersive optomechanical coupling associated with cavity-length changes, the membrane motion also modifies the Fano-mode resonance and its hybridization with the cavity field. We consider two limits set by the membrane reflectivity: a transparent-membrane regime with a single FP-like mode, and a reflective-membrane regime with two coupled subcavity modes. In the latter case, only the symmetric cavity mode hybridizes with the Fano mode, while the antisymmetric mode remains decoupled. Using quantum Langevin equations together with a transfer-matrix description of the optical scattering problem, we show that the Fano-induced hybridization can generate narrow optical normal modes that remain efficiently accessible to the external drive for experimentally realistic parameters. These modes can provide effective sideband resolution and enable ground-state cooling of the membrane motion even when the bare cavity is in the unresolved-sideband regime. Our results establish Fano MIM systems as a promising platform for spectral and optomechanical engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper examines how heat spreads (thermalizes) in a localized spin chain coupled to an external bath under quasiperiodic driving. By comparing two diagnostics—correlations and entropy—the authors find that only the entropy reveals clear signs of thermal avalanches, while correlations do not. This suggests that standard theories describing such spreading must be updated to account for the unique short-range physics introduced by quasiperiodicity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in quasiperiodic systems, a key area intersecting MBL physics with open quantum system theory. The finding that different observables yield conflicting results highlights the need for careful diagnostic tools when modeling complex many-body thermalization processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates bath-induced thermalization, specifically analyzing the mechanism of 'thermal avalanches' in a Many-Body Localized (MBL) system subjected to a quasiperiodic magnetic field.

**Main result.** The particle-number entropy clearly shows signatures of thermal avalanches propagating into the localized subsystem, whereas two-point correlations fail to show this signature, suggesting standard avalanche theory needs revision for quasiperiodic potentials.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum simulation techniques by comparing results from two complementary observables: the two-point connected correlation function and the particle-number entropy, within an XXZ spin chain model.

**Model / system.** The system is an XXZ spin chain partitioned into a localized subsystem (A) coupled to a thermal bath (B), subjected to both interacting and quasiperiodic magnetic fields. The dynamics are analyzed by engineering a thermal inclusion in the bath region.

**Key observables.** Two-point connected correlation function, particle-number entropy, correlation length ($\xi_d(t)$), and entanglement entropy ($S_E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quasiperiodic field strength ($W_A$), weak disorder in the bath ($W_B=0.5$), and the comparison between quasiperiodic vs. random potentials.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard avalanche theory framework is assumed to apply, but the authors argue this assumption breaks down due to short-range correlations inherent to quasiperiodic potentials.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates thermal bubble spreading, showing the localized subsystem A coupled to an ergodic bath B, with a red curve indicating the propagating thermalization front.

**Paper structure.** The paper sets up the MBL system under external fields, introduces two diagnostic observables (correlations and entropy), compares their predictions regarding avalanche signatures, and concludes by proposing necessary revisions to existing theoretical frameworks based on the observed discrepancy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study bath-induced thermalization in the many-body localized XXZ spin chain subjected to a quasiperiodic magnetic field. We engineer a thermal inclusion by setting the field strength in one part of the chain below the localization threshold and analyze thermalization via the avalanche mechanism. To study the nature of such avalanches, we use two complementary observables, namely the two-point connected correlation function and the particle-number entropy. Surprisingly, the correlations alone show no signatures of avalanches. Instead, they display a logarithmic growth of the correlation length throughout the dynamics and predict localization lengths of the local integrals of motion that remain well below the avalanche threshold. In contrast, the particle number entropy shows clear signatures of the thermal avalanche, with the avalanche front progressively propagating deeper into the localized subsystem for sufficiently large baths. The discrepancy between the two observables shows that two-point correlations are unreliable in identifying thermal avalanches in quasiperiodic systems, as opposed to the random case. On one hand, qualitative results are consistent with the analytical predictions of the standard avalanche theory. On the other hand, significant quantitative deviations persist, which could be due to short-range resonances generated by the quasiperiodic potential. Our results suggest that the standard avalanche framework requires revision to account for the short-range correlations of quasiperiodic potentials.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes particle transport on a 1D fermionic lattice subjected to localized sources/sinks and bulk dephasing. It demonstrates that dephasing suppresses quantum coherence signatures like Friedel oscillations and simplifies the system's complex dynamics from three distinct regimes down to two, ultimately leading to classical diffusive behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how environmental coupling (dephasing) fundamentally alters quantum transport phenomena, offering insights applicable to open quantum systems in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates particle transport dynamics, thermalization, and coherence decay in open quantum systems modeled on a 1D lattice.

**Main result.** Bulk dephasing suppresses coherent features like Friedel oscillations and reduces the system's dynamical regimes from three to two, driving the behavior towards a classical diffusive limit.

**Method.** The time evolution is calculated using the Lindblad master equation framework, analyzing two-point correlation functions via matrix exponentiation of superoperators.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional spinless fermionic system on a lattice governed by a tight-binding Hamiltonian. The dynamics are open, incorporating localized sources/sinks and bulk dephasing ($\gamma$) via the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Local density profile ($n_i(t)$), two-point correlation functions ($C_{i,j}(t)$), Friedel oscillations, and particle loss rate ($|dN/dt|$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dephasing rate ($\gamma$), source/sink rates ($\Gamma_G, \Gamma_L$), lattice hopping ($J$), and separation distance ($\Delta$). Key regimes include coherent ($\gamma=0$) vs. dephased ($\gamma>0$) transport.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is treated within the Lindblad framework; calculations often utilize approximations like the adiabatic limit or focus on specific correlation function evolutions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density profiles showing ballistic spreading and secondary peaks (coherent case), and subsequent collapse onto universal curves when dephasing drives the system into a diffusive regime. Loss rate plots show crossovers between three and two distinct dynamical regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the evolution of correlation functions using the Lindblad master equation, contrasting the coherent limit (showing multiple dynamic regimes) with the presence of dephasing noise, which simplifies the dynamics to fewer regimes and diffusive scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a general one-dimensional spinless fermionic system subject to a localized source, sink, and bulk dephasing. Within the Lindblad framework, we compute the time evolution of the density profile and spatial correlation functions. We find that the presence of bulk dephasing suppresses certain coherent quantum features, such as the Friedel oscillations, and it alters the transport dynamics to exhibit two distinct dynamical regimes instead of three as observed in the absence of dephasing. This effect can be understood as the destruction of ballistic motion caused by the dephasing noise. Under strong dephasing, the density profile becomes similar to the one expected for a classical diffusive regime. We also investigate the system in the presence of both a localized source and sink, placed at a distance of $Δ$. Interestingly, quantum coherence generates secondary density peaks at integer multiples of the source-sink separation $Δ$, which are systematically washed out as bulk dephasing drives the system toward the classical diffusive limit.

</details>

Red map represents coherence ρgs, and the purple map corresponds to ρgp. (b),(c) Simulated amplitudes of the stored atomic coherences as a function of the position along the atomic ensemble right after the storage of the signal and right before the readout. During the storage (from t = 6 µs to t = 20 µs), the dipolar interactions between the Rydberg atoms disturb the spinwave coherence in regions where both |s⟩and |p⟩overlap, reducing the readout from those positions. The...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper overcomes a major hurdle in using Rydberg spinwaves for quantum memory by introducing a specialized four-photon addressing scheme that minimizes momentum transfer. This technique extends the coherence time significantly, allowing for the storage and manipulation of long-lived excitations within multimode quantum memories compatible with Gradient Echo Memory protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the intersection of advanced quantum memory techniques (GEM) with highly interacting Rydberg systems, providing crucial engineering solutions to overcome fundamental decoherence limits in quantum networking architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is integrating Rydberg spinwave storage/interaction with multiplexing schemes like Gradient Echo Memory (GEM), which is typically limited by rapid motional dephasing due to large wavevectors.

**Main result.** A novel four-photon addressing scheme generates collective Rydberg excitations with near-zero momentum transfer, extending the spinwave lifetime significantly and reestablishing compatibility between Rydberg excitations and GEM protocols for multimode quantum memory.

**Method.** The authors propose a specific multi-photon interfacing scheme using off-resonant driving fields at a magic angle to create a closed wavevector loop, enabling long-lived storage.

**Model / system.** The system uses neutral atoms (e.g., Rb-87) excited to Rydberg states. The physics is modeled by solving coupled propagation equations for the probe pulse Rabi frequency and atomic coherence, incorporating interaction-induced decay terms.

**Key observables.** Rydberg spinwave lifetime ($	au$), signal decay curves, and controlled attenuation between stored excitation modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Near-zero momentum transfer ($\mathbf{k}_s \approx 0$), magic angle configuration, and the ability to manage inhomogeneous broadening from GEM.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on adiabatic elimination and approximations for pulse propagation in the semi-classical Hamiltonian framework. The initial problem assumes motional dephasing limits storage time.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the energy level structure, the experimental setup geometry forming a closed wavevector loop, the lifetime extension protocol sequence, and comparative decay data showing the extended coherence time.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the decoherence limitation in multiplexed Rydberg spinwave storage to proposing a novel four-photon interface. It then details the theoretical modeling using propagation equations and concludes by demonstrating the capability for long-lived, controllable multimode quantum memory operations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Collective Rydberg excitations offer strong and controllable interactions for quantum information processing, sensing, and nonlinear quantum optics, but their integration with temporally or spectrally multiplexed schemes, such as the Gradient Echo Memory (GEM) protocol, is hindered by rapid motional dephasing caused by the large spinwave wavevector. We demonstrate a new type of multi-photon addressing and interfacing scheme (with levels following the shape of the letter Ń) that allows us to generate collective Rydberg excitations with near-zero momentum transfer, extending the Rydberg spinwave lifetime almost tenfold. The scheme relies on two additional off-resonant driving fields arranged at a magic angle, forming a closed wavevector loop while remaining compatible with GEM-induced inhomogeneous broadening. This enables storage and manipulation of long-lived Rydberg spinwaves in a multimode quantum memory. Using microwave coupling between neighboring Rydberg states, we can control the attenuation between stored excitation modes by interaction-induced decay and demonstrate interaction-controlled diffraction of a retrieved optical signal. Our results reestablish compatibility between Rydberg excitations and GEM, providing a route toward multimode quantum memories with controllable long-range interactions and applications in quantum networking, sensing, and quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a significantly simplified experimental setup to test a theory of molecular rectification—the directed flow of molecules without external energy. By using a hydrophilic needle on a porous membrane, the authors can create and maintain an ionic concentration gradient at micrometer scales. This greatly lowers the technical barrier for experimentally validating this non-equilibrium transport mechanism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical thermodynamics and transport, the underlying principles of non-equilibrium steady states driven by interfacial energy minimization are highly relevant to open quantum systems modeling, particularly those involving dissipation or boundary effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the experimental verification of a theoretical model predicting sustained directed transport (molecular rectification) without external energy input.

**Main result.** A simplified, micrometer-scale experimental design is proposed that can sustain a measurable concentration difference and confirm the predicted directed flow cycle by observing steady liquid levels or pressure differences.

**Method.** The authors combine theoretical derivations based on chemical potential, Laplace's equation, and osmotic equilibrium with proposals for novel, low-complexity microfluidic/interfacial experiments.

**Model / system.** The system involves a hydrophobic porous membrane separating two liquid-vapor interfaces. Directed transport is driven by maintaining an ionic concentration gradient ($	ext{C}_a - 	ext{C}_b$) using a hydrophilic needle tip to locally modify the interface, analogous to semipermeable membranes.

**Key observables.** Sustained directed flow (steady state), measurable pressure differences ($P_n$ and $P_d$), and liquid-level differences ($	ext{A}_1$ vs $	ext{B}_1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fabrication scale reduced to micrometer level; theoretical maximum pressures up to 288,000 Pa for water.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes the charged groups act like a semipermeable membrane and employs ideal dilute solution approximations ($\pi = CRT$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup using a porous membrane and needle tip (Fig. 1, Fig. 3), showing interface deformation and ion release. Supplementary figures detail MD simulations of directed flux ($	ext{NNWM}$) and alternative experimental configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical basis for rectification via concentration gradients to proposing a simplified, low-complexity experimental platform using micrometer fabrication requirements, supported by both analytical derivations and simulation results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In our previous work, we proposed a theoretical model capable of inducing sustained directed transport without consuming information or external energy. However, the experimental verification schemes proposed previously were technically challenging, and the model has therefore not yet been experimentally tested. In this work, we propose a greatly simplified experimental design. By introducing hydrophilic functional groups onto the tip of a gold needle through surface modification and pressing the needle against one of two liquid-vapor interfaces, the compressed interface is maintained at a higher ionic concentration than the other interface, thereby sustaining a concentration difference between them. This design reduces the fabrication requirement from the nanometer scale to the micrometer scale, substantially lowering experimental complexity and facilitating experimental examination of the proposed mechanism.

</details>

### [When Can a Cavity Move a Mott Transition? A Spectral-Density Criterion within Gutzwiller Theory](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22283v1)

**Authors:** Nikhil Vamsodharakan Seshadri, Yu Zhang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22283v1>

**Summary.** The paper develops a criterion within Gutzwiller theory to determine when vacuum electromagnetic fluctuations can shift a Mott insulator transition. The key finding is that this requires the environment to provide finite, spatially varying spectral weight across the electronic hopping bonds. This bridges concepts from quantum optics and strongly correlated materials physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantitative link between quantum electrodynamics (vacuum fluctuations) and strongly correlated electron physics. It establishes that simple local density of states is insufficient; the spatial structure of the field coupling to electronic bonds dictates the phase transition outcome.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines the precise criterion by which vacuum electromagnetic fluctuations, such as those from a cavity or surface plasmons, can induce a shift in a bulk Mott metal-insulator transition.

**Main result.** A Mott transition shifts only if the coupling environment supplies finite thermodynamic spectral weight that varies spatially across the electronic hopping bonds. The magnitude of this shift depends on the joint frequency-spatial Pauli-Fierz density.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Gutzwiller variational method, deriving a critical criterion using the joint frequency–spatial Pauli–Fierz density ($J_{PF}$). This is complemented by Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) simulations for finite coordination systems.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a half-filled Hubbard model coupled to normalized electromagnetic modes or planar phonon-polariton surfaces. The Hamiltonian includes hopping, on-site repulsion ($U$), and the coupling term derived from the cavity environment.

**Key observables.** Mott boundary shift ($\Delta U_c$); Joint frequency–spatial Pauli–Fierz density ($J_{PF}$); Critical coefficient for phase boundary shift.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond-scale variation of spectral weight; Distance scaling ($d^{-3}$ to $d^{-5}$ crossover); Thermodynamic weight ($\eta_\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on Gutzwiller variational approximations, often assuming infinite coordination or using first-order expansions in mode density.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the cavity-induced displacement of the Brinkman–Rice boundary and show that only the bond-projected component of the local near-field weight drives the Mott transition shift, causing a crossover in scaling behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the criterion by analyzing the coupling Hamiltonian using Gutzwiller theory. It derives the leading shift formula based on $J_{PF}$, applies this to specific environments (like surface polaritons), and validates the results using VMC simulations for finite lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can vacuum electromagnetic fluctuations shift a bulk Mott transition? Within the Gutzwiller variational method, we derive a criterion that separates collective spectroscopic hybridization from thermodynamic phase control. We show that a Mott transition shifts only when the electromagnetic environment supplies finite thermodynamic spectral weight with bond-scale variation. A joint frequency--spatial Pauli--Fierz density gives the leading shift. Surface phonon polaritons yield a $d^{-3}$-to-$d^{-5}$ crossover, while finite-coordination variational Monte Carlo supports the predicted critical coefficient and $M/N$ scaling.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers studied a frequency-locked NV magnetometer by intentionally driving its feedback loop past its stable operational point. They modeled this using a discrete map exhibiting a period-doubling bifurcation, predicting an oscillation onset at $G_c=2$. Experimentally, they confirmed this transition, demonstrating the system's ability to self-oscillate and characterizing the approach to this critical threshold.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an excellent example of using nonlinear dynamics (bifurcation theory) to characterize and control a quantum sensor's operating regime, moving beyond simple linear sensing modes into controlled non-equilibrium dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates autonomous self-oscillating behavior in a frequency-locked Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centre magnetometer by deliberately operating the lock outside its stable regime.

**Main result.** The experiment successfully confirmed the theoretical prediction that the onset of self-oscillation occurs at a critical gain $G_c \approx 2.0$, validating the model's description of the flip bifurcation in the feedback loop.

**Method.** The authors employed continuous measurements on an NV magnetometer, analyzing the system dynamics by driving the frequency lock through a period-doubling (flip) bifurcation using software control.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a Continuous-Wave ODMR ensemble magnetometer. The operation is modeled as a one-dimensional discrete map whose stability and bifurcations are controlled by adjusting the dimensionless loop gain $G$.

**Key observables.** Coherent period-2 amplitude ($a_2$), sub-threshold orbit spread ($\sigma_f(G)$), and the critical gain threshold ($G_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Loop gain ($G$), calibration slope ($D_{	ext{cal}}$), true operating slope ($D_{	ext{true}}$), and the bifurcation point $G_c$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The nonlinearity is implemented in software after digitization, which limits its metrological advantage compared to physical nonlinearities.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize measurements showing the jump in coherent period-2 amplitude ($a_2$) around $G \approx 1.8-2.2$, and the divergence of the orbit spread following the predicted $\propto 1/(2 - G)$ trend.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical model based on a discrete map, derives the critical threshold condition ($G_c$), performs identifiability analysis, and concludes with experimental measurements confirming the bifurcation onset and divergence behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Feedback locking of a probe frequency to a spin resonance is the standard operating mode of precision quantum sensors. Here we deliberately operate such a lock outside its stable regime: a continuous-wave nitrogen-vacancy (NV) ensemble magnetometer, frequency-modulation (FM) locked to one flank of its optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR), is driven through the flip (period-doubling) bifurcation of its discrete feedback map by raising the software loop gain $G$. Beyond a critical gain $\Gc$ the lock becomes a self-sustained oscillator whose limit cycle is generated by the loop itself. We derive the threshold condition $\Gc = 2\,\Dcal/\Dtrue$, which identifies the measurable content of the threshold: the ratio of the transduction slope of the ODMR lock-in signal at calibration time $D_{cal}$ to its value at present $D_{true}$. We present an identifiability analysis showing which physical parameters this single scalar can and cannot distinguish, characterize the estimators of $\Gc$ under realistic noise, and report measurements on our current setup: an experimental bifurcation diagram with onset at $\Gc \approx 2$ as predicted for a self-calibrated loop, sub-threshold critical fluctuations following the predicted $\sqrt{G/(2-G)}$ divergence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theoretical framework for Quantum Electrodynamics in dispersive, dissipative, and time-varying materials. It establishes a generalized fluctuation-dissipation theorem applicable to non-equilibrium systems by treating temporal modulation exactly. The results demonstrate that accurately modeling dispersion and losses is critical for predicting phenomena like enhanced thermal emission and the dynamical Casimir effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it extends quantum electrodynamics into complex, realistic open quantum systems. The exact treatment of dispersion and dissipation in time-varying media provides powerful tools for understanding light-matter interaction dynamics beyond simple weak-coupling approximations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a rigorous theoretical framework for fluctuational quantum electrodynamics (QED) when applied to time-varying media that exhibit both frequency dispersion and dissipation.

**Main result.** The theory successfully derives a generalized Fermi Golden Rule applicable to non-equilibrium, time-modulated systems, showing that treating dispersion and losses exactly is crucial for accurate predictions of thermal emission and vacuum fluctuations.

**Method.** The authors utilize Kubo's linear response theory extended to time-modulated media, employing the Green's dyadic formalism in the frequency domain to handle dispersive and dissipative effects non-perturbatively.

**Model / system.** The system is a macroscopic dielectric body (e.g., SiC half-space) whose optical properties are modulated periodically in time. The theory models the interaction between the electromagnetic field and the material polarization density $P(r, t)$.

**Key observables.** Local Density of States (LDOS), spectral density of thermal radiation, correlation functions of the electric field, and entangled polariton pairs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulation strength ($\alpha$), modulation frequency ($\Omega$), temperature ($T$), and material dispersion/loss parameters ($\epsilon(\omega)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes linearity for the media; key advancements overcome limitations of previous work that treated modulation perturbatively or assumed lossless behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spectral density vs. frequency comparisons, contrasting results from non-dispersive versus dispersive time modulation in a SiC half-space, and showing sideband structures related to phonon frequencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the generalized fluctuation-dissipation theorem for time-modulated systems (Chunk 1 & 2). It then applies this formalism to calculate the spectral density of thermal emission, explicitly comparing the necessity of including dispersion versus assuming non-dispersive modulation (Chunk 3). Finally, it analyzes the temperature dependence and low-temperature limit of these fluctuations (Chunk 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the theoretical framework of fluctuational quantum electrodynamics in frequency-dispersive and dissipative time-varying media. Our theory accounts for dispersion and losses in the temporal modulation, which is treated in an exact manner, without relying on perturbative methods. Thus, our work constitutes the first consistent quantization of the electromagnetic field in time-modulated material bodies. We derive a Fermi Golden Rule for time-varying media and use it to define the local density of states for these time-dependent systems, which includes both loss and gain contributions. Additionally, we prove the equivalence between the quantum Fermi Golden Rule and the power emitted by a classical harmonic point dipole. Moreover, we show that neglecting the dispersive and dissipative nature of the time modulation leads to erroneous predictions for both slow and fast modulations. Furthermore, we analyze the thermal radiation emitted by a time-varying material body, revealing new features in the enhancement of thermal emission in time-varying media. Finally, we study the dynamical Casimir effect, showing how the time modulation amplifies vacuum fluctuations and generates entangled pairs of polaritons exhibiting non-local spatial correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work applies concepts from quantum complexity theory to black hole evaporation within the AdS/CFT framework. It predicts that holographic complexity exhibits a Page-like transition when the radiation subsystem grows past half its size. This transition is attributed to an 'island' region, suggesting this mechanism resolves a deep paradox analogous to the information loss problem.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gravity/QFT, the core methodology—analyzing how information measures (complexity) undergo sharp transitions based on subsystem size relative to a critical point—is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether a Page-like transition occurs for the holographic complexity of subsystems during black hole evaporation, analogous to the resolution of the information paradox.

**Main result.** A Page-like transition is predicted in the radiation complexity due to the emergence of an 'island' contribution when using the Complexity=Volume (CV) proposal, which resolves a complexity paradox similar to the information paradox.

**Method.** The authors employ quantum information tools like basis-spectrum decomposition and holographic complexity calculations, comparing them against circuit models for subsystem complexity.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an evaporating Anti-de Sitter (AdS) black hole coupled to a bath, analyzed using concepts from AdS/CFT correspondence and quantum circuits representing the interior dynamics.

**Key observables.** Subsystem complexity (general and holographic), radiation complexity, and the volume contribution of the 'island' region.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The subsystem size relative to half ($	ext{size} > 1/2$), and the time evolution across the Page time.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Complexity=Volume (CV) proposal for subregions, and models black hole evaporation via random circuit evolution on an interior subsystem.

**Figures summary.** Figures sketch maximal volume slices in AdS/Schwarzschild diagrams showing how the island forms post-Page time, causing a dramatic change in the calculated volume contribution.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from established results on complexity transitions for random states to applying these concepts to the holographic complexity of an evaporating black hole, culminating in the prediction and interpretation of the Page-like transition via the island structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent results demonstrate that there exists a sharp, Page-like transition for the complexity of subsystems of Haar-random states as their fractional subsystem size surpasses one half. They further demonstrate that this transition also occurs for the holographic complexity of boundary subregions of eternal AdS black holes, assuming the Complexity$=$Volume (CV) proposal for subregions. We interpret this transition as a crossover from spectrum-dominated to basis-dominated subsystem complexity, reflecting the breakdown of approximate thermality beyond half-system size. We then apply this reasoning to an evaporating AdS black hole coupled to a bath, modeled by a quantum circuit undergoing random evolution on an interior subsystem of diminishing size. Using the basis-spectrum decomposition of subsystem complexity, we argue for a similar Page-like transition in the radiation complexity. We then show, using CV for subregions, that the same transition appears in the holographic complexity of the radiation subsystem through the emergence of an island, whose volume gives the dominant contribution. We argue that the island volume contribution resolves an apparent complexity paradox analogous to the information paradox.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an Optical-Field-Dependent Nonlinear Quantum Battery model that predicts a superlinear advantage in charging power due to multiphoton absorption. It provides both the rigorous theoretical derivation and a detailed experimental blueprint using superconducting circuits, successfully demonstrating that the achievable charging time saturates the fundamental quantum speed limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges advanced quantum optics theory (nonlinear cavity QED, multiphoton effects) with cutting-edge solid-state hardware (superconducting qubits), providing concrete pathways to realizing enhanced energy storage devices based on fundamental quantum mechanics principles.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To enhance the charging performance of quantum batteries (QBs) by developing a theoretical model for an optical-field-dependent nonlinear quantum battery and demonstrating its feasibility in superconducting circuits.

**Main result.** The proposed Nonlinear Quantum Battery exhibits a superlinear quantum advantage in charging power, confirming that this enhancement stems from multiphoton absorption effects. Furthermore, the experimental design successfully verifies that the charging time saturates the theoretical Quantum Speed Limit (QSL).

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involves deriving an unbiased nonlinear interaction function by comparing energy metrics and QSL benchmarks; experimentally, it uses dispersive readout on superconducting circuits to measure charging time.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an Optical-Field-Dependent Nonlinear Quantum Battery (O-NQB) comprising a two-level atom coupled to an optical cavity. The physical realization utilizes superconducting quantum circuits with tunable Josephson junctions, allowing precise control over the coupling strength and resonance conditions.

**Key observables.** Stored energy ($\Delta E(t)$), average charging power ($\langle P(t) angle$), charging time ($t_f$), and relative entropy of coherence ($C_{	ext{rel}}(ho)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinearity order $k\geq2$, multiphoton absorption number $n$ (tunable in the range 2 to 6), detuning ($\Delta$), and coupling strength ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on establishing an unbiased benchmark by equating QSL times across different models, eliminating arbitrary parameter tuning.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the O-NQB model, energy exchange dynamics in nonlinear QBs, and time evolution plots showing coherence decay and energy accumulation under varying detunings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from theoretical modeling (deriving the Hamiltonian and finding the unbiased nonlinearity) to quantitative analysis (comparing charging metrics against the QSL benchmark), culminating in a detailed experimental design scheme using superconducting circuits for verification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A quantum battery is a novel energy storage device that operates on the principles of quantum mechanics. To enhance the charging performance of quantum batteries and further provide theoretical support for their physical implementation, we constructed an optical-field-dependent nonlinear quantum battery model. Meanwhile, we solved for the unbiased form of nonlinear interactions in this model, where the charging power of the proposed model exhibits a superlinear quantum advantage, and the charging time saturates the quantum speed limit. Through theoretical analysis, we confirm that this quantum advantage arises from the quantum effect of multiphoton absorption. Subsequently, with the derived nonlinear function form, we further investigated other properties of this nonlinear quantum battery. Finally, an experimental design scheme for this nonlinear quantum battery in superconducting quantum circuits is presented.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally characterizes the detrimental effect of photon-phonon coupling on microcavities intended for quantum state generation. By measuring excess phase noise as a function of temperature, the authors confirm that this noise originates from thermal acoustic fluctuations within the crystal lattice. The findings emphasize that controlling this environmental coupling is a necessary prerequisite for utilizing these cavities in robust quantum information applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses decoherence mechanisms (phonon coupling) in solid-state photonic platforms used for generating entangled states, which is a core challenge in practical quantum networking and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how photon-phonon coupling introduces excess noise into microcavities, thereby degrading their performance when used as sources for quantum states.

**Main result.** The observed excess noise is confirmed to be phononic in nature and its temperature dependence allows for the quantification of the coupling rate. Controlling this source of environmental noise is crucial for implementing these devices in quantum networks.

**Method.** The authors analyzed the spectral covariance matrix of the output field, measuring excess phase noise as a function of temperature across different resonant conditions.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ micro-cavities coupled to a thermal phonon reservoir. The analysis uses input-output formalism and Langevin equations to model the field dynamics, incorporating noise terms from acoustic fluctuations.

**Key observables.** Excess phase noise power ratio ($\eta$), spectral covariance matrix components ($V(\Omega)$), and temperature coefficients of loss ($\delta\eta/\delta T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature dependence ($T$) and driving field power ($P$); the coupling rate was estimated to be proportional to $TP$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating the phonon reservoir as unaffected by the electromagnetic field, ignoring parametric processes below threshold, and simplifying acoustic correlation functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show spectral matrix components measurements for multiple cavities under different resonance conditions ($R1$ and $R2$), illustrating noise power ratios versus temperature dependence.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using input-output formalism to measuring excess noise, quantifying its temperature dependence across two distinct resonant conditions, and concluding with recommendations for noise mitigation in quantum network applications.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum systems, the contact with the environment is detrimental to the purity of the state, thus limiting the practical use of entangled sources in quantum information applications. This loss of purity is observed in the form of additional noise in the tomography of the state. We investigate this noise dependence in $Si_3N_4$ micro-cavities, previously used for quantum state generation, and demonstrate that the dependence of this noise on the temperature is compatible with a coupling of the photonic chips to a thermal reservoir. The control of this noise source is a necessary condition for the efficient implementation of these devices as sources of entangled states in quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a systematic effective field theory framework for nonlinear fluctuating hydrodynamics in 1D systems. It overcomes limitations of previous models by enforcing key thermodynamic symmetries like the Maxwell relation through a master potential. The work successfully predicts superdiffusive transport regimes and provides robust numerical methods for non-Gaussian equilibrium states.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical tools for describing non-equilibrium dynamics in low dimensions where simple perturbative methods fail, connecting macroscopic hydrodynamics directly to fundamental thermodynamic symmetries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a general and systematic effective field theory approach for nonlinear fluctuating hydrodynamics in one dimension that rigorously respects fundamental symmetry constraints of local equilibrium states.

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct EFTs by introducing a master potential that manifestly enforces the Maxwell relation, demonstrating superdiffusive transport ($z=3/2$) alongside diffusive modes in a minimal non-Gaussian model.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical construction based on symmetry principles (Maxwell relation) with numerical simulations of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) and advanced sampling techniques like tilted ensembles.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one-dimensional interacting systems exhibiting emergent phenomena, modeled via nonlinear fluctuating hydrodynamics described by coupled stochastic Langevin-type equations. The analysis is tested on a minimal model featuring two interacting hydrodynamic modes with a non-Gaussian stationary equilibrium measure.

**Key observables.** Superdiffusive algebraic exponents ($z$), static covariance matrices ($C_{ab}$), and the preservation of symmetry relations like $B_{a|b} = B_{b|a}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality (one dimension) and the nature of the equilibrium measure (non-Gaussian vs. Gaussian).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous NLFHD formulations are shown to have conceptual issues when applied to non-Gaussian measures, necessitating a symmetry-preserving EFT construction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structure factors showing superdiffusive and diffusive broadening over time; other figures compare numerical sampling methods (Metropolis vs. exact) for minimal models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying limitations in existing NLFHD/MCE approaches to developing a symmetry-enforcing EFT via a master potential, followed by applying this framework to analyze transport properties and test the numerical stability of the resulting SPDEs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Describing emergent macroscopic phenomena in low spatial dimensions is known to be notoriously challenging, primarily due to strong interactions that render perturbative approaches inapplicable. On the other hand, low-dimensional systems host a wealth of unorthodox phenomena. A prominent example is the emergence of superdiffusive transport in one-dimensional interacting systems, traditionally studied in the framework of nonlinear fluctuating hydrodynamics. After identifying and discussing internal inconsistencies in the previous formulations, in this work we develop a general and systematic approach for constructing effective field theories of one-dimensional hydrodynamic systems in the form of coupled stochastic Langevin-type equations compatible with the physical requirements of local equilibrium states such as the thermodynamic Maxwell relation and fluctuation-dissipation symmetry. We implemented a general numerical integration scheme and exemplified our construction on a simple model of two interacting hydrodynamic modes with a non-Gaussian stationary equilibrium measure.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how self-propelled particles confined by a harmonic potential behave under different activity levels. By analyzing multiple dynamical signatures—including MSD and PSD—the authors distinguish between regimes where residual activity dominates versus those where the activity is effectively quenched by the confining force field.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, multi-diagnostic framework (beyond just distribution shape) to characterize non-equilibrium steady states in active matter systems, which is highly relevant for understanding energy dissipation and effective potentials in complex physical settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to reliably identify and quantify the signature of residual or remnant activity in the dynamics of an Active Brownian Particle (ABP) confined within a harmonic potential.

**Main result.** The nature of the particle's motion depends critically on the ratio between the persistence time ($	au_R$) and the confinement equilibration time ($	au_k$): $	au_R \ll 	au_k$ shows residual activity signatures, while $	au_R \gg 	au_k$ suggests the activity is balanced by the restoring force.

**Method.** The analysis combines analytical calculations of correlation functions and position distributions with numerical simulations solving the underlying Langevin equations for the system.

**Model / system.** The model describes an ABP undergoing self-propulsion ($V$) confined in 2D by a harmonic potential $U(r) = rac{1}{2}kr^2$. The dynamics are governed by coupled Langevin equations involving propulsion, confinement restoring forces, and thermal noise.

**Key observables.** Position distribution ($P_{ss}(r)$), residual/resultant velocity components ($\mathbf{v}_{	ext{res}}$), Mean Square Displacement (MSD), and Power Spectral Density (PSD).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Activity strength quantified by the Péclet number ($	ext{Pe}$); characteristic timescales $	au_R$ (persistence time) and $	au_k$ (confinement equilibration time).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on examining two extreme limits ($	au_R \ll 	au_k$ and $	au_R \gg 	au_k$) and assumes that cross-terms involving uncorrelated Brownian velocity noises vanish.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate position distributions ($P(x, t), P(y, t)$) and steady-state radial distributions $P_{ss}(r)$, comparing analytical predictions to simulations across different activity regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the dynamics by deriving theoretical expressions for observables (like MSD and distribution functions) in the two limiting cases ($	au_R \ll 	au_k$ and $	au_R \gg 	au_k$), contrasting these results with the passive Brownian counterpart ($V=0$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A confined self-propelled particle exhibits a range of intriguing dynamical phenomena dictated by the interplay between the intrinsic activity of the particle and the imposed confinement. This competition manifests as a crossover in the steady-state position distribution of a harmonically bound active Brownian particle (HBABP) from Boltzmann-like to bimodal, commonly recognized as the passive and active regimes, respectively, upon variations in activity and confinement strength. We present a comprehensive analysis of the resultant dynamics of an HBABP employing analytical calculations and numerical simulations, examining the variations in the position distribution, residual or resultant velocity, mean square displacement, power spectral density, and effective harmonic confinement at varying activities in the characteristic regimes across the crossover. These analyses provide a reliable identification of the signature of residual or remnant activity in ABP dynamics after being impeded by the harmonic confinement. Our results show that the resultant HBABP dynamics in the regime with a Boltzmann-like position distribution is dominated by residual activity, and the motion in the other regime, with a bimodal position distribution, is similar to that of a harmonically bound Brownian particle--devoid of residual activity--at a displaced position, where the activity is balanced by the restoring force field.

</details>
