**Summary.** The paper investigates the feasibility of measuring topological order crossovers in the Wen–plaquette model on noisy quantum computers. Using local plaquette stabilizers and Wilson loops as diagnostics, the authors show that these signatures remain surprisingly robust even when subjected to realistic hardware imperfections like disorder and amplified circuit noise. This provides a practical pathway for using near-term quantum processors to probe fundamental aspects of topological matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges abstract topological theory with near-term quantum hardware constraints. By providing concrete metrics for signal degradation due to realistic noise models, it offers a crucial roadmap for realizing fault-tolerant topological quantum computation using current NISQ devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether signatures of topological crossovers, crucial for robust quantum information processing, remain resolvable when implemented on current Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that plaquette-sector signatures are substantially stable deep within the strong-stabilizer regime and exhibit only weak degradation when extended to a physical 2D IBM processor under local coherent perturbations.

**Method.** A two-stage strategy is employed: first, characterizing crossovers on small systems using variational circuits; second, scaling this approach to a physical lattice while quantifying robustness against disorder, noise, and non-Hermitian effects.

**Model / system.** The Wen–plaquette model is used, which exhibits topological order characterized by plaquette stabilizers ($F_{i,j}$). The study analyzes crossovers between stabilizer-dominated, trivial, or disorder-dominated regimes on IBM Quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** Local plaquette stabilizers ($F_i$), nonlocal Wilson loops ($W$), and the lattice-averaged plaquette response.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strengths of coupling constants ($J$ vs. $g$) defining different topological regimes, disorder strength ($h$), and circuit noise/depth ($\gamma$, identity layers).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes that controlled finite-size characterization can inform a scalable hardware implementation, although limitations regarding gate error rates and classical feedforward are noted.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Wen–plaquette model structure (lattice diagrams), schematic comparisons of clean vs. disordered crossover trajectories, and workflow diagrams for implementing measurements using ancilla qubits or coherent representative-qubit methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the Wen–plaquette Hamiltonian, details diagnostic tools like plaquettes and Wilson loops, develops simulation protocols (variational circuits, projector construction), analyzes the system's response to various perturbations (disorder, noise), and concludes by validating the approach on a physical 2D processor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological phases of matter provide a promising route toward robust quantum information processing, but on present-day noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices the experimentally relevant question is whether signatures of topological crossovers remain resolvable under realistic imperfections. Here, we address this question in the Wen--plaquette model through a two-stage strategy on the IBM Quantum hardware. We first use a tractable system to systematically characterize crossover signatures. Using variationally compiled equilibrium and quench-generated states, we resolve crossovers between stabilizer-dominated and trivial or disorder-dominated regimes through local plaquette stabilizers and a Wilson loop, and quantify their robustness against static disorder, deliberately amplified circuit noise, and effective non-Hermitian fields. The quench dynamics further reveal that plaquette-sector signatures remain substantially more stable deep in the strong-stabilizer regime than near the finite-size crossover. Building on the properties established in the small system, we extend the implementation to a physical two-dimensional IBM processor using a layered representative-qubit construction. The resulting lattice-averaged plaquette response exhibits only weak degradation under intentionally amplified local coherent perturbations. Together, these results connect controlled finite-size characterization with a scalable hardware implementation, providing a practical route for preparing and probing topological signatures on near-term quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates that tin-vacancy centers in diamond can generate single photons with extremely high indistinguishability ($\approx 0.999$). By meticulously separating emitter physics from technical noise sources, the authors establish this platform as a leading candidate for building long-distance quantum networks and photonic quantum information processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete experimental metrics (high visibility, quantified intrinsic indistinguishability) crucial for assessing solid-state emitters as viable nodes in future quantum communication infrastructure, directly impacting photonic QI protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is demonstrating highly indistinguishable single photons from a tin-vacancy (SnV) spin qubit in diamond for use in long-distance quantum networks.

**Main result.** The authors achieved raw Hong-Ou-Mandel interference visibilities exceeding 0.95, establishing an intrinsic photon indistinguishability up to 0.999, even after frequency conversion to the telecom band.

**Method.** Measurements involve characterizing single-photon purity using Hanbury-Brown-Twiss setups and measuring indistinguishability via Hong-Ou-Mandel interference, complemented by detailed noise modeling using TCSPC data.

**Model / system.** The platform is the SnV center in diamond, acting as a solid-state quantum emitter. The work models photon generation fidelity, decoherence effects, and simulates performance improvements in quantum repeater links.

**Key observables.** Raw Hong–Ou–Mandel (HOM) interference visibilities (>0.95), intrinsic indistinguishability ($\approx 0.999$), single-photon purity ($g^{(2)}(0)$), and spectral linewidths ($\sim 30$ MHz).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Intrinsic indistinguishability, coherence times (electron/nuclear spin), temporal gating fractions (e.g., 65% of the wave packet), and frequency conversion efficiency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis separates intrinsic emitter properties from technical imperfections, assuming that residual reflections are the main limitation to raw purity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Rabi oscillations, photoluminescence excitation spectra, $g^{(2)}(	au)$ measurements showing background subtraction, and comparisons of HOM visibility in visible versus telecom regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first demonstrating high single-photon purity from the SnV center, then quantifying indistinguishability via HOM interference, and finally extending this potential by showing preservation of indistinguishability after quantum frequency conversion to the telecom C-band for network applications.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum networks promise secure communication, distributed sensing and modular quantum computing by interconnecting distant quantum nodes through photonic links. Extending such networks beyond metropolitan distances requires quantum repeaters to overcome the exponential attenuation of photons in optical fiber. Across all architectures, a key requirement is the indistinguishability of single photons, which directly impacts the fidelity of photonic operations based on two-photon interference, such as Bell-state measurements and fusion gates. Here, we demonstrate generation of highly indistinguishable single photons from a coherently excited tin-vacancy center in diamond, achieving raw Hong-Ou-Mandel interference visibilities exceeding 0.95. By separating intrinsic emitter properties from technical imperfections, we show that decoherence plays a negligible role and that the remaining limitations are predominantly technical in nature, arriving at an intrinsic indistinguishability of up to 0.999. We further show that quantum frequency conversion to the telecom C-band preserves the photon indistinguishability. In combination with the long-lived electron and nuclear spin coherence times, these results establish tin-vacancy centers in diamond as a competitive platform for long-distance quantum networks and photonic quantum information processing. We further substantiate this potential through Monte Carlo simulations of a quantum-repeater link, demonstrating that the SnV-center platform surpasses the bound set by direct transmission.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present an MR-based technique to determine the absolute chirality of single molecules by analyzing symmetry-breaking dynamics among coupled nuclear spins. They show that while standard MR spectra are blind to chirality, measuring spin evolution after polarizing the system reveals a unique signature dependent on molecular handedness. This method offers a novel quantum tool for chemistry and biology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful quantum sensing platform using solid-state defects (NV centers) to probe fundamental symmetries related to molecular structure, bridging quantum information concepts with chirality detection in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to achieve direct molecular chiral discrimination at the single-molecule level using magnetic resonance (MR) spectroscopy.

**Main result.** Chirality is successfully determined by observing how the spin dynamics diverge when the system transitions from unpolarized to partially polarized states, breaking the joint symmetry of mirror reflection and time reversal in spin space.

**Method.** The method involves constructing and analyzing the symmetry-breaking dynamics of nearby nuclear spins coupled to an electron spin using single-molecule MR techniques.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, probing the quantum three-body dynamics of coupled $^{13}	ext{C}$ nuclear spins. The analysis involves Hamiltonian modeling and measuring coherence under dynamical decoupling sequences.

**Key observables.** Chirality-related phases ($\Phi(ij)$), population differences ($P|_{+angle} - P_{|0angle}$), and the deviation of spin dynamics from unpolarized expectations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron spin gap ($\Delta$), magnetic field strength, and hyperfine coupling parameters derived from DFT calculations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that molecular chirality manifests as a breaking of MT-symmetry in the measured spin dynamics when polarization is induced.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy spectra comparison (Fig. 1(c)), dynamical profiles showing divergence upon polarization (Figs. 2, 3), and methods for determining NV axis direction using electric fields (Fig. S8).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for chiral discrimination to detailing the MR-based measurement technique, analyzing two-body and then three-body spin dynamics under varying polarization levels, and finally confirming the results via field reversal and comparison with theoretical calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molecular chirality plays a crucial role in physics, chemistry, life sciences and pharmacology. Nowadays, the chiral discrimination and control at the single-molecule level is urgently needed to reveal the origin of the chirality-relevant phenomena by recovering the information disturbed by the ensemble averaging. The method of magnetic resonance (MR), as one of powerful tools for structure analysis, is blind to the molecular chirality in the absence of a chiral reagent. Here we propose and experimentally demonstrate a direct MR-based method for determining the chirality at the single-molecule level through constructing the symmetry-breaking dynamics of nearby nuclear spins. In principle, the mirror asymmetry of two enantiomers in real space is manifested by breaking the joint symmetry of the mirror reflection and time reversal in spin space under spin dynamics. Experimentally, two enantiomers are indistinguishable from the dynamics of strongly-coupled but unpolarized nuclear spins, but diverge evidently in the dynamical results that break the field-inversion symmetry after spins are polarized. Our method and results will benefit the study of chirality-induced properties in the fields of chemistry and biology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how heavy quarks thermalize and diffuse in a hot medium by extending standard Langevin descriptions to include memory effects. It calculates transport coefficients like the diffusion coefficient using correlation functions derived from generalized Fokker-Planck equations. The findings show that while memory introduces oscillatory behavior, it maintains consistency with established results in certain limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The incorporation of non-Markovian dynamics is crucial for accurately modeling relaxation processes in strongly coupled systems where memory effects cannot be ignored, providing a more realistic description than simple white noise approximations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates heavy-quark (HQ) equilibration and correlation functions within a thermal medium, specifically focusing on incorporating non-Markovian memory effects.

**Main result.** Memory effects qualitatively alter the transient structure of current correlations but do not change the transport coefficient in the non-relativistic limit; however, they can induce damped, oscillatory equilibration in the relativistic case.

**Method.** The analysis employs kinetic theory approaches, utilizing generalized Langevin equations and comparing solutions derived from both the generalized Fokker-Planck equation and the corresponding Langevin framework.

**Model / system.** The system models heavy quarks (charm/beauty) propagating through a hot, dense medium like the Quark-Gluon Plasma. The dynamics are governed by stochastic processes incorporating memory kernels into the standard Markovian description.

**Key observables.** Heavy-quark diffusion coefficient ($D_s$), HQ momentum distribution function $f(\mathbf{p}, t)$, and current–current correlation functions $\langle \mathbf{j}(t) \cdot \mathbf{j}(0) angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), heavy quark mass ($m$), drag coefficient ($A$), and memory time ($	au_m$). The analysis compares non-relativistic (NR) and relativistic limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes space homogeneity, uses the Green-Kubo formula to relate transport coefficients to equilibrium correlations, and often relies on approximations like constant drag coefficients or assumed exponential decay forms for correlation functions at large times.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of $D_s(t^*)$ calculated via integrating the current-current correlation function $C(t)$, showing plateaus that approach an asymptotic value, which is sensitive to the chosen integration cutoff time $t^*$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the foundational Langevin/Fokker-Planck framework (Markovian case), extending it to include memory effects via generalized equations, calculating key observables like $D_s$ using Green-Kubo relations in both NR and relativistic limits, and finally comparing numerical solutions across different time regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compute the charm-quark current-current correlation function within the Langevin framework and extract the heavy-quark diffusion coefficient using the Green--Kubo formula. The formalism is further extended to evaluate the correlation function using a generalized Langevin equation that incorporates memory effects via an exponentially decaying memory kernel. We find that while memory effects qualitatively modify the transient structure of the current correlations, the value of the transport coefficient remains unchanged in the non-relativistic limit. In addition, we investigate heavy-quark thermalization in the presence of memory and compare the non-equilibrium solution of a generalized Fokker--Planck equation with the one obtained from the generalized Langevin equation in the non-relativistic limit. We also consider the relativistic version of the correlated noise case and observe that memory effects can give rise to a damped, oscillatory equilibration of the heavy quark in a non-Markovian bath.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using D-Wave quantum annealers to estimate the energy gap of the Transverse-Field Ising Hamiltonian by inducing and measuring Rabi oscillations. By applying a triangular-wave modulated magnetic field, they circumvent classical limitations on accessing excited states. This technique proves a promising pathway for simulating complex quantum systems on current quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges fundamental condensed matter physics (Ising model) with near-term quantum hardware capabilities, providing a concrete protocol for accessing excited state information beyond simple ground-state optimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is estimating the energy gap between the ground and first excited states of the Transverse-Field Ising Hamiltonian for large systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a method using D-Wave quantum annealers to estimate these gaps by observing Rabi resonance, confirming applicability on small 2D lattices.

**Method.** They propose inducing Rabi oscillations using a triangular-wave modulated magnetic field on the quantum annealer, and then fitting the resulting spectral response with a Lorentzian function to extract the gap frequency.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the Transverse-Field Ising model ($\hat{H}_p$), which models competition between quantum fluctuations and interactions. The computation is performed using a D-Wave quantum annealer, simulating periodic driving via linear control of the longitudinal magnetic field $g(t)$.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the population transfer (or ground state occupation probability) as a function of the sweeping driving frequency ($\omega_{drive}$), which exhibits resonance peaks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the energy gap $\omega_{01}$, the driving strength, and hardware limitations such as the time resolution ($\approx 10	ext{ ns}$) and waveform programmability limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that higher harmonics of the triangular wave are negligible near resonance, and it relies on approximating complex periodic driving fields with linear control over $g(t)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate annealing schedules, the time evolution of the magnetic field $g(t)$, and crucially, the spectral response curves showing Lorentzian fits to determine $\omega_{01}$ for various qubit numbers and 2D lattices.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical problem (energy gap estimation) and computational limitations. It then details the novel methodology using D-Wave hardware to simulate Rabi oscillations via triangular driving fields. Finally, it presents numerical results confirming the method's accuracy on small systems like 2-qubit and $2 	imes 3$ lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The transverse-field Ising model is a fundamental quantum spin system that captures the competition between quantum fluctuations and interactions, playing a central role in studies of quantum phase transitions and non-equilibrium dynamics. However, classical computations of ground and excited states in large-scale or high-dimensional systems are severely limited by the exponential growth of the Hilbert space. Here, we propose a novel approach using a D-Wave quantum annealer, where a triangular-wave oscillating magnetic field is applied to induce Rabi oscillations, allowing the estimation of energy gaps between the ground and excited states. Unlike conventional quantum annealing methods limited to ground-state searches, this approach can directly access excited-state information. It is potentially applicable to larger systems, providing a new avenue for quantum-device-based simulation. The validity of the method is demonstrated through numerical simulations of relatively small systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a generalized framework for calculating entanglement across topological interfaces connecting different Conformal Field Theories. The key finding is that duality interfaces project the system's quantum correlations onto specific symmetry sectors, mirroring symmetry resolution but arising naturally from the physical boundary itself. This provides a rigorous tool to quantify how dual theories constrain quantum information at their junction.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism described—where physical boundaries naturally impose symmetry constraints on entanglement—provides a powerful theoretical tool applicable to understanding boundary effects and correlation structure in complex many-body systems beyond simple thermalization assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work extends the theory of entanglement through topological defects/interfaces, specifically focusing on interfaces connecting two distinct Conformal Field Theories (CFTs), particularly when these theories are dual to each other.

**Main result.** For duality interfaces, the reduced density matrix acts as a projector onto a fixed-charge subsector of the vacuum's entanglement spectrum, naturally reflecting quantum correlations back into the entangling interval.

**Method.** The authors utilize a framework involving 'twisted states' to construct the full reduced density matrix (RDM) and employ relative entropy to quantify the distinguishability between the interface RDM and the vacuum RDM.

**Model / system.** The study is set in 1+1 dimensional CFTs, with specific examples including the free boson CFT and systems exhibiting duality relations (e.g., Kramers-Wannier or T-duality).

**Key observables.** Reduced Density Matrix (RDM), Entanglement Entropy ($S$), Relative Entropy, and the entanglement spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nature of the CFTs (diagonal/non-diagonal rational, free boson) and the specific type of interface (duality vs. general).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous foundational theories for interface entropies are noted to be incompatible with numerical results; the framework extends prior work on defects to distinct CFT interfaces.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mapping between two phases P and Q connected by an interface I (and $\bar{I}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework for entanglement through topological defects, then applies it to study interfaces connecting distinct CFTs, culminating in a detailed analysis of how duality interfaces project quantum correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement through interfaces has attracted considerable attention in 2d conformal field theory (CFT). However, it is known that field-theoretic predictions based on the existing framework are in general incompatible with numerical results [1-4]. A new framework for entanglement through topological defects was recently proposed in [5]. It provides a general description of entanglement through topological defects and successfully reproduces the numerical results for the Ising model in all tested cases and regimes. The key insight is that the relevant quantum correlations are encoded in twisted states, allowing for the construction of the full reduced density matrix (RDM). In this work we pursue two objectives. First, we provide new examples by studying defects in the free boson CFT. Second, we extend the framework to topological interfaces connecting two, possibly distinct, CFTs. Of particular interest are interfaces relating dual theories. We show that the reduced density matrix for a duality interface is the projection of the vacuum reduced density matrix onto a single symmetry sector, closely paralleling the framework of symmetry resolution. Unlike symmetry resolution, however, the projection is imposed by the physical interface itself, demonstrating that duality interfaces reflect quantum correlations back into the entangling interval. We establish this mechanism for diagonal and non-diagonal rational CFTs as well as the free boson CFT. Relative entropy allows us to quantify the distinguishability of the duality interface RDM from the vacuum RDM.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Higher Berry Curvature (HBC) as a novel, geometric tool to calculate a topological order parameter ($\Omega$) for quantum spin chains. This integer invariant quantifies the spin carried by gapless edge modes created when the chain is cut. By applying this method to various models, the authors demonstrate that $\Omega$ successfully maps out phase transitions and classifies different ground states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful geometric tool—the HBC integral—to calculate topological invariants for interacting quantum systems where simple single-particle descriptions fail. It connects bulk topology directly to measurable edge properties (edge modes), which is highly relevant for understanding boundary physics in condensed matter and AMO settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To define a geometric, integer-valued topological order parameter for quantum spin chains that can distinguish between different phases where traditional methods fail.

**Main result.** The Higher Berry Curvature (HBC) integral yields an integer invariant ($\Omega$) that quantifies the spin of gapless edge modes exposed by an entanglement cut. This invariant successfully distinguishes multiple gapped sectors in models like the staggered J1-J2 chain.

**Method.** The method involves constructing a closed, extended family of Hamiltonians and integrating the associated Higher Berry Curvature (HBC) 3-form over the parameter space ($S^3$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum spin chains, exemplified by $S=1/2$, $S=1$, and $S=3/2$ spin-Peierls chains, as well as the staggered $J_1$-$J_2$ chain. The analysis uses Hamiltonian extensions parameterized over a 3-manifold.

**Key observables.** Higher Berry Curvature (HBC), Topological Order Parameter ($\Omega$), Gapless edge modes count, Spin content of entanglement boundary states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin quantum number ($S$), Dimerization parameter ($\delta$), Coupling constants ($J_1, J_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method requires constructing a smooth family of normalized states over a compact manifold and relies on specific extensions to define the relative invariant.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the HBC ($\omega$) and the resulting order parameter ($\Omega$) for spin-Peierls chains across varying staggering parameters, showing $\Omega$ approaching integer values (0 or 1). Another figure shows the phase diagram of the $J_1$-$J_2$ chain with plots of $\omega(\alpha)$ for different phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the concept by first defining HBC as a diagnostic tool, then constructing an interpolating Hamiltonian family over $S^3$, calculating the quantized invariant ($\Omega$), and finally applying this machinery to various physical models (Spin-Peierls, $J_1$-$J_2$) to map out phase transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the higher Berry curvature (HBC) can be used to count the gapless edge modes created by an entanglement cut, and thus defines an integer-valued topological order parameter for quantum spin chains. Given an individual spin-chain Hamiltonian, we construct an extending family by interpolating to a reference product Néel state, and show that the integral of the HBC over this extension is equal to the ordinary Berry phase of half of the chain swept out in response to an \textit{infinitesimal} field. It thus counts the spin of the gapless edge modes exposed by the cut, and a change in its integer value signals a phase transition. We illustrate this with several examples: $S=1/2$, $S=1$, and $S=3/2$ spin-Peierls chains, which undergo `singlet flop' transitions between different patterns of dimerisation; the bilinear-biquadratic chain, which clarifies the connection to the strict symmetry-protected topological phases classification; and the staggered $J_1$--$J_2$ chain, which has both nearest-neighbour and third-neighbour patterns of singlets depending on the signs of the interactions.

</details>

<a id="paper-2607.21907"></a>
### [Entanglement purification for arbitrary multipartite high-dimensional Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger state](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21907v1)

**Authors:** Yu-Qing Kong, Jia-Ye Liu, Hui-Hui Kong, Hai-Rui Wei

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced entanglement purification protocols specifically tailored for multipartite Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states encoded in high-dimensional qudits. By employing QND measurements and iterative error correction steps, the authors demonstrate methods to counteract various noise channels, theoretically boosting state fidelity towards perfect purity. This research is vital for realizing scalable quantum communication networks using high-dimensional encoding.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly advances the theoretical toolkit for quantum communication by providing robust methods to maintain high entanglement quality in complex, high-dimensional systems, which is crucial for scaling up photonic quantum networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of mitigating environmental noise and channel losses when utilizing high-dimensional quantum states for quantum information processing.

**Main result.** It proposes entanglement purification protocols that can asymptotically improve the fidelity of arbitrary multipartite qudit GHZ states to unity, correcting both qudit-flip and phase-flip errors.

**Method.** The methods involve iterative application of Entanglement Purification Protocols (EPPs) utilizing Quantum Non-Demolition (QND) measurements and Fourier transformations on entangled mixed states.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the high-dimensional qudit ($d$-level) $n$-partite Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state. The protocols are designed to function using nonlinear interactions, such as those modeled by a cross-Kerr Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Fidelity of the output state ($ar{F}_0$), which can be asymptotically improved to unity; detection of qudit-flip and phase-flip errors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $d$ (where $d>2$) and the number of entangled parties $n$. The fidelity threshold $ar{F}_0 \in (1/d, 1)$ is a key constraint derived for phase-flip error correction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocols rely on the ability to perform specific QND measurements and subsequent classical feed-forward operations based on measurement outcomes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the principle of the QND detector in 3D systems, the schematic steps for correcting qudit-flip errors, and the protocol for rectifying phase-flip errors using Fourier transforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the general framework for EPPs, first focusing on specific cases like tripartite qutrit GHZ states (addressing qudit-flip errors), then generalizing to arbitrary $d$-dimensional $n$-partite states under both qudit-flip and phase-flip error models, concluding with an analysis of fidelity thresholds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-dimensional qudit (i.e., $d$-level or $d$-state) systems outperform two-dimensional qubit (i.e., 2-level or 2-state) systems in some quantum information processing tasks. We exploit entanglement purification protocols (EPPs) for extracting a subset of high quality arbitrary $d$-dimensional $n$-partite Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states from a large set of less entangled GHZ states. In our protocols, qudit-flip and phase-flip errors can be corrected, and the fidelity of the output state can be asymptotically improved to unity by iterating the EPP process. Moreover, the schemes are immune to the number of polluted photons, the fidelity thresholds of the proposed EPPs are developed, and the spatial-based single-qudit operations can be well manipulated with a range of balanced beam splitters, and phase shifters. These features make the proposed schemes offer an alternative method for high-dimensional multipartite entanglement purification.

</details>

### [Exact Neural-Network Representations of the Motzkin States](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22522v1)

**Authors:** Runde Zha, Yuntian Gu, Chaohui Fan, Jia-lin Chen, Hai-Jun Liao, Tao Xiang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22522v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper develops analytic, fixed-weight neural network representations for Motzkin spin chains, which are known to exhibit entanglement scaling beyond the area law. By adapting recurrent, convolutional, and transformer architectures, the authors prove that NNs can faithfully encode these complex combinatorial constraints. This establishes a general design principle for using neural networks as powerful tools to model strongly entangled quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, constructive framework showing that advanced NNs can encode quantum correlations beyond simple area-law scaling, offering new benchmarks for simulating strongly correlated systems where standard tensor networks fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is constructing exact, training-free neural network representations for Motzkin states whose entanglement properties exceed those captured by standard Matrix Product States.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated that multiple neural architectures (RNN, FNN, CNN, Transformer) can analytically encode the complex support of these highly entangled quantum states.

**Method.** They systematically designed fixed-weight NQS layers incorporating causal prefix-sum modules and specialized gates to enforce combinatorial path constraints (height matching and color matching).

**Model / system.** The system studied is the Motzkin spin chain, which includes colorless generalizations defined by height constraints and colorful versions requiring an additional last-in-first-out color-matching rule.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy scaling ($\log N$ for colorless; $\sqrt{N}$ for colorful), and the ability to reproduce the exact support of the ground state wavefunction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $N$, and the specific architectural components (e.g., causal prefix-sum module, color stack).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The representations are fixed-weight constructions based on the assumption that the state's support is governed by a finite causal algorithm.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Motzkin path constraints, including height violations and legal/illegal color matching paths for small system sizes ($N=4$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical properties and entanglement scaling of Motzkin states based on their Hamiltonian. It then systematically details how four different neural network architectures (RNN, FNN, CNN, Transformer) can mathematically reproduce the exact support required by these path constraints.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motzkin spin chains are paradigmatic frustration-free one-dimensional quantum systems whose ground states feature exactly solvable combinatorial structures and exotic, area-law-violating entanglement scaling. Specifically, colorless Motzkin states exhibit critical logarithmic entanglement divergence \(\log N\) with system size \(N\), while their colorful counterparts host supercritical sublinear \(\sqrt{N}\) entanglement growth. Such unconventional entanglement behaviors place these states well beyond the expressive capability of standard matrix product states, which are fundamentally constrained by the entanglement area law. Here, we systematically construct exact, training-free neural-network representations for both colorless and colorful Motzkin states across four mainstream architectures, including recurrent, feedforward, convolutional, and transformer networks. Our core design leverages a causal prefix-sum module, implementable via recurrent updates, feedforward mappings, or masked attention layers, combined with position-selective rectified linear gates that enforce the Motzkin height constraints. For the colorful states, we further introduce a dedicated causal stack module that explicitly encodes the last-in-first-out color-matching rule. Our results demonstrate that neural architectures can accurately capture highly non-trivial entanglement features inaccessible to conventional tensor networks, providing prototypic examples for benchmarking and a constructive design framework for future neural-network quantum state developments targeting strongly entangled quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_5	ext{F}$ as a tunable cuprate analogue, using first-principles calculations to probe its electronic and magnetic properties. The research demonstrates that the material maintains a robust, two-dimensional Ni-$d_{x^2-y^2}$ Fermi surface even when subjected to chemical pressure or epitaxial strain. Ultimately, it establishes lattice engineering as a powerful tool for controlling correlated electron behavior in nickelates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use lattice engineering (strain/pressure) to continuously control correlated parameters like doping and magnetic interactions in a solid-state system is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phases in strongly correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how structural stability, electronic structure, and magnetism of the double infinite-layer oxyfluoride $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_5	ext{F}$ are influenced by external tuning parameters like chemical pressure and epitaxial strain.

**Main result.** The material is identified as a promising cuprate analogue because its electronic structure remains robustly two-dimensional, while lattice engineering allows for continuous fine-tuning of carrier doping and magnetic frustration.

**Method.** First-principles calculations using DFT (PBE functional) are employed to calculate phonon spectra, band structures, Fermi surfaces, and magnetic ground states under varying strain and chemical substitutions.

**Model / system.** The system is the double infinite-layer nickelate $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_5	ext{F}$, which is modeled as a correlated electron material exhibiting strong electronic correlations in the Ni $d$-orbitals, analogous to high-Tc cuprates.

**Key observables.** Phonon spectrum (stability), Fermi surface character ($	ext{Ni-}d_{x^2-y^2}$ dominance), self-doping level, and magnetic ground state energy/frustration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Epitaxial strain ($\epsilon$), chemical pressure (rare-earth substitution $	ext{La} 	o 	ext{Sm}$), and the charge-transfer energy ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on DFT approximations, treating the system as a solid-state material where electronic correlations are captured via orbital analysis and magnetic modeling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phonon spectrum confirming stability under strain, band structures showing 2D cuprate-like features across substitutions, and energy comparisons detailing competing magnetic ground states.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the structural and electronic baseline of $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_5	ext{F}$. It then systematically explores how chemical pressure (substitution) and epitaxial strain modify the robust 2D electronic structure and tune the magnetic frustration landscape.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nickelate materials exhibit rich electronic properties that can be engineered toward cuprate-like regimes through topotactic and mixed-anion chemistry. Using first-principles calculations, we investigate the newly synthesized double infinite-layer oxyfluoride La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_5$F and its evolution under chemical pressure and epitaxial strain. The calculated phonon spectrum confirms the dynamical stability of the reported double infinite-layer crystal structure. Further, we find a highly two-dimensional cuprate-like Fermi surface dominated by Ni-$d_{x^2-y^2}$ states, with a moderate rare-earth-derived self-doping yielding an effective $\sim d^{1.2}_{x^2-y^2}$ filling. These electronic features remain remarkably robust under both chemical pressure and epitaxial strain. Spin-polarized calculations further reveal an extended manifold of nearly degenerate magnetic configurations with different in-plane and out-of-plane spin arrangements. Compressive strain further enhances this magnetic frustration while leaving the underlying electronic structure largely unchanged. Our results thus identify La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_5$F as a promising cuprate analogue and establish lattice engineering as an effective strategy for fine tuning its electronic and magnetic properties.

</details>

**Summary.** The research investigates how modifying the interface between tantalum nitride ($	ext{TaN}$) thin films and their silicon substrate affects superconducting resonator performance. By introducing a thin Ta buffer layer, the authors significantly boost the internal quality factor ($Q_i$) from $\sim 1.5 	imes 10^5$ to $5.9 	imes 10^5$. This improvement is attributed to suppressing structural disorder and minimizing energy loss mechanisms concentrated at the metal-substrate interface.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses material science limitations in superconducting quantum hardware, providing actionable engineering guidelines for improving coherence times and resonator quality factors in solid-state qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is enhancing the performance of superconducting resonators used in qubit circuits by engineering the interface between the deposited metal films and the substrate.

**Main result.** Introducing a thin Ta buffer layer significantly enhances the internal quality factor ($Q_i$) of the resonator, showing that controlling this substrate-metal interface is crucial for minimizing energy loss mechanisms like Two-Level Systems (TLS).

**Method.** The study uses superconducting microwave resonators patterned on Si substrates and measures their $Q_i$ using VNA measurements. Structural analysis via TEM/EELS confirms the physical origin of performance changes.

**Model / system.** The platform is a superconducting circuit utilizing TaN/Ta thin films deposited on silicon, tested at cryogenic temperatures (100 mK). The loss mechanism is modeled using the Two-Level System (TLS) theory to quantify interface-related energy dissipation.

**Key observables.** Internal quality factor ($Q_i$), critical temperature ($T_c$), structural disorder observed via TEM/EELS, and TLS loss parameters ($Q_{TLS,0}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Film thicknesses (few nm scale), operating temperature ($\sim 100$ mK), and the presence/absence of a Ta buffer layer.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance degradation is dominated by interface-related TLS losses, and that modifying this interface directly mitigates these losses.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the three investigated film stacks ($	ext{TaN}$ only, $	ext{TaN}/	ext{Ta}$, $	ext{Ta}/	ext{TaN}/	ext{Ta}$) and show TEM/EELS evidence contrasting structural disorder at the direct $	ext{TaN-Si}$ interface versus the clean interface achieved with the Ta buffer layer.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing baseline performance ($	ext{TaN}$ only), then comparing it to structures with different interfaces, and finally using advanced characterization (TEM/EELS) to correlate observed structural disorder at the $	ext{TaN-Si}$ interface with the measured reduction in $Q_i$ when a Ta buffer is added.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tantalum has been demonstrated as a promising material for superconducting qubits. However, comparatively little attention has been given to its nitrides. Tantalum nitride exhibits a range of stoichiometries, resulting in a variety of material properties, including both superconducting and non-superconducting phases. Owing to this versatility, tantalum nitrides can serve multiple purposes in superconducting qubits: as seed layers for alpha-Ta growth, as a superconducting base material and as a non-superconducting barrier in the Josephson junction. In this study, we explore the performance of superconducting TaN and Ta thin film combinations on silicon substrates in terms of internal quality factor Qi. We find that standalone TaN films exhibit Qi values of about 1.5x10^5 at 100mK in the single-photon regime. Surprisingly, a resonator made from Ta grown on a few-nanometers-thick TaN seed layer yields largely the same performance. However, adding an additional, few-nanometers-thick Ta buffer layer between the Si substrate and this TaN seed layer enhances Qi significantly up to 5.9x10^5. Supporting transmission electron microscopy measurements reveal nitrogen accumulation and structural disorder at the TaN-Si interface, while this interfacial modification is suppressed when the Ta buffer layer is introduced. The observed improvement in resonator performance is consistent with a reduction of interface-related two-level system losses and strongly supports the hypothesis that controlling the substrate-metal interface is pivotal for the performance of superconducting qubit circuitry.

</details>

False-colored scanning electron microscopy (SEM) images of the silicon edge profile for edges fabricated with (a) aluminum etching only, (b) anisotropic deep etching with CHF3 following the aluminum etch, (c) the rough-sidewall Tropic etch (with 60 mTorr pressure during SF6 etch followed by CHF3; ‘Rtropic’), and (d) the smooth-sidewall Tropic etch (with 5 mTorr pressure during SF6 etch followed by CHF3; ‘Stropic’).</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the fundamental challenge of material losses limiting qubit performance in superconducting resonators. The authors propose a two-pronged solution: engineering aluminum grain size to reduce non-TLS loss and using advanced etching techniques to smooth dielectric interfaces and minimize TLS loss. This combined approach results in a significant, measurable enhancement in resonator quality factor.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations in superconducting quantum circuits by providing actionable, materials-science based fabrication protocols to boost coherence times, which is critical for scaling up quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Material losses, stemming from Two-Level Systems (TLS) and non-TLS sources, fundamentally limit qubit coherence times and resonator quality factors in superconducting circuits.

**Main result.** By combining aluminum microstructural engineering (increasing grain size) with dielectric trimming (smoothing the silicon edge), the authors achieved a two-orders-of-magnitude improvement in the resonator quality factor ($Q$), reaching $2.3 	imes 10^6$.

**Method.** The study employed advanced nanofabrication techniques, including controlled aluminum deposition temperatures and specialized multi-step etching (Tropic etch), followed by cryogenic characterization.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting coplanar waveguide (CPW) resonator fabricated on high-resistivity silicon using aluminum films. The loss mechanism is modeled as the sum of TLS losses (related to surface disorder) and non-TLS losses (related to metal microstructure).

**Key observables.** Resonator quality factor ($Q$), which was measured at cryogenic temperatures; Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) roughness measurements ($	ext{R}_t$); Grain size distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Deposition temperature of aluminum film ($25^\circ	ext{C}$ to $300^\circ	ext{C}$); Etching profiles (e.g., Tropic etch); Quality factor improvement factor ($\sim 40$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The improvements in microstructure and edge profiling are assumed to be generalizable enhancements for other superconducting quantum devices.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the full fabrication process combining both engineering steps; AFM images show grain size evolution with temperature; SEM/AFM compare silicon edge profiles from different etching methods; $Q$ factor plots demonstrate the combined enhancement effect.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically identifies material loss sources (TLS vs. non-TLS), proposes two complementary mitigation strategies—microstructural control via heating and surface smoothing via specialized etching—and presents quantitative evidence showing that combining both yields superior resonator performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Material losses in superconducting circuits fundamentally limit qubit coherence times and resonator quality factors. Most research efforts focus on mitigating losses at circuit interfaces, including metal--substrate, substrate--air, and metal--air interfaces. However, the correlation between TLS and non-TLS losses with the intrinsic properties of the superconducting metal and the dielectric edge smoothness is not well studied. In this work, we link the aluminum film grain size to non-TLS losses and the dielectric trimming profile and roughness to TLS loss; both loss mechanisms are subsequently mitigated. To reduce metal-related losses, we engineer the aluminum microstructure by heating during deposition, increasing grain size and reducing grain boundary density. Beyond mitigating metal losses, we introduce a two-step etching technique, Tropic etching, to suppress dielectric TLS loss by producing an ultra-smooth silicon surface with minimal defects and redeposition. These results lay out the fabrication pathway for aluminum resonators with lower loss, demonstrating two-orders-of-magnitude improvement in quality factor from $6\times10^{4}$ to $2.3\times10^{6}$. Since aluminum is the basis for most high-coherence Josephson junctions and dielectric edges are inherent to all common device geometries, the improvements in aluminum microstructure and edge profiling, presented here, can enhance the performance of superconducting quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports on an experimental realization of tunable nonlinear electromechanics using a coupled nanotube oscillator and double quantum dots. By operating in the ultrastrong coupling regime, they achieve significant mechanical anharmonicity ($\alpha=1.4\%$) and demonstrate purely quadratic readout sensitivity near zero-point motion. This establishes a versatile platform for fundamental studies in nanoscale quantum control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics (cavity QED), nanomechanics, and solid-state physics, providing a highly tunable platform for observing nonlinear dynamics near the quantum ground state, which is central to modern AMO research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is achieving tunable nonlinear electromechanics at the zero-point motion scale for nanomechanical systems.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate a platform exhibiting a mechanical Kerr nonlinearity ($\alpha=1.4\%$) and purely quadratic continuous readout, both controllable via ultrastrong coupling (USC).

**Method.** Theoretical modeling using Hamiltonian approaches combined with experimental measurements of frequency shifts and linewidths.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid setup involving a carbon nanotube mechanical oscillator coupled to an electronic two-level system (ETLS) formed by quantum dots, all integrated within a microwave cavity.

**Key observables.** Mechanical Kerr nonlinearity ($\alpha$), purely quadratic readout signal ($\Delta\omega_C \propto x^2$), and the thermal linewidth ($\langle \delta\omega_M^2 angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrastrong coupling (USC) regime, zero-point motion scale, gate tunability of ETLS parameters ($t_E$, $\epsilon$), and mechanical frequency ($\omega_M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the dispersive ultrastrong-coupling regime where low-lying eigenstates remain predominantly mechanical.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate system setups, quantum dot control mechanisms, measurements confirming quadratic readout scaling, and spectral density plots comparing analytical thermal linewidth estimates with numerical diagonalization results.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform and Hamiltonian to demonstrating strong nonlinearity via Kerr effects, followed by characterizing the purely quadratic continuous readout mechanism, and finally analyzing thermal broadening using advanced spectral density calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinearity at the scale of zero-point motion opens new possibilities for the control and readout of nanomechanical systems, but achieving this remains a formidable challenge. Here we demonstrate that ultrastrong coupling (USC) between a nanotube mechanical oscillator and a double-quantum-dot electronic two-level system enables a mechanical Kerr (Duffing) nonlinearity at the zero-point motion scale. In the dispersive regime, this large coupling yields a mechanical anharmonicity of $α= 1.4\%$ - three orders of magnitude larger than in previous work - while preserving the predominantly mechanical nature of the lowest energy states. We further demonstrate a purely quadratic cavity-based continuous readout of the mechanical motion. This continuous nonlinear optomechanical readout is enforced by a double-quantum dot symmetry, which can be broken by gate tuning to introduce a large linear transduction. These results establish a tunable USC platform that enables strong mechanical anharmonicity and nonlinear continuous readout at the zero-point motion scale.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper outlines an experimental proposal to test Hawking's Chronology Protection Conjecture using an optical time machine built from a fiber loop and parametric amplifier. By analyzing both classical and full quantum field dynamics, the authors show that while time travel is classically possible under high gain, quantum noise generation fundamentally limits or prevents it.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges general relativity concepts (closed timelike curves) directly into the domain of quantum optics and open quantum systems by using parametric amplification as a physical model for vacuum energy buildup.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes an optical analogue experiment to test Hawking's Chronology Protection Conjecture (CPC), which suggests quantum mechanics prevents time travel.

**Main result.** While classical analysis suggests that time travel is possible under conditions of high gain exceeding loss, the quantum mechanical treatment predicts that vacuum fluctuations will generate measurable extra noise, thereby imposing limitations on any achievable time machine.

**Method.** The authors use both classical differential equation solving (Fourier transforms) and full quantum field quantization techniques (mode operators, Wigner functions) to model light propagation through an amplifier feedback loop.

**Model / system.** The system is an optical setup involving a fiber connected to a loop via a parametric amplifier ($\chi^{(2)}$ crystal), designed to mimic the closed null geodesics required for time travel in general relativity.

**Key observables.** Classical fidelity ($F$), extra quantum noise quantified by particle number ($ar{n}$), and the temporal relationship between input and output pulses.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Amplifier gain parameter ($\zeta$), loss parameter ($\gamma$), and the time delay in the loop ($t_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that quantum noise generation (amplification of vacuum fluctuations) is the dominant effect, and it compares regimes where classical causality might be violated versus where quantum constraints must hold.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spacetime diagram for time travel, the optical setup using a fiber loop/parametric amplifier, and temporal comparisons showing output pulses preceding input arrivals.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical link between GR time travel and amplified vacuum fluctuations (Hawking's argument), developing classical solutions for light propagation in the ring, and finally quantizing the system to calculate the unavoidable quantum noise associated with the process.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical general relativity allows time travel, but Hawking [Phys. Rev. D 46, 603 (1992)] conjectured that quantum mechanics ultimately prevents it. Here we propose a feasible experiment to test Hawking's Chronology Protection Conjecture in optics. In this scheme, time travel is inherently related to lasing, and the amplified vacuum fluctuations above laser threshold establish the quantum limits of time travel

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces Evolution-Path Symmetry (EPS) as a unifying theoretical concept to explain interaction effects in quantum transport. By showing that interactions selectively break the path symmetries responsible for destructive interference in flux lattices, the authors successfully account for phenomena like AB caging destruction and chiral current generation. This framework provides a powerful tool for analyzing correlated many-body dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of a generalized 'Evolution-Path Symmetry' provides a powerful, non-standard diagnostic tool for understanding complex many-body dynamics beyond simple eigenstate analysis, relevant for strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to find a unifying physical picture for interaction-driven quantum transport, specifically addressing how interactions destroy Aharonov-Bohm (AB) caging and induce chiral currents in flux lattices.

**Main result.** The concept of Evolution-Path Symmetry (EPS) successfully explains that interactions break the path phase balance responsible for destructive interference, thereby enabling delocalization and chiral current flow.

**Method.** The study employs a theoretical framework based on analyzing quantum dynamics via interfering trajectories in Fock space, utilizing the concept of EPS to diagnose symmetry breaking.

**Model / system.** The models involve bosonic lattice systems (flux lattices like $\pi$-flux rhombic lattices and flux ladders) governed by tight-binding Hamiltonians with interaction terms ($H = H_0 + H_{	ext{int}}$).

**Key observables.** AB caging effect, interaction-induced delocalization rate, non-vanishing chiral current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), magnetic flux ($\pi$-flux), and the path structure connecting neighboring doublon states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on analyzing the shortest evolution paths connecting neighboring doublon states; it also compares bosonic vs. fermionic statistics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate particle density evolution showing delocalization when interactions are present, and detail the phase factors associated with various short-time evolution paths connecting specific many-body states.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of AB caging in non-interacting systems. It then develops the EPS framework, demonstrating how interaction terms selectively modify path phases (those involving double occupancy) to break symmetry and induce transport. Finally, it generalizes this mechanism to explain chiral currents and compares the robustness of EPS across different quantum statistics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The destruction of Aharonov-Bohm (AB) caging by interaction and the emergence of interaction-induced chiral currents in flux lattices are two paradigmatic examples of interaction-driven quantum transport. While various mechanisms, such as bound-state formation and chiral spectral imbalance, have been proposed, a unifying physical picture remains elusive. Here, we employ the concept of \textit{evolution-path symmetry} (EPS) and its interaction-induced breaking as a framework to understand interaction-induced delocalization in flux lattices. EPS is defined as the invariance of a path's contribution under combined geometric and phase transformations. We demonstrate that in a $π$-flux rhombic lattice, interactions break the EPS present in the non-interacting limit by modifying the phase accumulation of many-body paths, thereby lifting the destructive interference responsible for AB caging. Furthermore, we apply this framework to explain interaction-induced chiral transport in flux ladders, where interactions break the phase relationship between symmetric paths, leading to a non-vanishing chiral current. Our work establishes EPS as a powerful tool for understanding transport phenomena beyond conventional eigenstate analysis.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using controlled electron shuttling in silicon spin qubits to map and characterize charge defects (TLFs). By measuring the resulting loss of spin coherence (infidelity), researchers can localize these noise sources and determine their switching dynamics. This offers a practical, hardware-agnostic method for improving solid-state quantum computing platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, scalable measurement protocol for characterizing noise sources (TLFs) that fundamentally limit solid-state quantum memories, directly impacting qubit coherence time predictions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Charge noise, caused by Two-Level Fluctuators (TLFs), limits the performance of silicon spin qubits for quantum computation.

**Main result.** Electron shuttling acts as a scalable scanning probe to localize charge defects and characterize their switching rates without requiring additional hardware.

**Method.** The authors analyze the dephasing factor by modeling the time evolution of the spin's coherence during controlled back-and-forth electron shuttling over a defect site.

**Model / system.** The system involves single spin qubits in silicon interacting with charge noise modeled by TLFs, whose effect is incorporated into the qubit Hamiltonian via g-factor modulation. The dynamics are solved using coupled differential equations for probability amplitudes.

**Key observables.** Dephasing factor ($W(T)$) and its derived quantity, the infidelity ($1 - |W(T)|$), which quantifies decoherence due to defects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fluctuation regime ($\gamma_T \ll 1$ vs. $\gamma_T \gg 1$) and the shuttling distance $L$ are key parameters used to extract defect properties.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations, such as expanding exponentials or assuming specific limits for the fluctuation rate relative to the measurement time.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the infidelity versus shuttling distance ($L$) under different fluctuation regimes and show how this dependence allows defect localization. Another figure compares results with varying interface roughness ($	ext{RMS}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by deriving the evolution of the coherence factor based on the time-dependent g-factor modulation from a TLF, solving the resulting differential equations in different fluctuation limits, and demonstrating defect localization via the measured infidelity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Silicon spin qubits are a leading platform for scalable quantum computing, but their performance is limited by charge noise, widely attributed to two-level fluctuators (TLFs). The location of individual TLFs is generally unknown, and existing methods to localise them does not scale favourably with device size. Here, we show that electron shuttling turns a single mobile spin into a scanning probe of individual defects. We show that by shuttling a spin over a range of distances and tracking its coherence loss, one can localise defects along the channel and constrain their switching rate and fluctuation amplitude. Because one shuttled electron sweeps an extended region, the approach scales more favourably than previous methods. Our protocol requires no additional hardware and provides a practical route to mapping the charge-defect landscape of large silicon devices, enabling defect-aware calibration and avoidance in shuttling-based architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details the construction of rotor-based mechanical hysterons capable of realizing highly controllable and complex interactions. By manipulating geometric parameters like spring mounting positions and rotor angles, the authors achieve tunable split, mixed-sign, and three-body coupling terms in a physical system. This work expands the design space for mechanically computing matter based on interacting disordered elements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a tangible, mechanical platform for studying concepts typically confined to abstract spin models or theoretical Hamiltonians, offering experimental insight into non-equilibrium dynamics and complex many-body interactions relevant to disordered systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically demonstrate control over complex interaction types—split, mixed, and three-body interactions—in a mechanical system designed to model hysteretic spins.

**Main result.** The authors successfully build rotor-based mechanical hysterons that exhibit tunable non-reciprocal, state-dependent pairwise couplings and controllable three-body effects by manipulating geometric parameters.

**Method.** The study combines analytical derivations based on torque balance equations with experimental realization using coupled rotors to map abstract interaction terms onto physical hardware.

**Model / system.** The system consists of rotor hysterons coupled by mechanical springs, modeling interacting spins relevant for studying athermal disordered matter and developing intelligent matter.

**Key observables.** Switching thresholds (gamma), pairwise interaction strengths ($J_{\pm}^{ij}$), and three-body coupling terms ($K^{\pm}_{ijk}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spring stiffnesses ($k$), rotor angles ($	heta$), mounting positions ($\ell$), and the ratio of spring rest lengths to separation distances ($x_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations derived from small coupling spring rest lengths, though the work explicitly explores regimes where these assumptions break down.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the splitting of interaction strengths by varying rotor angles or mounting positions, demonstrate pseudoavalanches requiring mixed interactions, and show the emergence of three-body terms when nonlinear torque regimes are accessed.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general hysteretic model and its required interactions to demonstrating symmetry breaking (splitting), then showing how mixed signs can be engineered, culminating in the realization of tunable three-body effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interacting hysteretic spins are an appealing model for cyclically-driven athermal disordered matter. Because they provide a basis for storing and processing information from their environment, such models are also being pursued as a framework for intelligent matter. Recent proof-of-concept designs have begun to demonstrate the strong, controlled, pairwise interactions that are necessary for this endeavor. But, it is not yet clear what are the limits---practically or fundamentally---on such interactions. Here we build rotor-based mechanical hysterons that extend the generality of their interactions in three ways: (i) splitting the interaction strength based on the hysteron state, (ii) building a non-reciprocal interaction of mixed sign, and (iii) incorporating tunable three-body effects. We access these effects within a simple, replicable design platform, and we rationalize our results. Our work expands the space of behaviors for designed structures that compute on mechanical inputs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes that reliable computation in decentralized, asynchronous physical systems requires an inertial mechanism. By modeling the system using coupled position and momentum variables, the authors demonstrate how this inertia guides the state evolution to correctly retrieve temporal sequences. The findings are supported by theoretical phase diagram analysis and experimental implementation on neuromorphic hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent order from local, asynchronous interactions, mediated by an 'inertial' memory term, provides a rich analogy for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems or complex many-body physics where global control is absent.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is understanding how natural processes can perform reliable, ordered computation when components update asynchronously without a global clock.

**Main result.** Inertia, modeled by coupling position and momentum variables, provides the necessary mechanism to guide asynchronous state evolution toward correct computational sequences.

**Method.** The authors employ loop dynamical mean-field theory to analyze the phase diagram of the system, supplemented by experimental validation on neuromorphic chips.

**Model / system.** The model is an inertial asynchronous computation network where physical hardware is split into two asymmetrically coupled parts (position $\mathbf{x}$ and momentum $\mathbf{p}$). The dynamics are governed by coupled, non-variational update rules.

**Key observables.** Order parameters ($m_\mu(t)$ for static retrieval; $m_d$ for sequence retrieval), phase diagram boundaries (Blackout, Static Retrieval, Sequence Retrieval).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of patterns to neurons ($P/N$), the inertial coupling strength ($\lambda$), and the memory load.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that coupling position and momentum in this specific manner provides a general strategy for reliable, decentralized computation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the concept of inertia enabling successful asynchronous computation compared to failure modes. Later panels show concrete results contrasting synchronous success, asynchronous failure, and proposed model success in associative memory retrieval.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the mystery of natural asynchronous computation, proposes the inertial $\mathbf{x}/\mathbf{p}$ model, develops the theoretical framework using loop DMFT to map the phase diagram, and concludes with experimental demonstrations on neuromorphic hardware and software applications like movie denoising.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Computation is the controlled evolution of a state. Asynchronous evolutions, where all parts of the state change in their own time without stopping each other, put this control in jeopardy. It is in fact a mystery how natural processes perform asynchronous computations using many units with no global orchestration. Here we demonstrate how collective computational abilities can emerge in asynchronous many-body systems. The key insight is to split the physical "hardware" underlying the computation into two asymmetrically coupled parts, analogous to position and momentum in a harmonic oscillator. The resulting inertia nudges the evolution of the state so that the asynchronous computation proceeds in the right order. By treating our inertial asynchronous computer as a nonequilibrium material, we map out its phase diagram numerically and analytically using a framework we dub loop dynamical mean-field theory. We experimentally demonstrate our approach using analog spiking neuromorphic chips designed to mimic actual neurons in the brain. In addition, we construct software that can run on asynchronous hardware: we denoise movies whose clean versions were never seen during training, an instantiation of the generalization transition underlying modern machine learning. Our results point to a general strategy for reliable, decentralized computation in energy-constrained settings from dynamics self-assembly to cell differentiation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the rate of thermalization for the mean-field Heisenberg ferromagnet using the Davies generator. It establishes that the spectral gap scales differently depending on whether the system is above or below its critical temperature ($eta=2$). The results show that slow dynamics at low temperatures are linked to continuous symmetry breaking.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous quantitative estimates for thermalization rates in a highly symmetric quantum spin model, directly linking dynamical behavior (spectral gap) to underlying symmetries and phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is characterizing the spectral gap of the Davies generator associated with the mean-field Heisenberg model at noncritical temperatures.

**Main result.** The spectral gap exhibits different system size scaling depending on temperature: $\Theta(1)$ for high temperatures ($eta<2$) and $\Theta(n-1)$ for low temperatures ($eta>2$).

**Method.** The analysis employs comparison arguments using auxiliary generators, decomposing observables into spherical tensor operators, and utilizing Dirichlet form calculations based on the Davies generator.

**Model / system.** The system is the mean-field Heisenberg ferromagnet (spin-1/2 interactions on a complete graph). The dynamics are modeled by the open-system evolution governed by the Davies generator, which describes thermalization towards the Gibbs state $ho \propto e^{-eta H}$.

**Key observables.** Spectral gap of the Davies generator ($	ext{gap}(L_{	ext{loc}})$) and the total magnetization order parameter ($S_{	ext{tot}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), system size ($n$). The critical point is $eta=2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on symmetry properties (SU(2) and S_n) and the decomposition of observables into spherical tensor operators.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but calculations involve matrix elements derived from Pauli twirls ($	ext{d}(\ell)_{s,s'}$) and bounds on ratios involving moments of spin quantum numbers ($s$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops results by first establishing properties of the generator using Wigner 3j/6j symbols, then deriving lower bounds on the spectral gap via Dirichlet form calculations that exploit symmetry decomposition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The mean-field Heisenberg ferromagnet is a quantum spin model on the complete graph with isotropic spin-1/2 interactions. This non-commuting Hamiltonian is permutation and $\mathsf{SU}(2)$ invariant, and its Gibbs states undergo an $\mathsf{SU}(2)$ symmetry breaking phase transition at inverse temperature $β=2$. We consider the associated Davies generator, a canonical model of open-system thermalization, and prove tight asymptotic estimates for its spectral gap at all noncritical temperatures. For fixed $β<2$, the gap as a function of number of qubits $n$ is $Θ(1)$, while for fixed $β>2$ the gap is $Θ(n^{-1})$. The matching upper bound of the spectral gap is witnessed by the total magnetization order parameter, suggesting that the low-temperature ($β>2$) slowdown is associated with broken continuous symmetry. Two key ingredients in our approach are a comparison argument, which introduces auxiliary generators to bound dissipation on nontrivial representations of the symmetry groups $\mathsf{SU}(2)$ and $\mathsf{S}_n$, and a decomposition of the space of observables into spherical tensor operators to reveal a form of monotonicity.

</details>
