**Summary.** This review systematically examines turbulence in Bose-Einstein condensates by analyzing the Gross-Pitaskii Equation. It introduces sophisticated diagnostics to differentiate between wave-driven cascades and vortex-dominated dynamics. The paper is crucial for understanding how quantized topological excitations govern energy dissipation in quantum fluids.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles non-equilibrium dynamics and energy transport in ultracold atomic systems, requiring advanced knowledge of both superfluid hydrodynamics and quantum field theory techniques.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reviews the complex physics of turbulence in ultracold quantum gases, aiming to bridge classical fluid concepts like energy cascades with microscopic wave-function dynamics.

**Main result.** It establishes that simple power laws are insufficient evidence for a cascade and requires detailed diagnostics (like spectral fluxes and vortex correlations) to distinguish between different turbulent regimes.

**Method.** The analysis relies on deriving hydrodynamic equations from the Gross-Pitaevskii Equation (GPE), treating the system in terms of particle current, superfluid velocity, and considering both wave and topological excitation sectors.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a scalar contact-interaction Bose-Einstein condensate described by the GPE. The physics involves quantized circulation, density fluctuations, and the interplay between quantum pressure and interaction energy.

**Key observables.** Incompressible/compressible kinetic-energy spectra, wave-occupation spectra, spectral fluxes, vortex-resolved correlations, and velocity statistics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Healing length ($\xi$), quantized circulation ($\hbar/m$), critical velocity ($c$), and the system's dimensionality (2D vs 3D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between weak-wave turbulence (classical field approximation) and strong vortex turbulence, noting that the quantum potential term is crucial near cores.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates vortex properties for GPE, showing density profiles and velocity fields associated with quantized vortices.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the BEC model via the GPE, deriving the superfluid Euler equation using the particle current, detailing various diagnostic tools to characterize different turbulent regimes (wave vs. vortex), and concluding by placing the system in a broader context of quantum fluids.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We review turbulence in ultracold quantum gases, using the scalar contact-interaction Bose-Einstein condensate as the reference system for quantized circulation, compressibility, vortices, sound, and cascades. We focus on the quantitative diagnostics that connect helium and classical phenomenology to microscopic wave-function dynamics: incompressible and compressible kinetic-energy spectra, wave-occupation spectra, spectral fluxes, vortex-resolved correlations, and velocity statistics. These diagnostics distinguish equilibrium vortex organization, decaying turbulent relaxation, forced cascade dynamics, and weak-wave turbulence, and show why power laws alone are insufficient evidence for a cascade. We survey experiments on two-dimensional Onsager clustering, three-dimensional vortex-line turbulence, box-trap wave cascades, engineered dissipation, and turbulent equations of state. We close by briefly placing the contact-interaction scalar superfluid system in a broader landscape of nonlocal, multicomponent, fermionic, and driven-dissipative quantum fluids, where turbulence concepts can be tested for universality.

</details>

**Summary.** This work connects advanced concepts from algebraic topology (like Stiefel-Whitney classes) to the physics of Quantum Cellular Automata. It shows that gravitational topological responses are crucial for distinguishing true lattice entanglement from simpler circuit preparations. The findings unify several theoretical frameworks, providing a deeper understanding of topological order in quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous lattice framework for understanding topological invariants that are often glossed over in continuum field theory treatments of quantum matter, directly impacting how we model exotic correlated states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses a fundamental mismatch between continuum field theory descriptions of topological terms (which often ignore gravitational effects) and their essential, non-trivial manifestation on a discrete lattice.

**Main result.** It establishes that Quantum Cellular Automata (QCAs) associated with gravitational topological responses arise in four distinct physical settings, unifying the study across dualities, higher dimensions, and lattice models.

**Method.** The authors derive new projective SL(2, Z_N) relations using gravitational topological responses constructed from Stiefel-Whitney classes and provide protocols for state preparation using finite-depth circuits.

**Model / system.** The core model involves Quantum Cellular Automata (QCAs) derived from higher-dimensional analogs of Kramers-Wannier duality, analyzed via lattice realizations of projective SL(2, Z_N) relations and higher-form gauging.

**Key observables.** Gravitational topological responses, Ground State Degeneracy (GSD), and the comparison between states prepared by Finite-Depth Quantum Circuits (FDQCs) versus QCA entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lattice dimension ($3+1$d mentioned), the symmetry group ($	ext{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z}_N)$), and the topological invariants (Stiefel-Whitney classes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Field theory often treats gravitational topological terms as inessential counterterms, an assumption contradicted by lattice physics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mismatch between stabilizer groups for different SPT phases on a cubic lattice. Figure 8 shows two sets of stabilizers derived from ST and (ST)^3 operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes how gravitational topological responses manifest in four contexts: projective SL(2, Z_N) relations, dualities generalizations, higher-form gauging, and time-reversal protected phases. It builds mathematical machinery around Gauss sums to classify these responses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent work has constructed higher-dimensional analogs of non-invertible symmetries similar to 1+1d Kramers-Wannier duality. Although their continuum descriptions often treat purely gravitational topological terms as inessential counterterms, these terms can have an essential lattice manifestation: they distinguish states prepared by finite-depth quantum circuits (FDQCs) from those entangled by nontrivial quantum cellular automata (QCAs). Motivated by this mismatch, we show that QCAs associated with gravitational topological responses arise in several related settings: (1) lattice realizations of projective $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z}_N)$ relations generated by topological operations on symmetries; (2) squares of dualities that generalize the relation between fermionization and Kramers-Wannier duality; (3) lattice implementations of QCAs through higher-form gauging; and (4) invertible phases protected by generalized time-reversal symmetries. We derive new projective $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z}_N)$ relations whose projective phases are gravitational topological responses constructed from Stiefel-Whitney classes. We furthermore give a general protocol for preparing the associated QCA-entangled states using finite-depth unitary circuits, measurements, and error correction. These results unify the study of gravitational topological responses in field theories, higher dimensional dualities, and quantum cellular automata.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a general theoretical framework to calculate the extreme first-passage time for multiple diffusing particles that interact with each other. It proves that while statistical effects limit this time to $1/\ln N$, specific coherent interactions can accelerate the process, achieving rates as fast as $1/N$ or $1/(N \ln N)$. The work deepens understanding by separating acceleration sources into those from redundancy versus those from collective transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical diffusion, the rigorous mathematical framework for bounding collective transport times in interacting systems is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems, such as reaction rates or quench dynamics where rare events dominate.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses determining the extreme first-passage time (EFPT) for multiple interacting particles to reach a target, especially when this earliest arrival dictates the outcome of a physical process.

**Main result.** The authors establish that while independent searchers yield an EFPT scaling of $1/\ln N$, interactions can accelerate this rate up to deterministic pairwise interaction ($O(1/N)$) or stochastic forcing ($O(1/(N \ln N))$).

**Method.** The work employs rigorous probabilistic bounding techniques, including establishing no-go theorems and deriving unified lower bounds by analyzing the interplay between coherent drift and enhanced diffusion.

**Model / system.** The model consists of $N$ overdamped searchers diffusing in a $d$-dimensional space towards an absorbing target set $\Gamma$, governed by Langevin dynamics with various interaction potentials (e.g., repulsive, deterministic).

**Key observables.** Extreme first-passage time ($T_N$), initial minimum distance ($\ell_0$), and the scaling exponents related to drift ($B_N$) and effective diffusion ($	ext{Deff}_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of particles $N$, the dimensionality $d$, and the nature/strength of the pairwise interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key results rely on assumptions about bounded interactions, specific initial-state control (e.g., local coordination $\mathrm{M}_N$), or analyzing short-time logarithmic windows.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling differences between independent searchers and those subjected to various interaction mechanisms, showing how different resource types (drift vs. diffusion) determine the lower bound on $T_N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the benchmark for non-interacting particles ($1/\ln N$) via a no-go theorem, then systematically deriving sharp upper and lower bounds by identifying specific mechanisms—deterministic drift or stochastic forcing—that lead to faster acceleration regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extreme first-passage events are broadly relevant to biological, chemical, and physical processes in which the first successful arrival determines the outcome. Existing theories are confined to noninteracting searchers. Interacting extreme-statistics problems are notoriously difficult because correlations destroy probability factorization. We establish a general framework for interacting extreme search. A no-go theorem shows that broad classes of bounded interactions cannot beat the $1/\ln N$ extreme timescale of $N$ independent Brownian searchers, and complementary upper bounds prove that this scale is exact for broad classes of repulsive interactions. We then identify two sharp mechanisms beyond the logarithmic class and derive a unified interaction-driven acceleration limit. In particular, deterministic pairwise interaction can at most reduce the extreme search time to order $1/N$, while stochastic pairwise forcing attains $1/(N\ln N)$. Our results separate acceleration due to statistical redundancy from that generated by coherent many-body transport or amplified fluctuations, deepening our understanding of interacting stochastic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Spectral Subspace Purification (SSP-QST), a novel technique to clean up noisy density matrix estimates from photonic quantum state tomography. By adaptively thresholding eigenvalues based on a derived noise floor, SSP-QST avoids discarding valid signal information while removing measurement artifacts. This results in more reliable and efficient quantum sensing measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a computationally lightweight, non-iterative post-processing tool for quantum state estimation in photonic systems, directly addressing the practical limitations of noise handling in quantum sensing protocols relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard Quantum State Tomography (QST) estimates suffer from eigenvalue contamination due to noise, which inflates entropy and reduces the usable Quantum Fisher Information. Furthermore, fixed rank-1 purification discards valid signal modes if the true state has a higher effective rank.

**Main result.** The proposed SSP-QST method achieves the highest fidelity among non-iterative baselines by adaptively purifying the density matrix estimate while preserving valid signal modes for $r>1$ probes, significantly improving shot efficiency.

**Method.** SSP-QST is a rank-adaptive post-processing layer applied to the least-squares QST estimate ($ho_{LS}$). It eigendecomposes $ho_{LS}$, estimates a noise floor using Weyl-motivated theory, removes modes below this threshold, and renormalizes the remaining subspace.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on photonic quantum sensing utilizing low-rank entangled probes such as GHZ, Bell, and NOON states. The analysis is performed on the density matrix reconstruction ($\hat{ho}$) derived from Pauli measurements.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), Reconstruction Fidelity, Eigenvalue Spectrum of $ho_{LS}$, and the purified state $ho_{SSP}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Probe rank ($r$), finite-shot noise level, and the calculated spectral noise floor ($\epsilon_{th}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method requires no prior knowledge of the true rank, avoids iterative optimization, and relies only on a single eigendecomposition.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares conventional LS-QST (full-rank, noisy $\hat{ho}$) against SSP-QST's purified estimate ($ho_{SSP}$), showing improved QFI. Other figures validate performance across varying ranks and shot budgets.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing QST methods (LS-QST, fixed rank purification). It then introduces SSP-QST as a novel spectral thresholding technique derived from noise theory. Finally, it presents extensive simulations demonstrating superior fidelity and efficiency compared to all tested baselines.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic quantum sensing often uses low-rank entangled probes such as Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ), Bell, and NOON states. Although these probes are ideally rank-1, practical quantum state tomography (QST) can produce density-matrix estimates with many small finite-shot and noise-induced eigenmodes. This eigenvalue contamination can increase the estimated entropy of the reconstruction and reduce the quantum Fisher information (QFI) available for downstream sensing, while fixed rank-1 purification can discard valid signal modes when real probes acquire additional signal modes. We introduce Spectral Subspace Purification for Quantum State Tomography (SSP-QST), a rank-adaptive post-processing layer for least-squares quantum state tomography (LS-QST). SSP-QST eigendecomposes the least-squares estimate, computes a Weyl-motivated noise floor from the measured spectrum and shot count, removes eigenmodes below this floor, and renormalises the retained subspace. It requires no rank prior, no iterative optimisation, and only one eigendecomposition. In Qiskit Aer simulations, SSP-QST achieves the highest fidelity among the tested non-iterative baselines across the evaluated probe ranks, with a maximum fidelity gain of $+0.584$. It also improves shot efficiency by at least $8\times$ within the tested range. These results show that SSP-QST can make photonic QST more reliable under finite-shot noise while providing a lightweight reconstruction primitive for feedback-oriented quantum sensing pipelines.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper mathematically investigates $k$-photon light-matter interaction Hamiltonians, determining when they are self-adjoint operators. It proves that the Hamiltonian's mathematical properties change drastically depending on whether $k$ is less than or greater than three. The findings provide essential theoretical constraints for modeling nonlinear quantum optical processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to analyze quantum optical Hamiltonians that exhibit strong nonlinearity, which is crucial for understanding high-order light-matter coupling in cavity QED settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the self-adjoint extensions of Hamiltonians that describe nonlinear light-matter interactions involving the exchange of $k$ photons at a time.

**Main result.** The Hamiltonian is guaranteed to be self-adjoint only for $k \le 2$ under specific conditions; for $k \ge 3$, multiple self-adjoint extensions exist, all possessing purely discrete spectra if the matter system is finite-dimensional.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced spectral theory techniques, including block Jacobi decomposition and utilizing properties derived from the polar decomposition of the coupling operator $\Sigma$.

**Model / system.** The model describes a single bosonic mode coupled to an arbitrary quantum matter system ($\mathcal{H}$) via a $k$-photon interaction term. Examples include the $k$-photon Rabi and Dicke models.

**Key observables.** Self-adjointness of the Hamiltonian, deficiency indices, and the spectrum (which is shown to be purely discrete for finite $\mathcal{H}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $k$ (the number of exchanged photons), the coupling operator $\Sigma$, and the energy $\omega$ of a single excitation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial rigorous analysis often assumes that the coupling operator $\Sigma$ is normal ($\Sigma^* \Sigma = \Sigma \Sigma^*$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general Hamiltonian form, analyzes its self-adjointness based on $k$, derives deficiency indices for non-self-adjoint cases ($k\ge3$), and illustrates results using specific models like the Rabi and Dicke systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multiphoton light-matter interactions, in which a bosonic mode exchanges $k$ excitations at a time with a quantum system, are a source of genuine nonlinearity in quantum optics and are increasingly accessible experimentally. Here we study the class of operators $H = H_{\rm mat}\otimes I + I\otimesωa^\ast a + Σ\otimes(a^\ast)^k + Σ^\ast\otimes a^k$ on $\mathcal{H}\otimes L^2(\mathbb{R})$, coupling a single bosonic mode to an arbitrary matter system through a bounded operator $Σ$. When $Σ$ is normal and nonzero, we prove that $H$ is self-adjoint if and only if $k\leq2$; for $k\geq3$ we compute the deficiency indices, parametrise all self-adjoint extensions, and show that every extension has purely discrete spectrum whenever the matter system is finite-dimensional. Our analysis rests on a block Jacobi decomposition paired with a suitable unitary transformation depending on the polar decomposition of $Σ$. The normality of $Σ$ is optimal: a $k$-photon Jaynes-Cummings model, with non-normal coupling, remains self-adjoint for every $k$. We illustrate our results on the $k$-photon Rabi and Dicke models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the dark polaron theory to address limitations in cooling trapped ions using high-intensity lasers. By transforming the system into a polaron basis, the authors derive an effective Hamiltonian that remains simple and linear even when conventional approximations break down. This provides a robust theoretical framework for understanding laser cooling dynamics under strong driving fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool—the polaron approach—to analyze open quantum systems (trapped ions) operating under extreme conditions (high laser intensities), extending the applicability of standard quantum optics models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To overcome limitations of conventional laser control schemes for trapped ion cooling, which are restricted to weak laser intensities and small Lamb-Dicke parameters.

**Main result.** The dark polaron theory successfully derives a description valid in the high-intensity limit, showing that the coupling between electronic and motional degrees of freedom remains linear and intensity-independent.

**Method.** The authors employ a polaron transformation to map the system Hamiltonian into a simplified basis, allowing for the derivation of rate equations applicable beyond standard weak-coupling approximations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is trapped ions undergoing laser cooling via Electromagnetically Induced Transparency (EIT) in a Lambda ($\Lambda$) configuration. The model involves coupling electronic states to vibrational modes using two Raman lasers.

**Key observables.** Cooling rates, phonon population $\langle n angle$, and the dynamics of dark/bright polarons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Laser intensities (high intensity limit), Lamb-Dicke parameter ($\eta$), Rabi frequencies ($\Omega$), and spontaneous decay rates ($\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes counter-propagating beams ($\eta_1 = -\eta_2 = \eta$) and utilizes the polaron basis to handle high-order coupling terms that vanish identically in this three-level system.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares dynamics simulated using the original Hamiltonian versus the simplified polaron picture across various cooling regimes, showing agreement. Other figures illustrate the dark/bright polaron states and rate dependencies on Rabi frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of conventional treatments (Lamb-Dicke expansion). It then introduces the dark polaron basis via a transformation to simplify the Hamiltonian. Finally, it derives rate equations in this new picture, demonstrating its validity across all laser intensity regimes compared to full master equation simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional laser control schemes for cooling and gate operation of trapped ions are limited to the regime of weak laser intensities and small Lamb-Dicke parameters. To overcome this limitation, we present the concept of dark polarons: spatially extended states of pseudospin polarization that are fully decoupled from a lambda laser configuration. In this picture, all high-order Lamb-Dicke terms collapse into a single linear coupling independent of laser intensity. We apply it to definitively elucidate the reasons behind cooling rate limitations observed in recent experimental implementations of electromagnetically induced transparency with high-intensity lasers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on high-quality single crystals of MnTe to investigate its intrinsic altermagnetic properties. By using advanced measurements like EPR, NMR, and transport studies, the authors characterize complex domain kinetics near the magnetic transition and observe a low-temperature Anderson insulating state. The findings establish these materials as crucial platforms for studying strongly correlated phenomena in clean crystalline environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on condensed matter magnetism, the study's deep dive into domain dynamics, localization physics (Anderson insulator), and the interplay between magnetic order and charge carriers provides rich context for understanding correlated electron behavior in novel quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To study the intrinsic magnetic, electronic, and domain kinetics properties of MnTe, an archetypal altermagnet, by synthesizing ultra-high quality single crystals free from chemical imperfections.

**Main result.** The synthesized crystals exhibit robust hallmarks of altermagnetism (AHE, XMCD) alongside complex anisotropic domain kinetics near the Néel temperature ($T_N$), and transition into an Anderson insulating state at lower temperatures.

**Method.** The study employs a combination of advanced characterization techniques including transport measurements ($ho(T)$, Seebeck coefficient), magnetic susceptibility, Electron Paramagnetic Resonance (EPR), and Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) on single crystals grown via self-flux methods.

**Model / system.** The physical system is intrinsic single crystals of MnTe, which exhibits altermagnetism. The research probes the interplay between magnetic ordering, charge localization (Anderson insulating state), and structural/mechanical stress effects in this strongly correlated material.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall effect (AHE) signals, anisotropic hysteresis loops in magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), resistivity ($ho_{xx}$), Seebeck coefficient ($S_{xx}$), EPR linewidth divergence near $T_N$, and Variable Range Hopping (VRH) behavior below $T_{	ext{MI}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Altermagnetic transition temperature ($T_N \approx 310\,\mathrm{K}$), Metal-Insulator Transition temperature ($T_{	ext{MI}} \approx 150\,\mathrm{K}$), and carrier density ($\sim 1.6	imes 10^{17}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the synthesized crystals represent the intrinsic limit, minimizing extrinsic effects like Te-deficiency or mechanical stress.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structure confirmation (SCXRD), anisotropic magnetic hysteresis loops around $T_N$, temperature dependence of transport properties ($ho_{xx}, S_{xx}$), and EPR/NMR spectra showing domain-related broadening and changes across various transition temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically reports on the synthesis methodology to achieve high crystal quality, followed by detailed measurements covering magnetic transitions (domain kinetics via $\chi$), electronic transport ($ho, S$), and local probes of magnetism/localization (EPR, NMR).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the synthesis methodology, structure, and intrinsic properties of ultra-high quality single crystals of MnTe, an archetypal altermagnet. The crystals, obtained from self-flux method, are nearly free from crystal imperfections and disproportionate chemical compositions as seen by various investigation methods. In measurements under quasi free-standing configuration minimizing stress induced effects, the crystals exhibit complex and anisotropic domain kinetics in both superheating and supercooling regimes around the altermagnetic transition at $T_{\mathrm{N}} = 310\,\mathrm{K}$. An Anderson insulating state is observed below $T_{\mathrm{MI}}\approx 150\,\mathrm{K}$ with a carrier density of about $1.6\times 10^{17}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3}$, being sharply contrast to metallic states usually seen in Te-deficit samples. Nevertheless, hallmarks of altermagnetism, anomalous Hall effect and X-ray magnetic circular dichroism signal, are robust in this intrinsic limit, however with significantly reduced magnitudes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical challenge of capacitive loading when scaling fluxonium qubits to 2D arrays. By deriving analytical models based on capacitance budgets, the authors show that this loading can be mitigated through careful geometric engineering of the qubit-coupler layout. They demonstrate that high-fidelity gates are achievable in large processors by optimizing parasitic coupling paths.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, physics-based engineering guidelines for scaling superconducting qubits by quantifying parasitic losses, which is crucial for realizing large quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Capacitive loading is a major obstacle preventing the scaling of fluxonium qubits from 1D to highly connected 2D architectures, and its physical origin remains poorly understood.

**Main result.** The authors show that capacitive loading does not constitute a fundamental limit for 2D fluxonium quantum processors if proper design principles are followed by optimizing qubit-coupler geometry.

**Method.** They derive analytical relations and use circuit QED analysis to model the coupling strength ($J_{qc}$) based on capacitance participation ratios, comparing optimized vs. conventional layouts.

**Model / system.** The system is a fluxonium quantum processor extended to 2D arrays with double-transmon couplers (DTCs). The analysis uses circuit QED principles and Hamiltonian modeling for qubit-coupler interactions.

**Key observables.** Achievable two-qubit gate coupling strength ($J_{qc}$), capacitive loading factors ($	ext{C}_{	ext{ab}}/	ext{C}_{	ext{eff}}^q$, $	ext{C}_{	ext{bg}}/	ext{C}_{	ext{eff}}^{	ext{bg}}$), and gate infidelity ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Capacitance budget, coupling energy ($J_{qc}$), parasitic capacitances, and the minimum physical junction size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The leakage analysis assumes that the overall leakage probability is dominated by the most dominant parasitic transition path.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate coupling geometries, show $J_{qc}$ vs. capacitance budgets comparing optimized to conventional layouts, and detail high-connectivity processor unit cells (e.g., 7q6c).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the fundamental analytical relations governing capacitive loading in simple models, to developing quantitative design principles using participation ratios, and finally demonstrating these principles on complex, high-connectivity 2D architectures while analyzing gate fidelity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Capacitive loading has emerged as a major obstacle to scaling fluxonium qubits from one-dimensional to highly connected two-dimensional (2D) architectures, yet its physical origin remains poorly understood. We derive an analytical relation between the qubit capacitance budget and the achievable capacitive coupling to external circuit elements, identifying the parasitic capacitances of Josephson junctions and Josephson junction arrays as the dominant source of capacitive loading while showing that the qubit-pad geometry can instead be engineered to mitigate it. Building on these insights, we formulate practical design principles and numerically demonstrate ultrafast, high-fidelity two-qubit gates in 2D fluxonium architectures. Our results reveal that capacitive loading does not constitute a fundamental limit for 2D fluxonium quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates how non-invertible symmetries, particularly those involving duality, manifest on quantum lattices. By comparing continuum predictions from Witt group theory with explicit constructions using Quantum Cellular Automata, the authors characterize the underlying algebraic structure governing these exotic symmetries in 3+1 dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep connection between abstract algebraic structures (Witt groups) and concrete physical realizations in lattice models (QCA), which is crucial for understanding topological order beyond simple symmetry protection.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses constructing a lattice framework for non-invertible symmetries, particularly those involving duality operations, in quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** It establishes that the group of locality-preserving automorphisms on the lattice matches predictions from advanced categorical calculations (the pointed Witt group), identifying specific Quantum Cellular Automata (QCA) generators for these symmetries.

**Method.** The authors use algebraic methods, comparing continuum defect relations derived from the graded Witt group with explicit constructions using local operator algebras and QCA acting on spin lattices.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum many-body systems exhibiting a Zp one-form symmetry in 3+1 dimensions. The mathematical machinery involves analyzing automorphisms of constrained local spin operator algebras built upon a cubic lattice structure.

**Key observables.** The group structure of locality-preserving automorphisms, which is related to the Clifford group for p=2 and SL(2, Zp) for odd primes p.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry parameter p (prime), the dimensionality (3+1d), and the specific form of the symmetry ($\mathbb{Z}_p$ 1-form).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on conjectures regarding the full group of automorphisms modulo finite depth circuits, and some proofs are noted to fall short of rigor.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but notes reference Tables I and II comparing continuum defect theory relations with lattice operators (KWW, TW).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the symmetry problem using $\mathbb{Z}_p$ 1-form symmetries, then calculating the expected group structure via Witt groups, and finally realizing this structure microscopically through specific Quantum Cellular Automata acting on local operator algebras.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Self-dualities and the stacking of symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases are basic operations on quantum many-body systems. For a $\mathbb{Z}_p$ one-form symmetry in 3+1d these correspond to the Kramers-Wannier-Wegner duality $S$, which is the gauging operation underlying non-invertible duality symmetries, and the stacking of a 1-form symmetry SPT $T$. In the continuum, they form a central extension of $PSL(2,\mathbb{Z}_4)$ for $p=2$, and of $SL(2,\mathbb{Z}_p)$ for odd primes $p$, whose central elements are invertible theories with purely gravitational response. These central extensions are governed by a twisted, graded generalization of the Witt group of abelian anyon theories, which we determine. For $p=2$ the resulting group is the single-qubit Clifford group, with duality and entangler acting as the Hadamard and phase gates. We realize this entire structure microscopically as quantum cellular automata (QCA) acting on a certain local operator algebra associated with a spin lattice Hilbert space on a cubic lattice. Specifically, our local operator algebra is built by starting with all local operators commuting with a $\mathbb{Z}_p$ 1-form symmetry, and taking the quotient by all the (local) 1-form symmetry generators. The central elements can always be extended to the full tensor product algebra with a uniquely defined QCA class. For $p=2$ they are generated by the non-trivial semion QCA, and for odd prime $p$ they are generated by the non-trivial $\mathbb{Z}_p$ Clifford QCA. Consequently the lattice fusion rules reproduce the continuum ones only up to these QCAs and lattice translations, giving rise to fusion rules refined by QCAs.

</details>

The figure shows query-count and break-even diagnostics only, not simulator or quantum-hardware runtime. **Summary.** This paper introduces QC-PHAST, a formal protocol designed to guide the search for rare physical regimes within parameterized dynamical systems. It rigorously compares the potential efficiency gains of quantum query models (like Grover's algorithm) against classical simulation methods by accounting for noise and data access limitations. The work provides a decision framework rather than a single solution, helping researchers understand when advanced computational resources are truly necessary.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO physics, the rigorous framework for determining when advanced computational tools (like quantum queries) provide a genuine advantage over classical simulation/optimization techniques in complex physical modeling is highly relevant to developing robust theoretical tools for open quantum systems analysis.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses rare-regime discovery in parameterized dynamical systems: finding a specific parameter value where a qualitative physical threshold is crossed, especially when such parameters are scarce or complex.

**Main result.** It develops QC-PHAST, an auditable protocol that determines the optimal search strategy (quantum vs. classical) by quantifying the advantage of quantum query models against classical simulation costs and noise.

**Method.** The methodology introduces QC-PHAST, an evidence-gated decision protocol that compares theoretical quantum query complexity (using Grover/BBHT benchmarks) against practical metrics derived from dynamical system simulations.

**Model / system.** The framework is tested using parameterized dynamical systems, with the FitzHugh-Nagumo (FHN) system serving as a canonical reduced model for excitable dynamics. The search space involves varying parameters that govern phase portraits and stability.

**Key observables.** Simulator-derived criticality score ($s(\mathbf{x}_i)$), verified first-hit predicates, and the ratio of non-quantum to BBHT query counts are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rarity/density of acceptable candidates (marked set size $M$ relative to total pool size $N$), noise levels in measurements, and the specific dynamical system parameters ($\epsilon, a, b$, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum reference is treated purely as a theoretical query-count benchmark, not claiming physical circuit implementation or hardware speedup. The protocol itself is an auditable decision rule.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates FHN regimes and the search process; Figure 2 maps the scientific objective onto measurable components (object, score, predicate); Table 1 summarizes how different dynamical features relate to active-search implications.

**Paper structure.** The paper defines the rare-regime discovery problem, introduces QC-PHAST as a decision protocol, details the comparison between quantum query models and classical search methods using quantitative benchmarks (e.g., 875-configuration sweeps), and concludes by establishing the conditions under which each search paradigm is most informative.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rare-regime discovery in parameterized dynamical systems is an active-search problem: find one verified parameter at which a scientifically defined qualitative threshold is crossed, even when acceptable candidates are rare, nonconvex, or fragmented. We introduce Quantum-Classical Phase-space and Stability-Threshold Search (QC-PHAST), an evidence-gated decision protocol and query-accounting framework for finite candidate libraries. A candidate induces a dynamical object, simulator-derived criticality score, and verified first-hit predicate. Scientific metadata and charged pilot evidence are used to assess whether equation-aware search, scalar-score active search, predicate-only search, or only a query-model comparison is admissible. The quantum row is the inherited Grover/Boyer--Brassard--Hoyer--Tapp (BBHT) unknown-$M$ marked-set query reference; it is not a new quantum-search theorem, materialized circuit, or hardware-speedup claim. The result is a regime map. Direct boundary constructions, geometry controls, online simulator loops, and learned-label accounting further identify when classical structure, false positives, calibration cost, or state preparation erases the query-model margin. QC-PHAST is therefore an auditable protocol for deciding when a finite-pool marked-set reference is informative and when classical or resource-aware search should dominate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an automated method to design highly reliable circuits for preparing specific quantum states required in fault-tolerant quantum computing. By modeling the problem as an Integer Linear Program using circuit gauge operators, the authors achieve state preparation circuits with minimal gate counts while rigorously guaranteeing detection of multiple error types. This work demonstrates a significant step toward practical implementation on current quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation, the rigorous mathematical framework involving graph theory, linear programming, and error detection mechanisms provides deep insights into the structure of fault-tolerant quantum information processing protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing automated, fault-tolerant methods for preparing stabilizer states, which is a crucial subroutine in quantum error correction and initializing logical qubits.

**Main result.** The authors successfully optimize state preparation circuits using Integer Linear Programming (ILP) based on the circuit gauge operator formalism, achieving low gate counts while maintaining high levels of fault detection capability.

**Method.** They formulate the problem as finding a minimum-cost set cover by solving an ILP, which determines the necessary gauge operators to ensure that any error pattern is detectable or results in a distinguishable output error.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general quantum error-correcting codes (e.g., Steane code, two-block group algebra codes) and is tested on physical hardware platforms like Quantinuum's System Model H2.

**Key observables.** Logical block error rate ($\sim 10^{-4}$), circuit gate count, weight of gauge operators, and the ability to detect specific numbers of faults (e.g., up to three errors).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), number of qubits/blocks, and the cost function derived from operator weights.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that expressing fault detection as a weighted set cover solvable by ILP provides an optimal or near-optimal circuit construction compared to existing state-of-the-art methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate various state preparation circuits for multiple QEC codes (e.g., J16, J24), demonstrating how the designed flag measurements detect single and pairs of faults.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the necessity of fault-tolerant state preparation, details the gauge operator formalism, formulates the optimization as an ILP, presents results comparing gate counts to SOTA methods across various codes, and concludes with hardware testing on a specific quantum computer model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Post-selected stabilizer state preparation is a necessary subroutine in fault-tolerant quantum computation, both for initialization of logical qubits, and for logical-ancilla-based error correction gadgets (e.g. Steane and Knill). Therefore, reducing the number of gates needed to prepare a stabilizer state fault-tolerantly can simultaneously reduce time-to-solution and increase reliability. For small, low-distance codes such as the [[7, 1, 3]] Steane code, circuits with low gate counts can be found by inspection. This becomes impractical for larger codes, necessitating automation. There are two state-of-the-art methods for automated fault-tolerant state preparation, SAT-based stabilizer measurement and flag-at-origin. In this work, we optimize state preparation circuits using the circuit gauge operator formalism to express the construction of flag circuits as an integer linear program. This allows the construction of circuits with equal or lower gate count than the state of the art, while detecting up to three errors. We use this technique to derive a Steane error correction gadget for the [[24, 10, 4]] two-block group algebra code, and test it on Quantinuum's System Model H2 quantum computer with 10,000 shots, resulting in a logical block error rate ~0.00014 (~0.000014 per logical qubit), with ~1.6% of the shots post-selected due to weight-two errors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes Quantum Spectral Models (QSMs) to improve data encoding in quantum machine learning by deriving the unitary generator directly from the input matrix's spectral properties. By analyzing how different structural components—like eigenvalues versus eigenvectors—contribute to performance on various tasks, the authors demonstrate that structure-aware design yields superior results over standard models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML, the explicit use of spectral decomposition and structural constraints derived from physical Hamiltonians ($H(M)$) provides a powerful framework for designing quantum circuits that respect underlying mathematical symmetries or physical conservation laws relevant in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is designing quantum machine learning models whose inductive bias explicitly incorporates the structure of matrix-valued input data, moving beyond simple coordinate-wise encodings.

**Main result.** Quantum Spectral Models (QSMs) consistently outperform tested quantum and classical benchmarks across multiple datasets by leveraging input-conditioned spectral representations. The optimal structural reliance (spectral values vs. subspaces) is task-dependent.

**Method.** The authors introduce QSMs that construct the data-encoding unitary generator directly from the input matrix, leading to an output with a truncated Fourier representation governed by input-derived spectral gaps and subspaces.

**Model / system.** The model utilizes Quantum Spectral Models (QSMs) which encode data via Hamiltonians derived from the input matrix (e.g., symmetric or block structures). The architecture alternates between these Hamiltonian unitaries and trainable mixers.

**Key observables.** Mean test accuracy across four benchmarks, target alignment (KTA), fidelity gap, and ablation performance metrics comparing spectrum-only vs. subspace-preserving controls.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth (tested up to 32) and the specific structure of the input Hamiltonian (symmetric, global block, patch-local).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that conditioning the unitary generator on spectral properties provides a superior, analysable inductive bias for quantum data encoding.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare performance across depths and show diagnostic plots tracking target alignment (KTA) and effective rank evolution during training. Tables summarize quantitative comparisons against classical baselines and ablation studies.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QSMs, details the Hamiltonian construction and Fourier representation, evaluates them on multiple datasets (Pendigits/Synthetic), analyzes performance diagnostics layer-by-layer, and concludes with an ablation study to dissect the necessity of spectral vs. subspace information.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central design principle in modern machine learning and artificial intelligence is to align a model's inductive bias with the structure of its input data. For matrix-valued inputs, relevant matrix-level relationships can be characterised through spectral values and spectral subspaces; however, common coordinate-wise rotation-gate data-encoding unitaries used in most quantum machine learning models do not explicitly construct such a matrix-level representation. We introduce Quantum Spectral Models (QSMs), in which we construct the generator of the data-encoding unitary directly from each input matrix. We study three QSM variants based on symmetric, global block, and non-overlapping patch-local block Hamiltonians. Their outputs admit truncated Fourier representations in which input-dependent spectral gaps supply candidate phase carriers, while spectral subspaces help determine their coefficients. We evaluate the QSMs and comparison quantum models on two matrix representations of Pendigits and two controlled synthetic tasks defined by spectral statistics. At the largest evaluated circuit depth, QSM variants lead the tested quantum models in mean test accuracy across all four benchmarks. The patch-local QSM leads on Pendigits, whereas the global block-Hamiltonian QSM leads on the controlled spectral tasks. Ablations show a task-dependent reversal: subspace-preserving controls perform better on Pendigits, whereas spectral-value-only controls lead among the tested ablations on the synthetic tasks. Together, these results shed new light on quantum machine-learning model design by showing how input-conditioned spectral representations can provide an analysable inductive bias, while offering a broader perspective on structure-aware model design in machine learning and artificial intelligence.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes an advanced method to maintain fault tolerance in shuttling quantum processors despite significant physical damage. By adapting pathfinding algorithms to route syndrome measurements around defective lattice sites, the authors show that 10% damage only reduces the effective code distance by a predictable factor. This demonstrates that mobility significantly enhances hardware robustness compared to fixed-qubit approaches.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, quantitative framework for assessing hardware resilience in solid-state quantum computing platforms, directly addressing scalability limitations imposed by physical defects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses how fault-tolerant quantum computation can maintain functionality when subjected to physical damage (defects or 'drop outs') in the underlying hardware.

**Main result.** The proposed shuttling-based method allows for substantial damage tolerance, achieving performance equivalent to a pristine lattice with only 48% to 60% of its distance even after 10% node deletion.

**Method.** It adapts the CAbLECAR method by using route-solving software on time-expanded graphs to find efficient paths for ancillas performing syndrome measurements around damaged physical lattices.

**Model / system.** The model is based on semiconductor electron spin-qubit systems configured in a latticework architecture supporting shuttling. It implements the surface code and simulates damage by deleting nodes from this lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate (LER), reduced equivalent surface-code distance ($d_{	ext{equiv}}$), and the maximum achievable code distance ($d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Damage fraction ($f$), shuttle error rate ($p_{	ext{shuttle}}$), idling error rate, and the lattice size ($G 	imes G$ array).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The damage is assumed to be static and known before runtime; the analysis focuses on the surface code in memory mode.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the latticework structure showing pristine vs. 10% deleted nodes, detailing location, path, and ancillary node placements.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of damage tolerance in shuttling qubits, details the adaptation of CAbLECAR using route-solving on damaged lattices, presents results quantifying performance degradation via $d_{	ext{equiv}}$, and concludes by generalizing the approach to other QLDPC codes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This is a short study of an approach offering high tolerance to damage (i.e. defects or 'drop outs') in solid state fault-tolerant quantum computing. Our method is primarily aimed at semiconductor electron spin-qubit systems, which have been shown to support fast and high-fidelity shuttling along pre-defined paths. We adapt the recent CAbLECAR method of Chadwick and Chong: stabilisers are performed by ancillas which each follow a bespoke pre-programmed path. We consider the simple surface code but we damage the physical lattice, and rely on route-solving software to find efficient pathways under constraints enforcing stabiliser commutation and hook error avoidance. Solutions are then converted to detector error models for Stim and logical error rates are obtained. We express our results by gauging the logical performance against that of a pristine lattice, using the notion of a reduced equivalent surface-code distance; for reasonable underlying error rates we find that $10\%$ damage leaves roughly half of the pristine equivalent distance ($d_\text{equiv}\approx0.48\,d_\text{pristine}$ in the large-array limit, rising to $\approx0.60$ for our smallest array). This suggests that one can tolerate substantial damage by building oversized arrays. We note that investigating damage tolerance of other qLDPC codes is a straightforward generalisation, and potentially one could adapt to damage emerging at runtime.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper rigorously analyzes the limits of quantum parameter estimation using bipartite probes under $SU(d)$ symmetry. It derives exact mathematical identities demonstrating that maximal Fisher sensitivity conflicts with full local identifiability, leading to inherent 'blind directions' in the measurement spectrum. The findings establish a hierarchy among distinct resources—sensitivity, compatibility, and identifiability—in non-commuting generator systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural constraints on quantum estimation protocols, showing that achieving optimal precision requires sacrificing full parameter distinguishability in certain directions, which is a crucial concept for designing practical metrology schemes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental trade-offs between achieving maximal total Fisher sensitivity, ensuring full local identifiability, and satisfying weak compatibility constraints in quantum metrology.

**Main result.** It establishes exact identities linking the Fisher information trace to measurable probe properties, showing that maximizing sensitivity forces blind directions unless generalized GHZ probes are used for $N \ge 3$.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving exact sum rules relating the Fisher matrix and incompatibility measures, utilizing structural decompositions of the underlying Lie algebra $\mathfrak{su}(d)$, and comparing bounds derived from different probe symmetries.

**Model / system.** The system is a pure bipartite quantum probe state used to estimate an unknown collective $SU(d)$ transformation. The analysis focuses on the structure of the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) associated with this estimation process.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information Matrix ($\mathcal{F}$), Total Fisher Budget ($	ext{tr} \mathcal{F}$), Exchange Symmetry Expectation ($\langle\mathcal{S}angle$), and Weak Incompatibility Measure ($\Omega_{\mu
u}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $d$ (the dimension of the $SU(d)$ group), the number of qubits/probes ($N$), and the structure of entanglement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a single fixed collective setting for the probe measurement, and later compares results across different structural assumptions like exchange symmetry or weak compatibility ($\Omega=0$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 visualizes the exact conservation law between $	ext{tr} \mathcal{F}$ and $\|\Omega\|_{	ext{HS}}^2$ for $d=3$, while Figure 2 compares QFIM spectra on the maximally entangled boundary for $d=4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first deriving exact identities relating Fisher sensitivity to two measurable properties (Chunk 1). It then analyzes the implications of weak compatibility and entanglement structure (Chunk 2). Finally, it tackles lower bounds on the inverse QFIM trace using advanced techniques involving $	au$-involutions and Fourier-frame constructions (Chunks 3 & 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

For pure two-qudit probes used in a single fixed setting to estimate a common SU(d) transformation, maximal total Fisher sensitivity and full local identifiability are mutually exclusive. We derive exact identities that determine the trace of the quantum Fisher information matrix from two measurable probe properties: exchange symmetry and collective polarization. Every maximizer is exchange symmetric and maximally entangled. Its Fisher matrix has equal sensitivity in every visible direction, while $d(d-1)/2$ generator directions are blind. More generally, weak compatibility, defined by vanishing mean generator commutators, forces at least $\lfloor d/2\rfloor$ blind directions for every pure bipartite probe. A probe-defined antiunitary invariant classifies the complete Fisher spectrum at maximal entanglement, and approaches to the optimum that preserve exchange symmetry have a divergent Holevo cost. The bipartite obstruction is sharp in particle number: for $N\geq 3$, generalized GHZ probes can satisfy weak compatibility, attain the corresponding $N$-partite Fisher trace bound, and retain full local identifiability. Hence, Fisher sensitivity, weak compatibility, identifiability, and attainable precision are distinct resources in quantum metrology with noncommuting generators.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how the number of stable fixed points in disordered neural networks changes as coupling strength increases. Using large-deviation statistics and perturbation theory, the authors predict that SFPs disappear through bifurcations at a critical coupling $g_c$. The findings establish a quantitative link between disorder structure and dynamical stability in these complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical dynamics, the analysis of stability boundaries, bifurcations, and the role of disorder in determining system attractors shares conceptual links with understanding phase transitions or localization phenomena in quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the multiplicity of stable fixed-points (SFPs) in disordered neural ordinary differential equation models.

**Main result.** For small coupling strengths, there are no qualitative differences between symmetric and asymmetric dynamics; SFP depletion occurs via saddle-node bifurcations at a critical coupling strength $g_c$.

**Method.** The authors employ large-deviation statistics combined with a perturbative method in the disorder amplitude to estimate the number of stable attractors.

**Model / system.** The model consists of N neurons governed by nODEs, featuring local double-well potentials and interactions mediated by a random coupling matrix $A$.

**Key observables.** Average fraction of SFPs ($P_N(g)$), the minimum value $u$ derived from correlated Gaussian variables, and the multiplicity index $h(g)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($g$), disorder amplitude, and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The perturbative method is reliable for small $g$, and asymptotic analysis relies on large-deviation theory approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the sharp drop in SFP fraction vs. coupling strength (Fig. 1), comparisons between perturbative estimates and exact solutions near bifurcations (Fig. 2), and density estimations $ho_N(u)$ for different matrix ensembles (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using large-deviation theory to calculate SFP multiplicity, applies this to specific disordered neural models (asymmetric vs. symmetric coupling), derives analytical forms for the distribution of minima $u$, and compares these predictions against numerical simulations across different coupling regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show how large-deviation statistics allows one to obtain reliable estimates of the multiplicity of stable fixed-points in a model of neural ordinary differential equations previously employed in computational tasks. The result is obtained by developing a suitable perturbative method in the amplitude of the disorder. It turns out that for not-too-large coupling strengths there are no qualitative differences between the symmetric case, when the dynamics is a purely gradient evolution, and the asymmetric case, when limit cycles and chaos can, in principle, arise. The selection of this specific model is dictated by pedagogical reasons, but we are confident that the approach can be extended to other many-degree-of-freedom dynamical models characterized by different classes of random coupling matrices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Hyperspatial Replica Exchange (HS-REX), an advanced enhanced sampling technique that artificially increases the dimensionality of a physical system. By utilizing extra dimensions, HS-REX allows simulations to bypass high free-energy barriers inaccessible in the original space. This method is shown to efficiently sample complex conformational changes in biomolecules like alanine dipeptide, outperforming conventional temperature replica exchange.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the concept of mapping complex dynamics onto higher-dimensional spaces to achieve better sampling parallels theoretical approaches used in quantum simulation and optimization problems where high barriers limit exploration.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is simulating physical systems trapped in rugged free-energy landscapes due to high energy barriers between metastable states.

**Main result.** Hyperspatial Replica Exchange (HS-REX) successfully circumvents these barriers by artificially extending the system into higher dimensions, achieving enhanced ergodic sampling compared to standard methods.

**Method.** The method extends the physical Hamiltonian into extra spatial dimensions and uses a replica exchange framework where exchanges occur based on varying harmonic penalties in these added dimensions.

**Model / system.** Benchmark systems include a double-well model and alanine dipeptide simulated in explicit water. The formalism involves extending the Hamiltonian to $D = d + d_+$ dimensions with a quadratic penalty term applied selectively to solute atoms.

**Key observables.** Enhanced conformational sampling, successful crossing of energy barriers (e.g., L/D chirality inversion), and significantly reduced round-trip simulation times compared to Parallel Tempering (PT).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The harmonic penalty strength ($\mu$) is varied across replicas; the method's efficiency depends on maintaining appropriate acceptance rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique assumes that the added dimensions define a fast subsystem suitable for exact propagation methods, and the penalty is applied only to solute atoms to maintain physical relevance in solvated systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare free-energy surfaces ($\Delta F$) showing enhanced exploration via HS-REX versus trapping in PT. Other figures compare acceptance rates and round-trip time distributions, demonstrating superior mixing for HS-REX.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of hyperspatial extension to overcome barriers, validates it on a simple double-well model, and then applies it rigorously to biomolecular systems like alanine dipeptide in water, comparing performance metrics (acceptance rates, time scales) against established methods like PT.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating systems with rugged free-energy landscapes remains a central challenge in computational physics and chemistry. We introduce hyperspatial replica exchange (HS-REX), an enhanced sampling method in which the physical system is artificially extended by additional spatial dimensions. In higher dimensions, free-energy barriers can be circumvented through paths that are geometrically inaccessible in the original space. Restricting the penalty to only solute atoms dramatically reduces the number of replicas required for solvated systems compared to standard temperature replica exchange, a feature especially relevant for biological applications. As proof of concept, we demonstrate the method on a double-well model system and on alanine dipeptide in explicit water as benchmark system. In the latter case, HS-REX achieves enhanced conformational sampling of not only the slow backbone dihedral angles, but also both chiral configurations of the molecule, which are sterically inaccessible to standard sampling in three dimensions. This demonstrates enhanced ergodic sampling over conventional temperature replica exchange.

</details>

### [Non-Clifford quantum cellular automata from invertible topological quantum field theories](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21697v1)

**Authors:** Meng Sun, Zongyuan Wang, Bowen Yang, Nathanan Tantivasadakarn, Yu-An Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21697v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: Workflow for constructing the QCAs in this work. A commuting Hamiltonian is first obtained through condensation or higher-form gauging. Cup-product identities reorganize its generators into one candidate separator per microscopic degree of freedom. Compatible local flippers then complete the separator algebra, whose identification with the on-site clock and shift algebra defines the QCA.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a unified algebraic construction to derive Quantum Cellular Automata (QCAs) directly from invertible Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs). By extending the Hamiltonian generators into a complete separator-flipper algebra, the authors provide a systematic route to construct and classify QCAs in higher dimensions. This advances our understanding of how topological invariants dictate microscopic quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a systematic, algebraic bridge between abstract topological field theories (a mathematical/theoretical framework) and concrete, physically realizable quantum dynamics models like QCAs, which are crucial for understanding non-equilibrium many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a unified algebraic construction to derive Quantum Cellular Automata (QCAs) directly from invertible Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs). This addresses the challenge that a TQFT action alone does not specify the full local operator algebra automorphism required for a QCA.

**Main result.** The authors provide a general, systematic algebraic framework to convert invertible TQFTs into microscopic QCAs in various dimensions. They also proved that certain higher-dimensional QCAs are stably trivial by constructing finite-depth quantum circuits for them.

**Method.** The method involves extending the commuting generators of the Hamiltonian to a complete separator-flipper algebra on the full Hilbert space, utilizing techniques from cochain theory and cup products to define the necessary local automorphisms.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Quantum Cellular Automata (QCAs), which model locality-preserving quantum dynamics. The input physics is an invertible TQFT response action, leading to constructions in dimensions $d=4k-1$ and general higher dimensions.

**Key observables.** The resulting QCA must be an automorphism of the full local operator algebra; key diagnostics include identifying generalized U(1) QCAs associated with products of Wu classes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spatial dimension ($d$), specific topological invariants (e.g., those derived from Witt groups or Wu classes), and algebraic structures like separator-flipper algebras.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the QCA structure can be fully captured by extending the Hamiltonian generators into a complete, commuting algebra of local operators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the workflow: starting from a TQFT response action to obtain a Hamiltonian, then constructing candidate separators via cup-products, and finally defining the full QCA automorphism using flippers.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the algebraic necessity of moving beyond just the ground state Hamiltonian. It then details the unified construction for 3D systems, generalizes this to higher dimensions ($d=4k-1$), provides explicit constructions for arbitrary Wu class products, and concludes with proofs of triviality for specific cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum cellular automata (QCAs) describe locality-preserving quantum dynamics and connect quantum information, many-body physics, and topological quantum field theory (TQFT). Constructing a QCA from a TQFT, however, is challenging. Although a topological action can produce a commuting Hamiltonian realizing the desired ground state, it does not by itself specify an automorphism of the full local operator algebra. In this work, we develop a unified algebraic construction that extends the commuting generators of the Hamiltonian to a complete separator-flipper algebra on the full tensor-product Hilbert space, providing a microscopic definition of the corresponding QCA. In three spatial dimensions, our formalism unifies all previously known QCA constructions associated with the $\mathbb Z_8\times\mathbb Z_2$ subgroup of the Witt group, including the $U(1)_2$ and $U(1)_4$ QCAs. The same algebraic structure directly yields new infinite families of generalized $U(1)_2$ and $U(1)_4$ non-Clifford QCAs in dimensions $d=4k-1$. We also reformulate the 4-dimensional $w_2w_3$ QCA and use it to develop a general construction of QCAs from TQFTs associated with arbitrary products of Wu classes. This construction includes two infinite families. The first consists of $w_2^nw_3^m$ QCAs in dimension $d=2n+3m-1$, while the second consists of $w_2w_{4k-1}$ QCAs in dimension $d=4k$. As a contrasting result, we explicitly construct finite-depth quantum circuits for the 5-dimensional $w_3^2$ and $w_2^3$ QCAs, thereby proving that they are trivial, in agreement with the cobordism classification. Overall, these results convert invertible TQFTs into microscopic QCAs, provide a scalable route to higher-dimensional constructions beyond the Clifford setting, and open a systematic approach to classifying their stable structures and boundary anomalies.

</details>

**Authors:** Florian Lorenz, Jan Möseritz-Schmidt, Roland Ketzmerick  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22052v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

Resonance state of a scattering system with piecewise homogeneous properties showing its real part in position space (left) and on the boundary (right). While its norm is defined by an integral over infinite space, we show in various physical settings that it can be computed using a convenient boundary integral only.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the technical difficulty of normalizing resonance states whose wavefunctions diverge at infinity by replacing divergent volume integrals with boundary surface integrals using the divergence theorem. It provides general, rigorous formulas applicable to both quantum particle scattering problems and electromagnetic cavity analysis, making it a key theoretical resource for open quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides essential, rigorous mathematical tools for calculating quantities like coupling strengths or state normalization in open quantum systems where standard $L^2$ methods fail due to continuum coupling. This is vital for modeling resonance phenomena in quantum optics and scattering theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is defining and calculating the 'norm' of resonance states in quantum scattering and electromagnetic systems because the standard $L^2$-norm integral diverges at infinity.

**Main result.** The paper successfully derives methods to replace the divergent volume integral with a mathematically tractable boundary integral using the divergence theorem, yielding explicit formulas for the norm.

**Method.** The core method involves applying the divergence theorem (Green's identities) to rewrite volume integrals of products of resonance states into surface integrals over the system boundaries. Zel'dovich regularization is used to handle the asymptotic divergences.

**Model / system.** The work covers two main areas: quantum particles in $D$-dimensional spaces with hard-wall or piecewise constant potentials, and electromagnetic fields within cavities possessing piecewise homogeneous material properties (2D/3D).

**Key observables.** The resonance state norm ($\langle\psi_L|\psi_Rangle$ or $N_{TM}, N_{TE}$), which quantifies the normalization of these quasi-bound states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Complex eigenfrequencies ($\lambda$), boundary geometry (e.g., spherical scatterers, circular disks), and material properties defining piecewise homogeneity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies heavily on expressing the integrand as a divergence of a suitable vector field and employing regularization techniques to manage infinite integrals.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates that the norm, defined over infinite space, can be computed entirely via an integral restricted to the finite boundary surface ($\partial\Gamma$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops general mathematical frameworks by first establishing the need for regularization and then systematically applying the divergence theorem. It applies these derived boundary integral formulas separately to quantum scattering problems (hard-wall potentials) and electromagnetic cavity problems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Resonance states spatially diverge and are thus not square integrable. Instead, their norm is defined by the biorthogonal scalar product of left and right states. We replace the corresponding volume integral by a convenient boundary integral in piecewise homogeneous systems and apply this procedure to diverse physical settings. For a quantum particle in any number of dimensions we treat hard-wall and piecewise constant potentials. For electromagnetic systems with piecewise homogeneous material properties, we consider three-dimensional and effectively two-dimensional cavities of arbitrary shape. As examples, we treat the spherical scatterer and the circular disk.

</details>
