**Summary.** This review details Quasiparticle Interference (QPI) as a vital experimental technique for probing the low-energy electronic structure of quantum materials. By analyzing standing wave patterns observed via STM, researchers can map out momentum-space information related to electron scattering. This methodology has proven invaluable across diverse fields, from topological insulators to unconventional superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the underlying physics involves many-body dynamics and electronic correlations in strongly interacting systems, which shares conceptual overlap with open quantum system descriptions of quasiparticle interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Understanding the properties of quantum materials requires detailed knowledge of their low energy electronic structure to enable control and design.

**Main result.** Quasiparticle interference (QPI) imaging using STM provides a powerful, real-space tool to map out the underlying electronic structure, revealing details about correlations and symmetries in various novel materials.

**Method.** The study reviews QPI principles, which are experimentally detected via Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) by analyzing standing wave modulations in the Local Density of States (LDOS). Theoretical modeling is crucial for interpreting these complex scattering patterns.

**Model / system.** The focus is on various quantum materials, including topological insulators, Weyl semimetals, graphene, and unconventional superconductors. The analysis relies on STM measurements of tunneling current/conductance ($g(r, V)$) which are sensitive to the electronic structure via quasiparticle scattering.

**Key observables.** Standing wave patterns in LDOS modulations; Fourier Transform ($	ilde{g}(q_x, q_y, V)$) revealing momentum-space information (dispersion relations); superconducting gap structures and Fermi arc signatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low energy electronic structure details; temperature regimes down to 50mK; magnetic fields; scattering vectors ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation of QPI data requires advanced theoretical modeling, and experimental measurements must account for artifacts like the setpoint effect or raster scanning distortions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate initial QPI experiments on noble metal surfaces showing standing wave patterns and extracted dispersion relations, alongside diagrams detailing the STM working principle and tunneling current measurement geometry.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses by first establishing the necessity of electronic structure knowledge. It then details the experimental capabilities of STM/QPI, covering theory (elastic vs. inelastic tunneling), data acquisition ($g(r, V)$), and analysis (Fourier Transform). Finally, it reviews successful applications across diverse quantum materials.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

To understand the properties of quantum materials a detailed knowledge of the material's low energy electronic structure is key. Details of the electronic structure drive the ground state through electronic instabilities, electronic correlation effects, new electronic orders or just the absence of electronic states near the Fermi energy - making a realistic and detailed understanding crucial to be able to control and design properties of quantum materials. The past 25 years have seen a significant improvement in experimental techniques to observe the true electronic structure, in particular in techniques such as Angle resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) where energy resolutions of 2meV are routinely achievable now, which however is limited to zero magnetic field and only provides information about the occupied states. Scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) achieves a significantly better energy resolution <100$μ$eV and can operate at temperatures well below 50mK and in magnetic fields. While per se a real-space technique, by imaging quasiparticle interference (QPI) STM can also provide information about the electronic structure. This technique has been used over the past decades to study a wide range of quantum materials to understand correlated electron behaviour. Recent theoretical progress now enables routine modelling of QPI, a key requirement to interpret the complex data. Here, we review the principles of QPI, its origin, experimental detection, and the physical insight gained from the study of QPI and possible future directions for this technique.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically compares three quantum imaging techniques—Ghost Imaging, Two-Photon Imaging, and Undetected Photon Imaging—for estimating an object's transmission profile. By calculating Quantum Fisher Information matrices, the authors determine that while GI and TP are generally more precise, UP offers the unique advantage of allowing independent estimation for each spatial mode. The work provides practical guidelines for designing quantum sensing technologies based on photon correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for optimizing quantum sensing in optical systems by quantifying the fundamental limits imposed by quantum correlations, which is highly relevant to advanced quantum optics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the ultimate quantum precision limits for estimating multiple unknown transmission coefficients ($\{	au_k\}$) characterizing an object using different quantum imaging schemes.

**Main result.** While Ghost Imaging and Two-Photon Imaging generally offer higher precision than imaging with undetected photons, the latter is unique in allowing independent estimation of each spatial mode's transmission coefficient.

**Method.** The authors formulate the problem as a quantum multiparameter estimation task, calculating and comparing Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) matrices derived from various measurement protocols.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum imaging using spatially correlated photon pairs generated via Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC). The object's transmission is modeled by unknown coefficients ($	au_k$) acting on the probe state.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information matrices ($F$ and $Q$), achievable variances ($\sigma(	au)$), and the comparison between Ghost Imaging (GI), Two-Photon Imaging (TP), and Undetected Photon Imaging (UP).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Object transmission coefficients ($	au_k$), Schmidt number ($K$), and the spatial correlation properties of the photon pairs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the singular-mode basis characterizing the object structure is known beforehand; furthermore, some analyses simplify by assuming the object basis matches the signal Schmidt basis.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 plots variances ($\sigma_{kk}$) for GI, TP, and UP as functions of transmission $	au_k$, showing a comparative performance landscape across different schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops quantum metrology tools (QFI/QCRB) to analyze three distinct imaging protocols (GI, TP, UP), deriving the optimal measurement settings for each case and comparing their resulting precision limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compare the performance of quantum imaging schemes based on spatially correlated photon pairs by formulating them as quantum multiparameter estimation problems, in which the object is characterized by transmission coefficients associated with different spatial modes. Our work focuses on standard quantum imaging techniques such as ghost imaging, two-photon imaging, and imaging with undetected photons. Specifically, we compute the quantum Fisher information matrices and show that they are saturated by Fisher information matrices corresponding to measurements in the object-mode basis, which generalize the common detection schemes employed in each imaging configuration. We find that ghost imaging and two-photon imaging generally provide higher precision for transmission estimation than imaging with undetected photons, but the latter is the only one that naturally does not couple transmission estimation across different spatial modes. These results identify which imaging protocols are best suited for specific tasks and provide practical guidelines for the design and optimization of quantum sensing technologies based on spatial correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work resolves a fundamental ambiguity in calculating the Quantum Geometric Tensor by proposing a physically mandated definition using a covariant derivative that includes the full orbital position operator. The authors demonstrate that ignoring this embedding leads to unphysical results, particularly in low-energy effective theories near gapped regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state theory, the concept of defining observables via covariant derivatives incorporating full position operators is a general mathematical tool applicable to open quantum systems where boundary conditions or spatial degrees of freedom must be rigorously accounted for.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard calculation of the Quantum Geometric Tensor (QGT) suffers from an ambiguity dependent on the choice of unit-cell convention when dealing with Bloch states and $k$-derivatives.

**Main result.** A physically mandated, convention-independent QGT must be defined using a covariant derivative that explicitly incorporates the full position operator, revealing leading-order failures in standard k.p effective theories for gapped systems.

**Method.** The authors introduce a covariant derivative $D_\mu = i[\cdot, \hat{r}_\mu]$ to define a physical QGT, contrasting it with conventional $k$-derivative approaches.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses general condensed matter models, specifically demonstrating the issue using the Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) model and a one-dimensional massive Dirac Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Quantum Geometric Tensor ($	ext{QGT}$), quantum metric ($g_{\mu
u}$), Berry curvature ($\mathcal{F}_{\mu
u}$), Wannier spread, interband optical conductivity ($\sigma_{xx}(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond-ordered gaps, orbital embedding parameter ($c$), hopping amplitudes ($t_1, t_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard $k$-derivative approach is insufficient because it neglects the explicit dependence on intra-unit-cell coordinates ($\hat{X}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare results for SSH models and Dirac Hamiltonians, showing that convention A leads to unphysical asymmetries in observables like conductivity, while convention B restores necessary symmetries.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the ambiguity in QGT calculation; it then uses the SSH model to demonstrate the failure of standard derivatives; subsequently, it analyzes gapped effective theories using the Dirac Hamiltonian to show that orbital embedding corrections are essential for accurate physical responses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Quantum Geometric Tensor, encompassing the quantum metric and Berry curvature, is a central concept in modern condensed matter physics. However, its standard calculation via $k$-derivatives of the Bloch projector conceals a fundamental ambiguity regarding the choice of unit-cell convention, specifically in the treatment of intra-cell orbital positions (i.e., with or without the orbital position $e^{ikx_α}$). We resolve this inconsistency by introducing a convention-independent physical QGT defined via a covariant derivative that explicitly incorporates the full position operator. We demonstrate that this formulation is uniquely mandated by the microscopic derivation of the physical current via the Peierls substitution. Notably, we uncover a leading-order failure in standard $k \cdot p$ effective theories for systems with bond-ordered gaps, identifying a need for caution in their application. Finally, we propose geometric engineering as a new design paradigm, enabling the independent tuning of geometric responses without altering the energy dispersion.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a theoretical framework to solve biharmonic boundary-value problems using advanced quantum linear algebra techniques. By constructing explicit block-encodings for the discretized operators, the authors apply QSVT-VTAA algorithms to achieve efficient solutions across various physical boundary conditions. This work advances the application of quantum computation to classical computational physics challenges.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on PDE solving rather than direct AMO Hamiltonians, the techniques developed—especially efficient block-encoding of structured operators and leveraging QSVT for large sparse matrices—are foundational tools applicable to simulating quantum many-body systems governed by differential equations or complex lattice models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing quantum linear-system algorithms for solving high-order partial differential equations, specifically the biharmonic boundary-value problem.

**Main result.** The authors developed explicit block-encodings tailored to various boundary conditions (periodic, simply supported, Dirichlet–Neumann) and demonstrated that QSVT-VTAA can solve these systems with condition number scaling improvements over direct methods.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing explicit block-encodings of the discretized differential operators and applying Quantum Singular Value Transformation combined with Variable Time Amplitude Amplification (QSVT-VTAA).

**Model / system.** The model is the biharmonic equation ($\Delta^2 u = f$), which arises in continuum mechanics. The problem is discretized using finite-difference or spectral methods, leading to large, sparse linear systems $Ax=b$.

**Key observables.** Condition number scaling ($\kappa(A)$), gate complexity, discretization error ($L^2$ and $L^\infty$ norms), and solution extraction cost.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the PDE (fourth-order), boundary conditions (periodic, simply supported, Dirichlet–Neumann), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes that normalized quantum states for the right-hand side and boundary data are efficiently preparable; separates discretization errors from quantum state-preparation errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic circuits for LCU construction, basic QSVT diagrams, and visualizations of boundary reflections used in Laplacian block-encodings.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the challenge of high-order PDEs. It then details the quantum algorithmic framework (QSVT-VTAA) and how to construct necessary block-encodings for specific boundary conditions, culminating in numerical validation across different physical scenarios.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The biharmonic equation is a prototypical fourth-order partial differential equation whose high-dimensional discretization suffers from rapidly growing degrees of freedom and severe ill-conditioning. We develop QSVT--VTAA quantum linear-system algorithms by constructing explicit block-encodings tailored to periodic, simply supported, and Dirichlet--Neumann boundary conditions. For periodic and simply supported problems, Fourier and sine-transform diagonalizations yield augmented Poisson systems with the condition-number scaling of a second-order operator. For Dirichlet--Neumann problems, we introduce a second-order boundary-corrected finite-difference discretization, establish mesh-independent stability, and construct an explicit block-encoding of the resulting nonsymmetric matrix. We also formulate a coupled-Laplace system with additional boundary unknowns and characterize its complexity in terms of the condition number of the complete augmented matrix. The analysis covers discretization error, block-encoding normalization, gate complexity, and solution extraction under an amplitude-input and quantum-state-output model. Numerical experiments validate the proposed discretizations and the corresponding linear solves.

</details>

### [Frame-Dependent Traces and the Third-Particle Paradox](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21703v1)

**Summary.** This theoretical paper tackles the Third-Particle Paradox by developing the Perspective Relational Trace (PRT) condition. It shows that the paradox is an artifact of comparing inconsistent physical descriptions derived from different Quantum Reference Frames (PN vs. QI). The work provides a refined understanding of subsystem description consistency in quantum theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous framework for understanding how local measurements or subsystem definitions can fail when global reference frames are not properly accounted for in quantum field theory contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Third-Particle Paradox, which arises from inconsistencies when comparing subsystem descriptions across different Quantum Reference Frame (QRF) perspectives.

**Main result.** It introduces the Perspective Relational Trace (PRT) condition, showing that the paradox stems from comparing inequivalent physical layers rather than being a true contradiction.

**Method.** The authors develop novel statistical consistency conditions (PRT) and compare different algebraic structures arising from various tracing procedures (PN vs. QI).

**Model / system.** The analysis uses abstract quantum systems defined on kinematical Hilbert spaces $H = igotimes H_i$, often involving symmetries described by a compact Lie group G.

**Key observables.** Consistency of subsystem discarding, the Perspective Relational Trace (PRT), and entropy decomposition formulas for reduced states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distinction between Perspective-Neutral (PN) and Quantum-Information (QI) approaches; the structure of the underlying symmetry group G.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ideal QRFs and relies on comparing different algebraic structures derived from partial traces over subsystems.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but the discussion implies comparisons between different types of invariant algebras and trace equalities.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the paradox's origins, introduces the PRT condition to characterize state consistency, compares PN and QI descriptions across multiple subsystems, and concludes with implications for boundary-charge structures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Paradox of the Third Particle arises when comparing subsystem descriptions across Quantum Reference Frame (QRF) perspectives. We isolate two distinct origins of the Paradox: the QRF covariance of the partial trace and the failure of the physical Hilbert space to inherit the kinematical tensor-product structure. We give an explicit counterexample to the Relational Trace (RT) resolution: an uncorrelated product state for which the RT statistical condition trivialises. We then introduce a new statistical consistency condition comparing subsystem discarding between external and internal QRFs, together with an associated frame-dependent map, the Perspective Relational Trace (PRT). We argue that our condition captures the operational content of the Paradox: rather than imposing consistency on the whole state space, we characterise exactly the states on which it holds in the Perspective-Neutral (PN) and Quantum-Information (QI) approaches. This separates three levels of description: a PN subsystem of a PN whole, where consistency fails on a characterised set that includes product states; a QI subsystem of a QI whole, where it holds for all states; and a QI subsystem obtained from a PN whole by kinematical partial trace, where the full weakly invariant algebra is recovered, yet consistency holds only on a proper subset. These results show that the PN approach can consistently describe only a closed, isolated system, while the QI approach can accommodate arbitrary subsystems. Tracing out a subsystem from a globally PN state yields a charge-superselected algebra, reproducing in a minimal QRF model the boundary-charge structure of edge modes. We understand the Paradox not as a genuine contradiction, but as the consequence of comparing inequivalent physical layers without tracking which information is externally and which internally accessible.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum-Informed Surrogate Sampling (QISS), a post-processing technique that uses low-order correlations measured from shallow quantum circuits (like QAOA) to build an effective classical probability distribution. This surrogate distribution is then sampled via MCMC to generate candidate solutions for combinatorial optimization problems like MaxCut, achieving performance superior to the raw output of the quantum algorithm itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete pathway for near-term quantum devices: instead of demanding perfect state preparation or deep circuits, it extracts robust statistical information that can be leveraged by scalable classical algorithms to solve hard optimization problems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a post-processing framework, Quantum-Informed Surrogate Sampling (QISS), to generate high-quality candidate solutions for computationally hard combinatorial optimization problems.

**Main result.** QISS significantly outperforms vanilla QAOA by using low-order quantum correlations as input to a classical sampling procedure, showing noise resilience on near-term hardware.

**Method.** The method estimates local observables (low-weight correlations) from shallow quantum circuits and uses these values to construct a factorized surrogate probability distribution over the solution space, which is then sampled via MCMC.

**Model / system.** The framework applies to classical optimization problems like MaxCut and Maximum Independent Set. The underlying physics involves using QAOA on qubits (validated on a 54-qubit QPU) to generate these informative statistics.

**Key observables.** Cut fraction, Energy density ($\epsilon$), low-weight correlations ($\mu_{S_\alpha}$), and comparison against theoretical bounds like the SDP value or Parisi energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($p$), inverse temperature ($eta$), and graph structure (e.g., 3-regular graphs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that low-weight expectation values are sufficient to capture the necessary structural information, allowing for classical sampling without needing full quantum state simulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QISS procedure: estimating correlations from QAOA (Fig. 1A), constructing a factor distribution (Fig. 1B), and showing improved solution quality over vanilla QAOA (Fig. 1C). Other figures compare QISS performance against other algorithms for MaxCut/MIS.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QISS framework, details how low-order correlations from shallow circuits are used to build a classical surrogate distribution, demonstrates its superior performance over direct quantum sampling methods (QAOA), and validates its noise resilience on real hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce Quantum-Informed Surrogate Sampling (QISS), a post-processing framework that generates candidate solutions to combinatorial optimization problems from low-weight correlations of shallow quantum circuits. The quantum device estimates local observables, which are directly accessible by repeated measurements and for which a wide range of error-mitigation tools are available, while candidate solutions are generated classically without explicit dependence on the combinatorial optimization problem itself. We evaluate QISS on Maximum Cut and Maximum Independent Set problems on $N$ variables and show that only $O(N)$ low-order correlators from shallow circuits suffice to produce competitive solutions that surpass vanilla QAOA. For MaxCut on 3-regular graphs, QISS from $p=3$ QAOA correlators outperforms vanilla QAOA at $p=17$ on average, with further improvements possible by warm-starting QAOA. We validate the procedure on the 54-qubit IQM Emerald quantum device and demonstrate its noise resilience. Our results support a regime for near-term optimization in which shallow circuits serve not as direct samplers but as generators of informative statistics for scalable classical sampling.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors theoretically and experimentally characterize $	ext{KCuIn}(	ext{PO}_4)_2$ as a quasi-two-dimensional quantum antiferromagnet. They use DFT to determine its insulating nature and calculate magnetic exchange couplings, which reveal a spin-chain like structure. QMC simulations then successfully model the material's low-temperature magnetic susceptibility, confirming its behavior as a strongly correlated quantum magnet.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed case study of how fundamental solid-state theory (DFT) combined with advanced many-body simulation techniques (QMC) can map out the phase diagram and underlying physics of real, complex quantum magnets.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electronic structure and magnetic properties of the quasi-two-dimensional quantum antiferromagnet $	ext{KCuIn}(	ext{PO}_4)_2$. The goal is to establish a consistent theoretical framework linking its electronic structure, exchange interactions, and collective magnetic behavior.

**Main result.** The compound is confirmed as a quasi-two-dimensional quantum antiferromagnet composed of weakly coupled spin chains. Quantum Monte Carlo simulations successfully reproduce experimental signatures like the susceptibility maximum and field-induced saturation characteristic of low-dimensional magnetism.

**Method.** A combination of Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations using GGA+U, microscopic evaluation of magnetic exchange interactions via the magnetic force theorem, and large-scale Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations were employed. Experimental measurements included magnetization and susceptibility ($\chi(T)$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the distorted honeycomb compound $	ext{KCuIn}(	ext{PO}_4)_2$, featuring localized $	ext{Cu}^{2+}$ ($S=1/2$) moments. The magnetism is modeled using an effective spin-$1/2$ Heisenberg Hamiltonian incorporating dominant isotropic exchange interactions and single-ion anisotropy.

**Key observables.** Temperature-dependent magnetic susceptibility ($\chi(T)$), field-dependent magnetization, calculated exchange couplings ($J_{NNN}$ dominating $J_{NN}$), and the insulating band gap ($\approx 2.0 	ext{ eV}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of next-nearest-neighbor to nearest-neighbor coupling ($J_2/J_1$) is identified as the key parameter controlling the magnetic ground state regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system can be accurately described by an effective spin-$1/2$ Heisenberg Hamiltonian, and interlayer couplings are negligible due to structural separation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the crystal structure showing the distorted honeycomb network. Figure 2 presents experimental data for $\chi(T)$ vs $T$, which is compared against theoretical fits.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the electronic structure using DFT, then calculating the magnetic exchange interactions to derive an effective Hamiltonian. Finally, QMC simulations are used on this model to reproduce and explain the measured low-temperature magnetic response.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the electronic structure and magnetic properties of the distorted honeycomb lattice compound KCuInP2O8 through a combination of experimental measurements, first principles calculations and quantum monte carlo simulations. Density functional theory calculations within the GGA+U framework establishes KCuInP2O8 as an indirect gap insulator with Cu2+ local moments and finite magnetocrystalline anisotropy arising from spin orbit coupling. A microscopic evaluation of magnetic exchange interactions using the magnetic force theorem reveals a pronounced hierarchy of couplings, with the next nearest neighbor interaction dominating over the nearest neighbor exchange, while interlayer couplings remain negligible. This exchange hierarchy naturally maps the system onto weakly coupled antiferromagnetic spin chains embedded in a distorted honeycomb lattice. Motivated by the ab initio estimated exchange interactions, we construct an effective spin half Hamiltonian and investigate its magnetic response using large scale quantum Monte Carlo simulations. The calculated temperature dependent susceptibility and field dependent magnetization quantitatively reproduce the experimental behavior and capture key signatures of low dimensional quantum magnetism, including a broad susceptibility maximum and a field induced saturation at low temperatures. Our results establish KCuInP2O8 as a quasi-two-dimensional quantum antiferromagnet composed of coupled spin chains, providing a consistent theoretical framework that links electronic structure, exchange interactions, and collective magnetic behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the conservation of intrinsic orbital angular momentum for electron vortices moving off-axis through magnetic lenses. The authors prove that this conservation is guaranteed by a hidden SU(1,1) dynamical invariant, even when simple symmetries are broken. This finding is confirmed via detailed theoretical derivations and robust 3D numerical simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The discovery of a non-obvious dynamical symmetry (SU(1,1)) protecting a key quantum observable ($L_i$) in a complex physical setting like nonuniform fields is highly relevant for understanding topological robustness in wave propagation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the intrinsic orbital angular momentum (OAM) of off-axis electron vortices is conserved when propagating through nonuniform magnetic fields, contrasting this with on-axis cases protected by rotational symmetry.

**Main result.** The intrinsic OAM is found to be robustly conserved due to a hidden SU(1,1) dynamical invariant, which is confirmed by both theoretical derivation in the paraxial limit and high-fidelity 3D numerical simulations.

**Method.** The work combines analytical derivations using Hamiltonian mechanics (Heisenberg equations of motion) with advanced numerical techniques like operator-splitting Fourier pseudo-spectral methods to simulate wavepacket evolution.

**Model / system.** The system models an electron vortex propagating in a magnetic field, specifically utilizing the Glaser lens profile $B(z) = B_0(1 + z^2/a^2)^{-1}$. The dynamics are governed by the time-dependent Schrödinger equation with a vector potential $\mathbf{A}$.

**Key observables.** Intrinsic Orbital Angular Momentum ($L_i$), Extrinsic OAM ($L_{	ext{ext}}$), and the conserved SU(1,1) norm.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field variation scale ($a$), initial transverse offset ($y_0$), and topological charge ($\ell$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical proof relies on the paraxial approximation, although numerical results suggest robustness beyond this limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate off-axis vortex propagation through a magnetic lens, showing centroid trajectories and confirming that $L_i$ remains nearly constant despite field gradients and initial offsets.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian formalism for electron dynamics in nonuniform fields, derives equations of motion for expectation values (including $L_{	ext{ext}}$), identifies the hidden SU(1,1) invariant, and validates this conservation law through both analytical proofs and extensive 3D numerical simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rotational symmetry protects the topological charge of on-axis electron vortices but not of off-axis vortices. We identify an additional SU(1,1) dynamical invariant that guarantees conservation of their intrinsic orbital angular momentum within the near-axis approximation. First-principles simulations of an off-axis electron vortex traversing a Glaser lens confirm this prediction, establishing a robust transport mechanism in axisymmetric nonuniform magnetic fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, efficient, and information-theoretically secure Unclonable Encryption scheme using random Pauli operators on $n$ qubits. It achieves state-of-the-art security bounds by leveraging the algebraic structure of the Pauli group within its rigorous proof framework. This advances quantum cryptography by providing a practical primitive for device-independent security.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, physically realizable quantum cryptographic primitive whose security proof is deeply rooted in the algebraic structure of Pauli operators, which are fundamental tools in characterizing quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient, plain-model, one-time information-theoretically secure Unclonable Encryption (UE) scheme for a single classical bit.

**Main result.** The Pauli construction achieves the best known security bound against information-theoretic splitting attacks for $n$-qubit ciphertexts, providing efficiency and security previously lacking in related schemes.

**Method.** The security proof relies on analyzing expectation values involving operators derived from the Pauli group structure, utilizing advanced spectral bound techniques and linear algebra to bound cloning success probabilities.

**Model / system.** The system is based on quantum information theory using the Pauli group ($\mathcal{P}_n$) acting on $n$ qubits. The key is a random non-identity phase-free Pauli operator $	heta$.

**Key observables.** The probability that both separated receivers correctly recover the bit, and expectation values involving operators like $\Xi$, $S_B$, $S_C$, and $D$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), which dictates the exponential security scaling ($	ext{negl}(n) \sim O(2^{-n/2})$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes the existence of pseudorandom function-like states for message extension; the core proof relies on complex spectral analysis techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Pauli construction, proves its security by bounding expectation values related to information leakage (Chunks 2 & 3), and discusses extensions and comparisons with prior work (Chunk 1).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give, to our knowledge, the first plain-model, one-time information-theoretically secure, efficient unclonable encryption scheme for one classical bit. Previous work by Bhattacharyya and Culf (Nature Physics, 2026) and Bhattacharyya, Broadbent, and Culf either only showed $1/\mathsf{poly}(λ)$ security loss or required inefficient encryption/decryption operations. We avoid both of these caveats; in doing so, we obtain (to our knowledge) the first plain-model construction of many-time secure $1 \to 2$ unclonable encryption for arbitrary polynomial-length messages, assuming the existence of pseudorandom function-like states (Bartusek and Goldin).   The key is a uniformly random non-identity phase-free Pauli on $n$ qubits, and bit $a$ is encrypted as a random $(-1)^a$ eigenstate of that Pauli. The scheme is exponentially secure; we prove that the probability that both receivers recover the bit is at most $\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{{2^n}/({4^n-1})} = \frac{1}{2} + O\left(2^{-n/2}\right).$ By a lower bound due to Broadbent, Culf, and Rochette, this is the best probability bound achievable with $n$-qubit ciphertexts (up to the constant hidden in the $O(\cdot)$).   The main conceptual idea is to leverage, in a precise spectral sense, the balanced commutation-anticommutation structure of the Pauli group. The proof is intricate but completely elementary and makes use of standard spectral bound techniques. The main technical workhorse is a standalone linear-algebraic lemma that informally relates the positivity of two different operators, each capturing the intuition that if the two receivers can individually decrypt unusually often then they must also disagree often.   GPT-5.6 Sol Ultra found this proof in an extended conversation with the author and drafted a preliminary version of this paper. The author is fully accountable for the correctness of this paper.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how well Self-Interaction Correction methods describe strongly correlated electron systems by modeling two electrons in a harmonic trap (harmonium). The authors prove that the symmetry-broken SIC approach yields exact results in the classical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$). This demonstrates SIC's utility for bridging the gap between standard quantum DFT and purely classical descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electronic structure theory, the concept of approaching a classical limit from quantum mechanics using specialized corrections (SIC) is relevant for understanding semiclassical approximations in many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses strong electron correlation, a major challenge for Density Functional Theory (DFT), by examining the exactness of symmetry-broken Self-Interaction Correction (SIC) in the semiclassical or classical limit.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that for the harmonium model, symmetry-broken SIC yields results exact in the $\hbar 	o 0$ limit and highly accurate across all intermediate values of Planck's constant ($\hbar$).

**Method.** The research employs a variational approach using localized Gaussian orbitals within the framework of PZ-SIC to calculate ground-state energies for the two-electron harmonium system.

**Model / system.** The model is the harmonium: two Coulomb-interacting electrons confined by a spherically-symmetric harmonic-oscillator potential. The Hamiltonian includes tunable Planck's constant ($\hbar$) and confinement strength ($\omega$).

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy ($E$), ground-state density ($ho(r)$), optimized localization distance ($R_{opt}$), and Gaussian exponent ($\alpha_{opt}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Planck's constant ($\hbar$), harmonic confinement strength ($\omega$), and the electron-electron interaction strength (Coulomb repulsion).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that symmetry-broken SIC correctly captures the physics in the strongly correlated limit, although limitations exist due to the restricted variational ansatz.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the total energy vs. $\hbar$ for various DFAs (showing DFA failure near $\hbar 	o 0$), and show how optimized parameters ($R_{opt}, \alpha_{opt}$) converge towards classical values as $\hbar$ decreases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical problem in the harmonium model, comparing standard DFT failures in the semiclassical limit, introducing and applying symmetry-broken SIC via a variational ansatz, and finally demonstrating convergence to the known exact classical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong electron correlation is an important challenge to both wavefunction and density functional theory. It has been argued that the Perdew-Zunger 1981 self-interaction correction to any density functional approximation, after symmetry breaking, can correctly describe the ground-state energy in the strongly-correlated limit in which each electron is described by a highly-localized and non-overlapped one-electron spin orbital. It has also been argued that the classical limit, in which Planck's constant tends to zero, is the strongly-correlated limit of quantum mechanics, where standard density functionals fail badly, as demonstrated by the exactly-solvable problem of harmonium (two Coulomb-interacting electrons bound by a spherically-symmetric harmonic-oscillator external potential). Here we combine these two ideas and demonstrate that, for harmonium, symmetry-broken self-interaction correction is exact in the limit where Planck's constant tends to zero, and usefully accurate for all values between 0 and the physical value (1 in atomic units). We also show that the Planck-constant-dependent symmetric ground-state density can be restored by spherical averaging of the broken-symmetry density.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the first observation of intrinsic nonlinear thermoelectric effects—including NLS, NLN, and NLMT—at room temperature in low-symmetry Weyl semimetals. By employing harmonic detection techniques, the authors successfully isolated these higher-order responses, demonstrating that they arise fundamentally from reduced crystal symmetry rather than external magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state transport, the ability to engineer higher-order energy conversion mechanisms via symmetry control is relevant for understanding non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to demonstrate and characterize intrinsic nonlinear thermoelectric effects at room temperature in low-dimensional semimetals, moving beyond reliance on external symmetry breaking.

**Main result.** All three symmetry-allowed components of the nonlinear thermoelectric tensor (NLS, NLN, NLMT) were observed intrinsically up to room temperature in $	ext{WTe}_2$ and $	ext{TaIrTe}_4$.

**Method.** Measurements utilized harmonic detection ($4\omega$) techniques on fabricated devices with specific geometries to isolate intrinsic nonlinear contributions from linear response terms.

**Model / system.** The physical systems are low-symmetry type-II Weyl semimetals ($	ext{WTe}_2$ and $	ext{TaIrTe}_4}$). The analysis uses a third-rank tensor formalism ($\mathbf{S}^{(2)}$) to describe the nonlinear thermoelectric response.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear Seebeck (NLS), Nonlinear Nernst (NLN), and Nonlinear Mixed-directional thermoelectric (NLMT) coefficients, measured as fourth-harmonic voltage responses ($E_{i, 4\omega}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature ($\sim 290 	ext{K}$); zero magnetic field; low-dimensional semimetals.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The intrinsic nonlinear contribution is isolated by exploiting the directional nature of linear effects through symmetrization and ruling out temperature dependence of heater resistance.

**Figures summary.** Figures show device schematics, measured fourth-harmonic responses for NLS/NLMT/NLN in $	ext{TaIrTe}_4$, and the temperature dependence of these nonlinear coefficients in both materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using second-order tensors for linear response and third-rank tensors for nonlinear effects. It details experimental measurements using harmonic detection to isolate intrinsic contributions, followed by analysis showing the origin (Berry curvature vs. scattering) and temperature dependence of these novel thermoelectric responses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonreciprocal control of thermoelectric responses offers a promising strategy for next-generation thermal-management and energy-conversion. While nonlinear electrical transport has recently emerged as an intrinsic property of low-symmetry quantum materials, their thermoelectric counterparts have not been demonstrated. Here, exploiting harmonic detection with gradient-reversal techniques, we report intrinsic nonlinear thermoelectric responses up to room temperature in the low-symmetry type-II Weyl semimetals $T_\mathrm{d}$-WTe$_2$ and TaIrTe$_4$ in the absence of magnetic fields or magnetic materials. We resolve all symmetry-allowed components of the second-order thermoelectric tensor, including the nonlinear Seebeck, nonlinear Nernst, and nonlinear mixed-directional thermoelectric effects and demonstrate that both Berry-curvature-related and scattering-induced contributions govern the different nonlinear thermoelectric responses. Our results show that nonlinear thermoelectricity arises intrinsically from reduced crystal symmetry and that engineering effects related to scattering in these materials provides a versatile platform for exploring higher-order heat-to-charge current conversion beyond the linear response.

</details>

<a id="paper-2607.22460"></a>
### [Algebraic structure of Tiger codes](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22460v1)

**Authors:** Clément Poirson, Anthony Leverrier, Christophe Vuillot  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22460v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.22460_figures/2607.22460_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 2: Graphical representation of the partial order in Definition 10: Let b =  3 −2⊤∈Z2. The blue shaded area represents all the vectors of Z2 less than or equal to b.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously develops the theory of Tiger codes, which are advanced multimode bosonic quantum error-correcting codes. It uses sophisticated algebraic tools to prove the code structure is mathematically sound, deriving an explicit basis and characterizing its logical degrees of freedom via homology. Crucially, it provides methods to implement non-Clifford gates necessary for universal quantum computation within this bosonic encoding framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical underpinning for bosonic quantum error correction, moving beyond simple stabilizer models by incorporating complex algebraic structures and characterizing universal gate sets necessary for fault-tolerant computation in superconducting circuits or cavity QED systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a rigorous algebraic treatment of Tiger codes, which are multimode bosonic quantum error-correcting codes unifying several known constructions.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that the code structure is governed by the homology of an underlying chain complex and develops criteria for implementing non-Clifford logical gates using polynomial phase rotations. This confirms Tiger codes as a robust framework for bosonic encoding.

**Method.** The authors employ algebraic techniques, including kernel definitions, Fourier transforms over the codespace, and analysis via homological algebra to characterize the code space and its logical operations.

**Model / system.** The system involves multimode bosonic quantum states (Fock states) protected by constraints derived from both annihilation-type ($\hat{a}$-type) and number-type ($\hat{n}$-type) operators. These codes generalize concepts from stabilizer codes to bosonic systems.

**Key observables.** Logical Pauli operators, the structure of the homology group ($Z^{d_1} 	imes \cdots$), and the required polynomial form $P(\hat{\mathbf{n}})$ for gate implementation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The constraints are defined by integer matrices ($G$ and $H$) and associated parameters ($\alpha, \Delta$). The complexity is related to the degree of polynomials used in gates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending definitions from linear constraints to non-linear number constraints. A key algebraic result involves decomposing preserving polynomials into univariate components under certain assumptions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference structural relationships derived from matrix decompositions (e.g., $L_f = H$) and specific code examples like the cat or paircat codes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the rigorous algebraic structure of Tiger codes using kernel definitions; it then develops tools like Fourier transforms to find dual bases; subsequently, it extends the framework to non-linear constraints; finally, it characterizes the implementation of logical operations, especially non-Clifford gates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tiger codes form a family of multimode bosonic quantum codes that unify several previously known constructions, including cat, paircat, and the two-mode binomial code. In this work, we give a rigorous algebraic treatment of these codes. Starting from a kernel definition of the codespace, we prove that the annihilation-type constraints admit a finite generating set, construct an explicit orthonormal basis, and show that the logical structure of the code is governed by the homology of an underlying chain complex, as expected in the original work on Tiger codes of Xu et al. We then develop a Fourier transform over the codespace to prove that the span of phase-rotated projected coherent states is dense therein, and to yield dual $X$- and $Z$-type descriptions of the code. We further extend the framework to non-linear number constraints, encompassing codes such as the four-legged cat or the repetition cat code. Finally, we investigate the implementation of logical operations. We first generalise the construction of logical Pauli operators proposed by Xu et al. to arbitrary logical spaces, and then construct non-Clifford gates using physical polynomial phase rotations of the form $e^{iP(\hat{\boldsymbol{n}})}$. We derive criteria on the real polynomial $P$ which, for positive single-logical-qubit Tiger codes satisfying an additional sign assumption, such as the paircat code, characterise the polynomials $P$ that preserve the codespace by decomposing them into a family of univariate polynomials. Through this decomposition, we relate the degrees of the resulting components to the induced logical action in the Clifford hierarchy. These results establish Tiger codes as a mathematically robust framework for describing a broad class of bosonic encodings.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the complex problem of calculating the Wigner angle, defined as the rotation angle resulting from combining two Lorentz boosts in 2+1 dimensions. It achieves this by reviewing and demonstrating the mathematical equivalence of formulas derived using vector, matrix, and spinor formalisms. The work confirms that such a composition is not simply another boost but rather a combination of a boost followed by a rotation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum information or AMO systems, the rigorous treatment of Lorentz transformations and their composition rules provides a deep theoretical understanding of how sequential physical boosts combine in spacetime geometry, which has analogues in relativistic quantum field theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is calculating the Wigner angle, which represents the rotation angle resulting from the composition of two nonparallel Lorentz boosts in 2+1 dimensions.

**Main result.** The paper derives equivalent formulas for the Wigner angle using vector, matrix, and spinor formalisms, confirming that the product of two boosts results in a boost followed by a rotation.

**Method.** It reviews and compares multiple mathematical derivations—including vector algebra, Lorentz matrix decomposition ($SO(1,2)$), and indefinite unitary group analysis ($	ext{SU}(1,1)$)—to determine the angle.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is special relativity in 2+1 dimensions, focusing on Lorentz transformations involving combinations of boosts and rotations. The mathematical tools include boost operators $B(\vec{u})$ and associated transformation matrices.

**Key observables.** Wigner angle ($	heta_{21}$ or $	heta_w$), composite energy ($\gamma_{21}$), and the rotation dyadic ($\leftrightarrow\sigma_{21}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Boost parameters (velocity $\vec{u}$, energy $\gamma$) and transformation angles ($	heta_1, 	heta_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The composition of two boosts is assumed to decompose into a boost followed by a rotation; equivalence between different mathematical formalisms (vector vs. spinor) is asserted.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but derivations involve equations for product operators, trigonometric relations for $\sin(	heta_w)$ and $\cos(	heta_w)$, and matrix decompositions.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by reviewing vector, matrix, and spinor derivations sequentially. It establishes the general structure of the combined transformation ($B_2 B_1 	o 	ext{Boost} 	imes 	ext{Rotation}$) before deriving explicit formulas for the Wigner angle using various mathematical frameworks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lorentz transformations in time and two space dimensions consist of boosts and rotations, and combinations thereof. In general, the combination of two boosts is not another boost: It is a boost followed by a rotation. The rotation angle is called the Wigner angle. Although it is straightforward to determine the energy and direction of the combined boost, it is difficult to determine the Wigner angle. In this article, the vector, matrix and spinor derivations of formulas for the Wigner angle are reviewed, and the underlying mathematics and physics are discussed briefly. Although the derivations are different, the results they produce are equivalent, as they should be. Like many physics problems, if one looks at the problem in the right way, it is not difficult to solve.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces ADAPT-GQE, an AI framework that uses Transformer models to automatically synthesize efficient quantum circuits required for calculating molecular ground states. By training on existing high-quality data and using reinforcement learning, it drastically reduces the time needed compared to state-of-the-art iterative methods like ADAPT-VQE. This establishes a pathway toward automated, scalable quantum chemistry simulations on modern hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the resource bottleneck in applying quantum computation to chemistry by replacing iterative optimization with a learned generative model, which is highly relevant for scaling quantum algorithms beyond small test cases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing scalable methods for quantum state preparation, specifically calculating molecular ground states, because existing iterative algorithms are computationally prohibitive for large molecules.

**Main result.** The proposed ADAPT-GQE framework achieves order-of-magnitude reductions in circuit generation time compared to ADAPT-VQE while maintaining or improving state-preparation accuracy on challenging molecules like imipramine.

**Method.** The authors use a generative AI approach, training Transformer models (like Gemma) on reference circuits generated by ADAPT-VQE. Reinforcement Learning is then employed to refine circuit generation beyond the initial training data's scope.

**Model / system.** The physical system is molecular quantum chemistry, using Hamiltonians derived from molecules like imipramine. The methodology involves mapping these Hamiltonians into a format suitable for large language models (LLMs) to predict optimal quantum circuits.

**Key observables.** Energy accuracy ($	ext{E}_{	ext{pred}}$ vs $	ext{E}_{	ext{ref}}$ in mHa), circuit generation time, and the number of required quantum gates/operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count (up to 16 qubits), target energy tolerance ($\epsilon$), and hardware platform (Quantinuum Helios-1).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary reward function relies on computationally expensive reference energies (CASCI or CCSD) for training, though an alternative reward function was shown to be effective.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the conformational sampling of imipramine using MD/NEB; tables compare computational costs and energy accuracies between ADAPT-VQE and the Transformer models across various qubit counts.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the limitations of VQE/ADAPT-VQE, details the data generation pipeline (MD/NEB for diverse conformers), presents the LLM architecture for Hamiltonian encoding, and finally demonstrates performance gains via training and RL fine-tuning on hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum state preparation is a key component of many quantum algorithms. Performing this step efficiently is essential for realizing practical quantum advantage in quantum chemistry applications. Iterative algorithms like ADAPT-VQE can produce shallow ground-state preparation circuits, but become computationally prohibitive for the larger molecules relevant to materials science and pharmaceutical development. Here, we introduce ADAPT-GQE, a generative AI framework that learns to synthesize ground-state preparation circuits for electronic structure calculations. We first use ADAPT-VQE to generate high-quality reference circuits, which are then used as targets for training models for circuit generation. Once trained, the model can efficiently propose and score circuits, enabling reinforcement learning (RL) to drive circuit generation accuracy beyond the accuracy of the ADAPT-VQE training data. This pipeline achieves order-of-magnitude reductions in circuit generation time relative to ADAPT-VQE while maintaining comparable or improved state-preparation accuracy. We demonstrate ADAPT-GQE on imipramine, a well-established tricyclic antidepressant that serves as a representative, challenging target for computational modelling in drug stability protocols. We execute generated circuits on Quantinuum Helios-1, representing a milestone for AI-generated quantum chemistry circuits on state-of-the-art quantum hardware. These results establish a pathway toward automated quantum circuit synthesis for utility-scale quantum computational chemistry.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21919v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QML-kNN, a quantum algorithm designed to overcome the computational bottleneck of traditional ML-kNN for multi-label classification. By utilizing quantum circuits for probability estimation and neighbor searching, it achieves significant theoretical speedups and superior performance compared to its classical counterpart.

**Why it may be interesting.** It demonstrates a direct application of advanced quantum algorithms (QAE, Quantum Counting) to solve a complex machine learning problem, providing concrete theoretical speedups that could motivate hardware development in the NISQ era.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The classical Multi-label k-Nearest Neighbor (ML-kNN) algorithm suffers from unacceptable time complexity when applied to large-scale, high-dimensional datasets.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum ML-kNN (QML-kNN) significantly reduces the time complexity and outperforms classical methods across all tested evaluation metrics.

**Method.** It integrates quantum techniques—including quantum counting circuits, c-SWAP tests, and a specialized Quantum Parallel Counting Circuit (QPCC)—to accelerate probability estimation and neighbor searching.

**Model / system.** The model is an algorithmic framework designed to replace classical ML-kNN steps with quantum subroutines. These include using Quantum Phase Estimation for prior probabilities and amplitude encoding/c-SWAP tests for similarity calculation.

**Key observables.** Hamming Loss, Coverage, Ranking Loss, One-error, Macro-AUC, and computational time complexity (Big O notation).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Feature dimension ($U$), training instances ($m$), number of neighbors ($k$), and number of labels ($Q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on theoretical models for quantum speedups; practical implementation is currently limited by existing quantum hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall QML-kNN workflow across three modules, show performance comparisons between classical and quantum approaches (e.g., Fig. 8), and plot the leading-order complexity reduction achieved by the quantum algorithm (e.g., Fig. 9).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the computational bottleneck of ML-kNN. It then details the QML-kNN framework across three modules: Module A for prior probabilities using QPE/Grover's, Module B for neighbor search via c-SWAP and quantum k-maximal search, and Module C for posterior probability calculation using a QPCC.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Although multi-label k-nearest neighbor (ML-kNN) is able to effectively solve multi-label learning (MLL) problem with local neighborhood similarity, its time complexity is nearly unacceptable with large-scale datasets. To solve this issue, we propose a novel ML-kNN algorithm with quantum computing techniques, which called quantum multi-label k-nearest neighbor (QML-kNN). In particular, we first accelerate the calculation of the prior probability by taking advantage of quantum phase estimation and Grover's amplitude amplification. Then, a controlled-SWAP test and a quantum k-maximal similarity search are used for efficiently identifying the neighbors. Subsequently, a quantum parallel counting circuit (QPCC) is designed to rapidly calculate the posterior probabilities. Experimental results demonstrate that QML-kNN is able to significantly reduce the time complexity of solving multi-label problems with performance improvement, achieving a substantial speedup over the classical MLL algorithm.

</details>

The Γ states (bottom right) are forced- dark at the AA-stacked regions, where the K states (bottom left) are bright. The Hartree potential is strongest at the AA regions, strongly increasing the energy at K, but not at Γ where the wavefunction avoids the Hartree potential.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel technique to determine band topology by analyzing where the wavefunction must vanish in real space, using symmetry constraints. By mapping these 'dark spots' at high-symmetry points, researchers can infer fundamental topological invariants like the Chern number or $\mathbb{Z}_2$ index. This connects advanced group theory directly to experimental techniques like STM imaging.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical link between abstract group representation theory (mathematics) and measurable real-space electronic structure (condensed matter), offering an alternative diagnostic tool to traditional momentum-space topological measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the topological invariants of electronic bands by probing real-space properties, specifically the symmetry-enforced zeros in the wavefunction's local density of states (LDOS).

**Main result.** The pattern and location of these 'dark spots' at high-symmetry momenta are sufficient to uniquely determine the irreducible representation ($ho$) of the band structure group, thereby diagnosing topological invariants like the Chern number or $\mathbb{Z}_2$ index.

**Method.** The method uses group theory to derive symmetry selection rules that force wavefunctions to vanish (create zeros) at specific real-space positions ($r^*$) when evaluated at high-symmetry momenta ($k^*$).

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to crystalline solids, including 2D materials like TMDs, and theoretical models such as the Haldane model, BHZ model, and Kagome lattice.

**Key observables.** Symmetry-enforced zeros ('dark spots') in the Local Density of States (LDOS) measured by STM; local indices ($m_{k^*, r^*}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High-symmetry momenta ($k^*$), symmetry group representations ($ho$, $	ext{irrep}$), and topological invariants (Chern number, $\mathbb{Z}_2$ index).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on probing high-symmetry momenta and assumes the bias energy intersects a single, isolated irreducible representation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the detection of band topology in TMDs by comparing LDOS changes between $\Gamma$ and $K$, showing zero patterns that distinguish topological phases (e.g., Chern number mod 3 or $\mathbb{Z}_2$ index).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a general theorem linking symmetry-enforced zeros to group representation theory, then applies this framework sequentially to specific physical models: TMDs, Haldane model, BHZ model, Kagome lattice, and Twisted Bilayer Graphene.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that the wavefunction of a crystal at a high-symmetry momentum, $Ψ_{\boldsymbol{k}_*}(\boldsymbol{r})$, has symmetry-enforced zeros at certain positions in the unit cell, using a new invariant fixed uniquely by the symmorphic symmetry representation of the wavefunction. This allows one to infer the topology of an electronic band by probing zeros of the charge density, and in turn to connect scanning tunnelling microscopy to the group representation theory of bandstructure. We apply the theorem to 1H transition metal dichalcogenides, where it detects the obstructed atomic limit of WSe$_2$, the Haldane model, where it detects the Chern number modulo three, and the Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang model, where it detects the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ index. In addition, the zeros have important consequences for interaction effects: in kagome metals, they fix the sublattice structure of Van Hove wavefunctions, and in twisted bilayer graphene, they explain the qualitative interaction-induced reshaping of the flat bands.

</details>

### [The Threshold Theorem in Watts: Fault Tolerance as a Question About Objective Probability](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22276v1)

**Authors:** Amit Hagar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22276v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper reframes the Threshold Theorem for fault-tolerant quantum computing by applying an objective probability measure based on resource costs (like energy). The authors argue that standard analyses neglect crucial overheads such as calibration and decoding power. By quantifying these missing resources in watts, they transform a theoretical debate into a concrete, measurable engineering challenge.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful physical lens—resource accounting—to re-evaluate fundamental computational complexity results in quantum information theory, connecting abstract theorems to measurable engineering constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reinterprets the Threshold Theorem for Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computing (FTQC) by framing it as a question of objective resource probability rather than just circuit combinatorics.

**Main result.** By incorporating previously unpriced overheads like calibration, decoding latency, and entropy flushing, the required resources to maintain logical coherence are quantified in watts per decade of error suppression.

**Method.** The authors translate FTQC feasibility into a measurable resource cost (watts/decade) using a novel objective probability measure derived from Hagar & Sergioli's work.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computation, specifically concerning the scaling of error correction overheads for logical qubits.

**Key observables.** Watts per decade of suppressed logical error; resource cost curves comparing quantum vs. classical computation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Logical error rate ($\epsilon$); machine size (number of physical qubits/distance); power budget (watts).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation relies on deterministic dynamics and the assumption that all necessary resources (calibration, decoding, etc.) must be accounted for in the objective probability measure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the resource-bounded objective probability framework. It then applies this to FTQC, identifying four previously unpriced overheads. Finally, it translates the theoretical threshold into a concrete measurement: watts per decade of error suppression.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In 2011 Hagar & Sergioli proposed a new interpretation of objective probability in deterministic physics. On it, the probability of a physical state supervenes on the resources, energy over time, required to realize it from a given state, relative to the resources available (arXiv:1101.3521). The motivation for that paper was an objective alternative to QBism, the view that sees quantum probabilities as subjective degrees of belief. Here I apply this interpretation to a more practical subject matter: fault-tolerant quantum computing (FTQC). Under the resource-bounded measure Hagar & Sergioli proposed, the threshold theorem becomes a claim about classification: it asserts that error-corrected logical states belong to the class of relatively cheaply realizable states, whose probability remains near 1 as the machine grows. The theorem originally derived this claim from a resource inventory that was partial, and left out four resources consumed by error correction: calibration of a drifting device, decoding within the correction cycle, coherence as a finite time budget, and entropy flush through fresh ancillas. These entered the original derivation at zero price. Here I translate the feasibility of FTQC into a measurable quantity, watts per decade of suppressed logical error (a decade, in the engineer's usage, being one factor of ten in the error rate). I then show that the published record already contains its first data points for this translation, and I state the two measurements that would settle the question empirically. The three-decade debate on FTQC, conducted so far as an exchange about noise-model assumptions, turns out to be, under this interpretation, a quantitative dispute about a single object: the resource-bounded objective probability of the target logical states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the classical hardness of boson sampling by deriving a weak anti-concentration bound for the permanent of random Gaussian matrices. Using advanced probabilistic methods, the authors show that while they cannot prove the full conjecture, their result implies that simulating boson sampling to high precision would have profound consequences for computational complexity theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the theoretical foundations of quantum computational advantage in photonic systems (boson sampling) by providing rigorous mathematical tools to bound key physical observables (the permanent).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a weak anti-concentration bound for the permanent of random Gaussian matrices, which is crucial for understanding the classical hardness of boson sampling.

**Main result.** The authors established a weak anti-concentration bound showing that the probability of the Gaussian permanent being superexponentially small is bounded by $O(n^{-c})$, implying potential consequences for collapsing the polynomial hierarchy if true.

**Method.** The proof employs an extension of the row-exposure strategy, utilizing advanced concentration inequalities like McDiarmid's inequality and analyzing recursive structures involving $\lambda$-heavy minors.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Boson Sampling, where the probability distribution is governed by the permanent of a submatrix approximated by entries i.i.d. from the standard complex Gaussian distribution ($G_{n 	imes n}$).

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the permanent, $	ext{Per}(M)$, and its concentration properties relative to its standard deviation ($\sqrt{n!}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Matrix size $n$, the exponent $c$ in the bound $O(n^{-c})$, and the relationship between classical simulation error (total variation distance) and computational complexity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result is a *weak* version of the Permanent Anti-Concentration Conjecture (PACC), and the final hardness implication relies on assuming other unproven conjectures hold.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon prior work by adapting techniques from Bernoulli matrices to Gaussian random matrices, proceeding through a structured proof involving warm-up, growth, and endgame phases using concentration bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent demonstrations of quantum computational advantage have been driven largely by sampling problems. A prominent model, boson sampling, involves sampling from the output distribution of a linear optical network. However, its classical hardness hinges on two plausible yet less-studied conjectures: the average-case hardness of approximating Gaussian permanents, and the permanent anti-concentration conjecture (PACC). The PACC is a purely mathematical assertion regarding the distributional properties of random Gaussian matrices. While the typical magnitude of the permanent has been established for discrete random matrices, the complex Gaussian case, which governs transition amplitudes in linear optical networks, has remained open. Here, we establish a weak anti-concentration bound by upper-bounding the probability that a random Gaussian permanent is superexponentially smaller than its standard deviation. Tightening this bound to an inverse-polynomial fraction would prove the original PACC. As a corollary, we establish the typical magnitude of Gaussian permanents, on par with Tao and Vu's seminal result for Bernoulli matrices. Combined with the Aaronson-Arkhipov framework, our result implies that classically simulating boson sampling to within a superexponentially small total variation distance would collapse the polynomial hierarchy, assuming the remaining conjectures hold.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work used neutron diffraction on EuSnP to map its zero-field magnetic structure. The findings confirm an A-type antiferromagnetism, where the local Eu2+ moments are ferromagnetically coupled within layers but antiferromagnetically stacked along the c-axis. This structural determination helps explain the material's complex magnetic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a concrete example of localized magnetism (Eu2+ moments) interacting with itinerant electrons, which is central to understanding strongly correlated electron systems and complex magnetic phase transitions in solids.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the zero-field magnetic structure of the antiferromagnetic metal EuSnP and understand its microscopic origin.

**Main result.** The material exhibits a collinear A-type antiferromagnetic structure, characterized by ferromagnetic alignment within the Eu–P layers and antiferromagnetic coupling along the stacking direction.

**Method.** Single-crystal neutron diffraction experiments were used to search for and analyze magnetic Bragg reflections as a function of temperature and scattering vector.

**Model / system.** The system is the antiferromagnetic metal EuSnP, containing localized divalent Eu2+ moments interacting with conduction electrons in a tetragonal lattice structure. The analysis compared observed magnetic structure factors against theoretical models (A-type vs G-type).

**Key observables.** Magnetic propagation vector q = (0, 0, 1/2); Magnetic Bragg peak intensities; Magnetic susceptibility and resistivity anomalies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Néel temperature TN ≈ 21 K; Estimated magnetic moment μ ≈ 6.5 to 7.1 $\mu_B$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting observed structure factors using theoretical models derived from the crystal structure and assuming specific sample dimensions for absorption corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature dependence of resistivity, susceptibility, and magnetic Bragg peak intensities; subsequent figures compare observed vs. calculated squared magnetic structure factors for candidate A-type and G-type models.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the bulk magnetic properties (resistivity/susceptibility) near TN, followed by detailed single-crystal neutron diffraction measurements to determine the propagation vector q. The core of the analysis involves fitting observed magnetic reflections against theoretical structure factor calculations for competing magnetic models to confirm the A-type ordering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigated the zero-field magnetic structure of the antiferromagnetic metal EuSnP through single-crystal neutron diffraction experiments. The magnetic propagation vector in the magnetically ordered phase was determined to be $(0,0,1/2)$, indicating that commensurate antiferromagnetic ordering is realized in this material. The detailed magnetic structure analysis revealed a collinear A-type antiferromagnetic structure with ferromagnetic alignment of Eu$^{2+}$ moments within the Eu--P layer. The present findings provide a microscopic basis for understanding the intriguing magnetic behaviors of EuSnP, as exemplified by the multiple metamagnetic transitions in the antiferromagnetic state and the strong pressure dependence of the magnetic transition temperature.

</details>
